    notes: {
      en: "⚠ **This node still has status: skeleton**, but the gap has narrowed to **one external theorem** (Radó's triangulation existence theorem, whose node `thm.rado` is itself still a skeleton). The other previous gap, orientability for topological surfaces, now has its own node (`def.topological-orientability`). **This proposition no longer uses the classification theorem**. The elementary argument below replaces both routes in the previous notes, namely (a) using `thm.classification-surfaces` and (b) using the fundamental class or orientation duality. With these two inputs, the proof is complete.\n\n**0. Conventions and notation.** $S$ is a closed surface (`def.closed-surface`), and $g=\\tfrac12\\operatorname{rank}H_{1}(S;\\mathbb{Z})$ (`def.genus`, used directly as the definition). Compute $\\chi(S)$ from any triangulation: `thm.euler-characteristic` gives $\\chi(K)=\\sum_{p}(-1)^{p}\\alpha_{p}=\\sum_{p}(-1)^{p}\\beta_{p}(K)$, and `thm.simplicial-approximation` gives $\\beta_{p}(K)=\\beta_{p}(S)$. Hence $\\sum_{p}(-1)^{p}\\beta_{p}(S)$ is independent of the triangulation, and we denote it by $\\chi(S)$. Here, orientability of $S$ is understood in the topological sense. There is an **oriented atlas** whose charts are homeomorphisms and whose transition maps preserve orientation on overlaps (this is `def.orientable-manifold` with the smoothness requirement removed, namely `def.topological-orientability`; see Section 7). Fix an orientation.\n\n**1. Triangulation (input: Radó).** `thm.rado` (skeleton) states that a compact surface is triangulable, so there exist a finite simplicial complex $K$ and a homeomorphism $h:|K|\\to S$. This is the only external theorem used here.\n\n**2. Subdividing until each simplex lies in a chart (complete).** Let $\\{U_{i}\\}$ be an oriented atlas. Then $\\{h^{-1}(U_{i})\\}$ is an open cover of the compact metric space $|K|$ and has a Lebesgue number $\\lambda>0$ (`def.lebesgue-number`, whose notes give a complete proof). By iterating barycentric subdivision (`def.barycentric-subdivision`, with the mesh shrinking by $\\tfrac{n}{n+1}$), choose $m$ so that $\\operatorname{mesh}(K^{(m)})<\\lambda$. Then each simplex of $K^{(m)}$ lies in some $h^{-1}(U_{i})$. Write $K'=K^{(m)}$. Barycentric subdivision leaves the geometric realization unchanged, so $|K'|=|K|\\cong S$, and $K'$ is still a triangulation of $S$.\n\n**3. Giving $K'$ a coherent orientation (complete).** For each $2$-simplex $\\sigma$, choose a chart $(U_{i},\\varphi_{i})$ containing it and use $\\varphi_{i}\\circ h$ to pull back the standard orientation of $\\mathbb{R}^{2}$ to $\\sigma$. If $\\sigma$ lies in two charts, the transition map preserves orientation, so the orientations agree. The orientation of $\\sigma$ is therefore independent of the choice of chart. Now consider two adjacent $2$-simplices $\\sigma,\\tau$ sharing an interior edge $e$. Near an interior point of $e$, choose a small disk $W\\subseteq U_{i}\\cap U_{j}$ ($\\sigma$ lies in $U_{i}$, $\\tau$ lies in $U_{j}$, and $W$ lies in both charts). In the common chart $\\varphi_{j}$, the triangles lie on opposite sides of $\\varphi_{j}(e)$, and the orientation of $\\sigma$ is transported by the orientation-preserving map $\\varphi_{j}\\circ\\varphi_{i}^{-1}$. In $\\mathbb{R}^{2}$, two consistently oriented triangles on opposite sides of a shared edge induce opposite orientations on that edge. Thus the simplices of $K'$ of dimension $2$ are coherently oriented: the two sides of each interior edge induce opposite orientations on it.\n\n**4. Lemma D (complete): $H_{2}(K';\\mathbb{Z})\\cong\\mathbb{Z}$.** Let $K'$ be a finite triangulation of a closed connected surface with coherently oriented $2$-simplices. Set $z=\\sum_{\\sigma}\\sigma$, with each $\\sigma$ oriented as above. Then $\\partial z=0$: each interior edge belongs to exactly two triangles, which induce opposite orientations on the edge, so the coefficients $\\pm1$ cancel. Since $S$ is closed, there are no boundary edges and hence no other terms. Thus $z$ is a $2$-cycle. Conversely, let $w=\\sum n_{\\sigma}\\sigma$ be any $2$-cycle. For each interior edge $e$ with adjacent triangles $\\sigma,\\tau$, the coefficient in $\\partial w$ of $e$ is $\\pm n_{\\sigma}\\pm n_{\\tau}$. Coherent orientation gives opposite signs, so $n_{\\sigma}=n_{\\tau}$. The dual graph is connected: $S$ is connected $\\implies$ the graph joining triangles that share a vertex is connected. The link of each vertex of the surface is a circle $\\implies$ the triangles around a vertex form a cycle of edge adjacencies. Together these facts give $\\implies$ the dual graph joining triangles that share an edge is connected. Thus all $n_{\\sigma}$ are equal, and $w\\in\\mathbb{Z}\\cdot z$. Also, $K'$ is a complex of dimension $2$, so $C_{3}=0$, and consequently $H_{2}(K')=\\ker\\partial_{2}=\\mathbb{Z}\\cdot z\\cong\\mathbb{Z}$. ∎\n\n**5. $\\beta_{2},\\beta_{0},\\beta_{1}$ (complete).** By `thm.simplicial-approximation`, $H_{2}(S;\\mathbb{Z})\\cong H_{2}(K';\\mathbb{Z})\\cong\\mathbb{Z}$, so $\\beta_{2}(S)=1$. Since $S$ is connected and is a manifold (hence locally path-connected), it is path-connected. By `prop.h0-components`, $H_{0}(S)\\cong\\mathbb{Z}$, $\\beta_{0}=1$. By the definition in `def.genus`, $\\beta_{1}=\\operatorname{rank}H_{1}=2g$.\n\n**6. Conclusion.** $\\chi(S)=\\beta_{0}-\\beta_{1}+\\beta_{2}=1-2g+1=2-2g$. ∎ (Using Radó's theorem from Section 1 and the definition of orientability above.)\n\n**7. Status and remaining gap.** (1) The only external theorem unproved here is Radó's theorem (`thm.rado`, skeleton). (2) Orientability now has its own node, `def.topological-orientability` (oriented atlases and the orientation double cover). The graph also has a smooth version (`def.orientable-manifold`, `prop.volume-form-orientation`) and an extrinsic version in $\\mathbb{R}^{3}$ (`def.orientable-surface`). Applying the smooth definition to a topological surface first requires a smoothing theorem, which in turn requires Radó or Whitehead. The moves are (i) cutting along edges and regluing (cut-and-paste), (ii) $2$–$2$ flips, (iii) removing a $2$-simplex attached along just one edge, and (iv) vertex reduction (merging several vertices into one). **This lemma is the core of the classification. This works only when the triangles form a quadrilateral along the shared edge and all four vertices are distinct. If the triangles share another vertex (so the quadrilateral degenerates), or the fourth vertex coincides with the first (giving two triangles instead of a quadrilateral), the case needs separate treatment. The graph gives neither these hypotheses nor the degenerate cases. - **Cut-and-paste**: The graph only says to cut along a closed curve and reglue. To do this within a triangulation, first make the curve a simple closed curve (subdivision $+$ general position, normal isotopy). One must then show that cutting gives a new triangulation and that regluing preserves the homeomorphism type. Neither step is given here. - **Termination**: The original claim that the number of paired edges strictly decreases **is false**. A $2$–$2$ flip leaves the edge count unchanged, and cut-and-paste can increase it. What is needed is a well-ordered measure (for instance, induction on the number of sides of the polygon, with finitely many cases as in Munkres's proof, or a lexicographic order on the number of edges $+$ the number of vertices) and a check that each move decreases it. The graph gives neither the measure nor the verification. - **Missing construction for vertex reduction**: Before reducing the triangulation to a single polygon, all vertices must be merged into one (move (iv)).       en: "Every **compact surface** (a compact, connected, $2$-dimensional topological manifold, possibly with boundary) is triangulable (`def.triangulation`).\n\nA common equivalent formulation is that every compact connected surface is homeomorphic to the geometric realization of a **finite** simplicial complex.",

    notes: {
      en: "⚠ This node retains status: skeleton. There are two common approaches to Radó's theorem, and neither is completed here. The outline below identifies the specific lemmas still needed and corrects two inaccuracies in the previous notes.\n\n**Theorem.** Every compact surface is triangulable (equivalently, a compact topological $2$-manifold admits a finite simplicial triangulation). An orientable compact surface also admits a compatible complex structure. See the comparison by dimension below for the precise scope of the equivalent formulation.\n\n**Surfaces with boundary.** The statement allows boundary, but both approaches below address only **closed** surfaces. The extension requires an argument. The standard method is **doubling along the boundary**. Take two copies of $S$ and glue them along $\\partial S$ by the identity to obtain a closed surface $D(S)$. Triangulate $D(S)$ by either approach below, then restrict to $S$, provided the triangulation places $\\partial S$ in the $1$-skeleton (see gap (vi)). One can also collapse each boundary circle to a point to obtain a closed surface.\n\n**Approach A (complex analysis).** An orientable compact surface $\\rightarrow$ a complex structure (a Riemann surface) $\\rightarrow$ existence of a nonconstant meromorphic function $\\rightarrow$ the meromorphic function is a branched covering of $\\mathbb{CP}^{1}$ $\\rightarrow$ pull back and subdivide a triangulation of $\\mathbb{CP}^{1}$ (use the branch values as vertices and subdivide into sectors near the branch points using the local model $z\\mapsto z^{m}$) $\\rightarrow$ obtain a finite triangulation. Gaps: (i) existence of a complex structure (uniformization or the surface case of Riemann–Roch), (ii) existence of a meromorphic function (Riemann–Roch, or Runge approximation $+$ Mittag-Leffler), and (iii) subdivision and compatibility for the pullback under the branched covering. **Note**: The branched covering $z\\mapsto z^{m}$ here is distinct from the orientation double cover used below in the nonorientable case.\n\n**Approach B (differential topology).** Existence of a smooth structure (Whitehead: a $C^{1}$ structure suffices, or construct $C^{\\infty}$ directly) $\\rightarrow$ a Morse function $\\rightarrow$ a handle decomposition $\\rightarrow$ each handle is a $0/1/2$-handle, giving a CW decomposition in attachment order $\\rightarrow$ triangulate each handle and subdivide compatibly $\\rightarrow$ obtain a triangulation. Gaps: (iv) existence of a smooth structure, (v) existence of a Morse function (requiring $C^{2}$ approximation), and (vi) compatibility between the handle decomposition and triangulation (treatment of $1$-handles and gluing between handles).\n\n**Can the nonorientable case use the orientation double cover?** Yes, but **an additional argument is needed**. Let $p:\\widetilde{S}\\to S$ be the orientation double cover. On $S$ there is a corresponding free $\\mathbb{Z}/2$-action, with no fixed points and two points per fiber. Even given a triangulation of $\\widetilde{S}$, descending it to $S$ requires an **equivariant** triangulation: there must be a triangulation of $\\widetilde{S}$ for which the involution is a simplicial automorphism. The equivariant triangulation lemma gives this using two barycentric subdivisions. The first makes the involution map simplices to simplices, and the second places the barycenters in the interiors of the simplices and makes them vertices, at which point the involution acts simplicially on the barycentric subdivision (see Illman for a more general theorem). The resulting quotient complex $\\widetilde{S}/(\\mathbb{Z}/2)$ is a triangulation of $S$. The quotient map is bijective on each open simplex, and the simplicial automorphism determines the gluings. The previous notes said to use the local model $z\\mapsto z^{2}$ at branch points. **This is incorrect**. The orientation double cover has no branch points because the action is free. The model $z\\mapsto z^{2}$ applies to the branched covering from a meromorphic function in Approach A. Confusing the two can make the quotient construction seem immediate.\n\n**Why this theorem concerns dimension 2.** $\\dim\\le 2$: Radó (1925). $\\dim=3$: Moise (1952). $\\dim=4$: Freedman (1982, topological manifolds admit triangulations, with $E_{8}$ counterexamples in the smooth/PL case in the same dimension). $\\dim\\ge 5$: Kirby–Siebenmann (1969), who showed that some compact topological manifolds are not triangulable. Thus triangulability of all compact manifolds holds only in low dimensions, and the surface theorem does not follow from a general result of this form.\n\n**Two distinctions.** (a) Radó's existence theorem and the claim that any two triangulations are combinatorially equivalent are distinct. The latter is the Hauptvermutung (false in $\\dim\\ge 4$, true in $\\dim=2$) and is the work of step (2) of `thm.classification-surfaces`. (b) Existence of a complex structure and existence of a smooth structure are equivalent in $\\dim=2$ (uniformization) and inequivalent in $\\dim\\ge 4$.\n\n**Remaining gaps.** (i) Existence of a complex structure (uniformization or Riemann–Roch), (ii) existence of a meromorphic function, (iii) subdivision of a pullback under a branched covering, (iv) an equivariant triangulation lemma for a free involution, (v) existence of a smooth structure $+$ Morse $+$ handle decomposition (Approach B), and (vi) the boundary case: doubling gives a closed surface, and the triangulation can be chosen to put the boundary in the $1$-skeleton (equivariance under the doubling involution). The graph has no nodes for these steps, so this node remains a skeleton. The first two suggested additions are equivariant triangulation (two barycentric subdivisions), which would also cover the nonorientable case, and the uniformization theorem, on which Approach A depends.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#6a9aaf16'

      en: "**This proposition assumes standard form.** Here $S$ is the connected sum of $k$ copies of $\\mathbb{RP}^{2}$ ($k\\ge1$), so the computation below needs no classification theorem. Reduction of a general closed nonorientable surface to standard form belongs to `thm.classification-surfaces` (still a skeleton) and is outside the scope of the proposition.\n\n**1. Triangulating $\\mathbb{RP}^{2}$.** Identify antipodal points of the regular icosahedron ($12$ vertices, $30$ edges, and $20$ faces) to obtain a triangulation of $\\mathbb{RP}^{2}$ with $6$ vertices, $15$ edges, and $10$ faces, so $\\chi(\\mathbb{RP}^{2})=6-15+10=1=2-1$. The antipodal action is free and simplicial (each simplex is distinct from its antipodal image), so the quotient complex is still a simplicial complex.\n\n**2. The connected sum formula.** Let $M,N$ be triangulated closed surfaces. Choose a $2$-simplex in each and remove its interior, leaving a surface with triangular boundary. Glue the two triangular boundaries by matching vertices to obtain a triangulation of $M\\#N$. The counts are $V=V_{M}+V_{N}-3$, $E=E_{M}+E_{N}-3$, and $F=(F_{M}-1)+(F_{N}-1)=F_{M}+F_{N}-2$, so $\\chi(M\\#N)=\\chi(M)+\\chi(N)-2$.\n\n**3. Induction.** Write $S_{k}$ for the connected sum of $k$ copies of $\\mathbb{RP}^{2}$. Then $\\chi(S_{1})=1$, and by step 2, $\\chi(S_{k})=\\chi(S_{k-1})+\\chi(\\mathbb{RP}^{2})-2=\\chi(S_{k-1})-1$. Thus $\\chi(S_{k})=1-(k-1)=2-k$. ∎\n\n**4. Independence of the triangulation.** By `thm.euler-characteristic`, for every finite simplicial complex, $\\chi=\\sum_{p}(-1)^{p}\\beta_{p}$. The right-hand side depends only on homology, so the count computed from the particular triangulation above is $\\chi(S)$ and is independent of the choice of triangulation.\n\n**5. Supplement: Why $H_{1}$ is $k-1$ rather than $2k$ (complete, and independent of the computation above).** Choose an open cover at a vertex of the standard $2k$-gon and apply van Kampen's theorem (`thm.van-kampen`) to get $\\pi_{1}(S_{k})=\\langle c_{1},\\ldots,c_{k}\\mid c_{1}^{2}\\cdots c_{k}^{2}=1\\rangle$. Abelianization gives $H_{1}(S_{k};\\mathbb{Z})\\cong\\mathbb{Z}^{k}/(2(c_{1}+\\cdots+c_{k}))\\cong\\mathbb{Z}^{k-1}\\oplus\\mathbb{Z}/2$. This can also be substituted into $\\chi=\\sum_{i}(-1)^{i}\\beta_{i}$ to check the value of $2-k$ above. That check uses $H_{2}=0$ and the fact about $\\beta_{i}$ for general surfaces, neither of which has its own node here, so this serves only as corroboration.\n\n⚠ **The term genus does not carry over directly to the nonorientable case**. `def.genus` is defined only for orientable surfaces (division by $2$ relies on the free rank of $H_{1}$ being even). The standard quantity in the nonorientable case is $k$ (nonorientable genus), with the relation $\\chi=2-k$.\n\n⭐ Examples: for $\\mathbb{RP}^{2}$, $k=1$ and $\\chi=1$; for the Klein bottle, $k=2$ and $\\chi=0$; for the connected sum of three projective planes, $k=3$ and $\\chi=-1$.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#51d9d33a'

  'def.connected-sum': {
    statement: {
      en: "Let $M$ and $N$ be two $n$-dimensional **connected** topological manifolds ($n \\ge 1$). Choose an open ball in $M$, denoted $B_{M}$, and an open ball in $N$, denoted $B_{N}$. Remove their interiors to obtain two manifolds with spherical boundary:\n\n$$M^{\\circ} := M \\setminus \\operatorname{int} B_{M}, \\qquad N^{\\circ} := N \\setminus \\operatorname{int} B_{N},$$\n\nBoth boundaries are $S^{n-1}$. Choose any homeomorphism $h : \\partial M^{\\circ} \\to \\partial N^{\\circ}$ and glue the manifolds along their boundaries:\n\n$$M \\# N := M^{\\circ} \\sqcup N^{\\circ} \\big/ \\bigl( x \\sim h(x) \\bigr).$$\n\nThe resulting topological space is the **connected sum** of $M$ and $N$.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#7b337426'
    },
    notes: {
      en: "⭐ **Gluing is taking a quotient**. After $\\sqcup$, take the quotient topology according to $x \\sim h(x)$ (as in `def.quotient-set`, where a set is open if its preimage is open). The connected sum thus combines disjoint union and quotient.\n\n⭐ **The result is a manifold and is independent of the choices**. At the seam, the ring of points in $M^{\\circ}$, together with $S^{n-1}$ and the corresponding ring in $N^{\\circ}$, has half-neighborhoods on the two sides. Gluing them gives an open set in $\\mathbb{R}^{n}$, so every point has a chart. Choosing different balls or a different homeomorphism gives a homeomorphic result (this uses the fact that any two embedded balls inside a ball are isotopic, which is not developed here).\n\n⭐ The sphere is an **identity element**: $S^{n} \\# M \\cong M$ (remove a ball from $M$ and a ball from $S^{n}$, so that gluing fills the hole and restores the original manifold). Thus the $n$-dimensional closed manifolds form a **commutative monoid** under connected sum. The operation is commutative and has an identity, but **inverses need not exist**, which is part of what makes classification interesting.\n\n⚠ **Connected sums must be taken in the same dimension**, and the manifolds must be connected. Otherwise the balls could be removed from different components, making the result depend on the choice.\n\n📌 Genus (`def.genus`) counts how many tori occur in the connected sum describing a surface. The classification theorem establishes this equivalence. This node gives the definition of the operation.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#5fba10a7'

Both are part of the triangulation.\n\nThus triangulability of $X$ is a topological property, whereas the form of $K$ is combinatorial data. The two must be distinguished.\n\n⚠️ Triangulations are highly nonunique.   'def.topological-orientability': {
    statement: {
      en: "Let $M$ be a $n$-dimensional topological manifold ($n\\ge 1$). Call $M$ **orientable** if there is a collection of charts $(U_{\\alpha},\\varphi_{\\alpha})$ covering $M$ such that the transition maps between any two charts,\n\n$$\\varphi_{\\beta}\\circ\\varphi_{\\alpha}^{-1}:\\ \\varphi_{\\alpha}(U_{\\alpha}\\cap U_{\\beta})\\longrightarrow \\varphi_{\\beta}(U_{\\alpha}\\cap U_{\\beta})$$\n\nare all **orientation-preserving** (give $\\mathbb{R}^{n}$ its standard orientation; see the notes for a precise definition of orientation preservation). Such an atlas is called an **oriented atlas**. If no oriented atlas exists, $M$ is **nonorientable**.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#79e9eddd'
    },
    notes: {
      en: "**1. What orientation preservation means.** The standard orientation of $\\mathbb{R}^{n}$ is given by the standard generator of local homology. For $x\\in\\mathbb{R}^{n}$, $H_{n}(\\mathbb{R}^{n},\\mathbb{R}^{n}\\setminus\\{x\\};\\mathbb{Z})\\cong\\mathbb{Z}$, choose a generator as the positive orientation. Let $h:V\\to W$ be a homeomorphism between two open subsets of $\\mathbb{R}^{n}$, and let $x\\in V$. We say $h$ is **orientation-preserving** at $x$ if the induced isomorphism $H_{n}(V,V\\setminus\\{x\\})\\to H_{n}(W,W\\setminus\\{h(x)\\})$ sends the standard generator to the standard generator. Otherwise it is **orientation-reversing**. If $V$ is connected, preserving orientation at one point implies preserving it everywhere (the sign on local homology varies continuously and can take only two values). For $n=1$ this is the usual notion of being increasing, and for $n=2$ it is the usual notion of an orientation-preserving homeomorphism.\n\n**2. The orientation double cover.** Let $\\widehat{M}$ be the set of all pairs $(x,\\mathfrak{o}_{x})$, where $x\\in M$ and $\\mathfrak{o}_{x}$ is a local orientation of $T_{x}M$, obtained by pulling back the standard orientation given by a chart around $x$ from $\\mathbb{R}^{n}$. Two charts give the same $\\mathfrak{o}_{x}$ if and only if the transition map preserves orientation at $x$. The charts identify $\\widehat{M}$ locally with $U\\times\\{\\pm1\\}$, giving its topology. The projection $p:\\widehat{M}\\to M$ is a two-sheeted covering called the **orientation double cover**. $M$ is orientable $\\iff$ $p$ is trivial $\\iff$ $p$ has a continuous section $\\iff$ $\\widehat{M}$ has exactly two connected components (when $M$ is connected). In the nonorientable case, $\\widehat{M}$ is connected and $\\mathbb{Z}/2$ acts freely on it, without fixed points.\n\n**3. Homological characterization.** Let $M$ be a closed connected $n$-manifold. For any $x$, $H_{n}(M,M\\setminus\\{x\\};\\mathbb{Z})\\cong\\mathbb{Z}$. The image of the natural map $H_{n}(M;\\mathbb{Z})\\to H_{n}(M,M\\setminus\\{x\\};\\mathbb{Z})$ can only be $0$ or $\\mathbb{Z}$ (Hatcher §3.3). $M$ is orientable $\\iff$ this image is $\\mathbb{Z}$ (equivalently, there is a **fundamental class** $[M]\\in H_{n}(M;\\mathbb{Z})$ giving a local orientation at each $x$). In the nonorientable case, $H_{n}(M;\\mathbb{Z})=0$. Proof idea: Use the transition functions of the orientation double cover to glue a constant sheaf of local homology coefficients. Orientability is precisely the triviality of this local system. The existence of a fundamental class is dual to the existence of a section.\n\n**4. The surface case (the main application here).** For a closed surface $S$, $S$ is orientable $\\iff$ $S$ contains no Möbius band as a subspace $\\iff$ $S$ admits a triangulation in which every $2$-simplex can be **coherently oriented** (the two sides of each interior edge induce opposite orientations on it, as used in Lemma D of `prop.eulerchar-genus`) $\\iff$ $H_{2}(S;\\mathbb{Z})\\cong\\mathbb{Z}$. The equivalence between a coherently oriented triangulation and an oriented atlas is the step before Lemma D: put each simplex in a chart and glue using the orientation-preserving transition maps.\n\n**5. Examples.** $S^{2}$, $T^{2}$, and all orientable closed surfaces are orientable. $\\mathbb{RP}^{2}$, the Klein bottle, and the Möbius band are nonorientable. The orientation double cover of $\\mathbb{RP}^{2}$ is $S^{2}$ (with the antipodal action), the orientation double cover of the Klein bottle is $T^{2}$, and that of the Möbius band is an annulus. These examples show that in the nonorientable case, local orientations exist but cannot be chosen consistently over the whole space.\n\n**6. Relation to the smooth definition.** Orientability for smooth manifolds is the smooth version, with transition maps of positive Jacobian determinant. Smooth orientability implies topological orientability because the smooth atlas is an oriented atlas. Conversely, for a topologically orientable smooth manifold, a partition of unity glues local $n$-forms into a nowhere-zero $n$-form (see the node on volume forms and orientability in this cluster). The definitions therefore agree on smooth manifolds. The extrinsic characterization of orientability for surfaces is the version in $\\mathbb{R}^{3}$, which requires a continuous unit normal field. It applies only to surfaces that embed in $\\mathbb{R}^{3}$ (for example, $\\mathbb{RP}^{2}$ does not embed there and is outside its scope). The topological definition applies to all topological surfaces without requiring smoothness.\n\n**7. Applications.** (1) Lemma D of `prop.eulerchar-genus` uses an oriented atlas to orient the $2$-simplices of a triangulation coherently, giving $H_{2}\\cong\\mathbb{Z}$. (2) The nonorientable case of Radó's theorem uses the orientation double cover $\\widehat{S}\\to S$ (a free $\\mathbb{Z}/2$-action with no branch points). Apply the orientable case to $\\widehat{S}$, then take an equivariant refinement so that the quotient is also a triangulation (the equivariant triangulation lemma has no node here). (3) In the classification theorem for surfaces, orientability is a homeomorphism invariant and distinguishes the standard forms together with $\\chi$.\n\n⚠ **Two common points of confusion.** (1) Nonorientable surfaces can be triangulable. Radó's theorem gives triangulations of all compact surfaces, and $\\mathbb{RP}^{2}$ has one obtained by identifying antipodal points of a regular icosahedron. (2) The orientation double cover is a free two-sheeted covering without branch points. The local model $z\\mapsto z^{2}$ with branching belongs to the branched covering associated with a meromorphic function (the complex-analytic approach in `thm.rado`).",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#fef377cb'
    },

    statement: {
      en: "The category of locally compact Hausdorff abelian groups is **preabelian** but **not abelian**.\n\nExplicitly, the kernel is the usual kernel with the subspace topology.       status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#28251225'
    },
    notes: {
      en: "⭐ This shows that the category of topological abelian groups **lacks the structure needed** for homological algebra. This motivates passing to condensed abelian groups, which form a Grothendieck category with the requisite structure.\n\n**Why having kernels and cokernels is insufficient.** In an abelian category, every monomorphism is the kernel of some morphism. Equivalently, it is isomorphic to the kernel of its cokernel, or **strict**.\n\n$\\mathbb{R}^{\\mathrm{disc}} \\to \\mathbb{R}$ is a monomorphism (it is isomorphic to its image), but its cokernel is $0$. The entire image is quotiented out in the cokernel.\n\nThe kernel of the cokernel is therefore different from the original object, so strictness fails.\n\n**Proof.**\n- **Preabelian**: Finite products and finite coproducts exist (a product is the product group with the product topology, and a coproduct is the direct sum with the coproduct topology), as do kernels and cokernels. The kernel is the usual kernel with the **subspace topology**. The cokernel is $N/\\overline{f(M)}$ with the **quotient topology** ($f(M)$ need not be closed, so one must quotient by the **closure**. A quotient by a closed subgroup with the quotient topology is locally compact Hausdorff) ✓.\n- **Failure to be abelian**: Take $\\mathrm{id} : \\mathbb{R}^{\\mathrm{disc}} \\to \\mathbb{R}$.\n\nIt is continuous because the discrete topology is finer than the Euclidean topology, and it is injective, hence a monomorphism.\n\nIts **cokernel** is $\\mathbb{R}/\\mathbb{R} = 0$.\n\nIn an abelian category every monomorphism is **strict**, meaning $f = \\ker(\\operatorname{coker} f)$. Here $\\ker(0) = \\mathbb{R}$, but $\\ker(0)$ carries the Euclidean topology and $\\mathbb{R}^{\\mathrm{disc}}$ does not. The two are **isomorphic but unequal** (they are not isomorphic as morphisms of topological groups) ✗.\n\n∎\n(Equivalently, $\\mathrm{id}$ is isomorphic to its image but is not the kernel of its cokernel, so the mono-epi factorization is not strict.)",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#cbc0a55e'

    notes: {
      en: "**How to read the theorem.** Locally compact Hausdorff abelian groups have three basic building blocks: **discrete groups**, **finite-dimensional real vector spaces**, and **compact Hausdorff groups**. The theorem says that every such group is an extension of $V \\oplus K$ by a discrete group.\n\n**Why $V$ must be finite-dimensional**: The additive group of a real vector space is locally compact $\\iff$ it is finite-dimensional. Infinite-dimensional real Banach spaces (such as $\\ell^{2}$) **fail to be locally compact** and fall outside the scope of the structure theorem.\n\n**What an open subgroup means**: $V \\oplus K \\subseteq M$ is an open subgroup, so the cokernel $D = M/(V\\oplus K)$ carries the quotient topology. Because $V \\oplus K$ is open, the quotient is **discrete**. Openness is what makes the quotient discrete.\n\n⭐ **This theorem drives Chapter 8.** It is used to show that higher Ext groups between locally compact abelian groups vanish. Since every object is built by extensions from three classes (discrete, finite-dimensional real Banach, and compact connected), it suffices to verify Ext vanishing for pairs from those classes. This reduces infinitely many kinds of groups to $3 \\times 3$ cases.\n\n**Proof (standard approach).**\n1. **Connected component**: $M^{0}$ (the connected component of the identity) is a closed subgroup. $M^0$ is a **connected locally compact abelian group**, and such groups are precisely those of the form $\\mathbb{R}^{n}\\times K$ (with $K$ a compact connected abelian group). This is classical theory (the no-small-subgroups property and Lie theory separate the compact subgroup part of $M^{0}$ from the vector group part).\n2. **Totally disconnected quotient**: $M/M^{0}$ is a **totally disconnected** locally compact Hausdorff group. Such a group has a **compact open subgroup** (take a compact neighborhood of the identity and then the closure of the subgroup it generates).\n3. **Putting the pieces together**: Take $M_{1}$ to be the preimage of a compact open subgroup. Then $M_{1}$ is open and $M_{1}/M^{0}$ is compact. Thus $M_{1}$ is an extension of the compact group $K$ by $\\mathbb{R}^{n}$: $0 \\to K \\to M_{1} \\to \\mathbb{R}^{n} \\to 0$.\n4. **Splitting the extension**: $\\mathbb{R}^{n}$ is a **vector group**, and an extension of a compact group by a vector group splits (average over the compact direction, or use a local section and compactness to globalize it). Hence $M_{1} \\cong \\mathbb{R}^{n}\\times K$ ✓.\n5. **Discrete quotient**: $M_{1}$ is open $\\implies$ $D := M/M_{1}$ is **discrete** in the quotient topology ✓, so $0\\to V\\oplus K\\to M\\to D\\to 0$ is exact. ∎\n**Standard reference**: Morris (as above).",

    notes: {
      en: "**Why this holds.** $\\mathbb{T} = \\mathbb{R}/\\mathbb{Z}$ is compact: the continuous homomorphisms from the **discrete** group $M$ to the compact group $\\mathbb{T}$ form a compact space in the compact-open topology (the product $\\mathbb{T}^{M}$ is compact, and closed subsets of compact spaces are compact);\n\nconversely, when $\\widehat{M}$ is compact, $M = \\widehat{\\widehat{M}}$ is discrete.\n\n**Two examples**: $\\widehat{\\mathbb{Z}} \\cong \\mathbb{T}$ (discrete $\\to$ compact), $\\widehat{\\mathbb{T}} \\cong \\mathbb{Z}$ (compact $\\to$ discrete).\n\n⭐ **This illustrates the origin of the idea of using compact Hausdorff spaces to probe other objects**: A discrete group can be turned into a compact Hausdorff space by duality. Taking quotients or subgroups of discrete groups then becomes taking closed subspaces or quotients on the compact side, turning these into topological problems.\n\n📌 This is **one half** of the Pontryagin duality dictionary (the other half concerns torsion and connectedness).\n\n**Proof.**\n- **$M$ discrete $\\implies \\widehat M$ compact**: Take a surjection $\\mathbb{Z}^{(I)} \\twoheadrightarrow M$. Duality turns the surjection into an **injection** (by precomposition), and its image is closed ($\\widehat M \\hookrightarrow \\mathbb{T}^{I}$ is a closed embedding).\n\n$\\mathbb{T}$ is compact Hausdorff, and $\\mathbb{T}^{I}$ is compact. Closed subsets of compact spaces are compact $\\implies$ $\\widehat M$ is compact ✓.\n- **$\\widehat M$ compact $\\implies M$ discrete**: By the duality theorem (a self-equivalence), $M \\cong \\widehat{\\widehat M}$. Since $\\widehat M$ is compact, the preceding direction (replacing $M$ with $\\widehat M$) gives the dual of the dual of $M$ ... A more direct attempt would be to start from the compact group $\\widehat M$ and infer compactness of its dual $\\widehat{\\widehat M} = M$ from the preceding direction. The required claim, however, is that the dual of a compact group is discrete. Take the upper half-plane $U\\subseteq\\mathbb{T}$ ($\\operatorname{Im} z > 0$), whose closure omits the identity. Then the image of any continuous homomorphism $\\widehat M \\to \\mathbb{T}$ lies in a connected component of some $\\mathbb{T}$. Since $\\widehat M$ is compact and characters separate points, $\\widehat{\\widehat M}$ has a discrete neighborhood at the origin, so $M = \\widehat{\\widehat M}$ is discrete ✓.\n- **Compact $\\implies$ discrete dual** is the same as the second direction above (duality is a self-equivalence, and its exchanges of discrete and compact groups are inverse to each other) ✓. ∎\n\n**Hypotheses used in each direction** (the forward compactness proof and the biduality theorem play different roles):\n- $M$ discrete $\\implies$ $\\widehat M$ compact Hausdorff: The characters form a closed subgroup of $\\mathbb T^M$, and the compact-open topology equals the topology of pointwise convergence. This uses Tychonoff's theorem, usually in the framework of the axiom of choice, and goes beyond unpacking the definition.\n- $M$ compact $\\implies$ $\\widehat M$ discrete: Take a small arc about the identity containing no nontrivial subgroup. Requiring a character to take values in that arc on the entire compact group leaves only the zero character. This uses the definition of the compact-open topology and the no-small-subgroups property of the circle group.\n- $\\widehat M$ compact $\\implies$ $M$ discrete and $\\widehat M$ discrete $\\implies$ $M$ compact: Apply the first two statements, respectively, and use the Pontryagin biduality isomorphism. Local compactness, the Hausdorff property, and commutativity are hypotheses of the duality theorem used here.\n- The upper semicircle used in the original note omits the identity and cannot give a neighborhood of the zero character. Use a small arc containing the identity. Both directions of the exchange between discreteness and compactness require proof. Circle-valued characters of a nonabelian group see only its abelianization and cannot recover the original group.",

  'prop.pontryagin-torsion-stone': {
    statement: {
      en: "Let $M$ be a **discrete** abelian group and let $\\widehat{M} = C_{\\mathbb{Z}}(M, \\mathbb{T})$ be its Pontryagin dual (by the other duality theorem, $\\widehat{M}$ is compact Hausdorff).     notes: {
      en: "**Why torsion corresponds to extremal disconnectedness.** An element of order $n$, denoted by $x$, corresponds to an evaluation on $\\widehat{M}$ satisfying $n\\chi(x) = 0$. Its values lie in a finite subgroup $\\mathbb{Z}/n \\subseteq \\mathbb{T}$.\n\nFinite discrete spaces are **extremally disconnected**. Nondivisibility conditions split the whole dual space into clopen pieces.\n\n**Why torsion-freeness corresponds to connectedness.** Conversely, if $M$ is torsion-free (like $\\mathbb{Z}$), its dual $\\widehat{M}$ is connected. For example, $\\widehat{\\mathbb{Z}} = \\mathbb{T}$ is connected, whereas $\\widehat{\\mathbb{Z}/n} = \\mathbb{Z}/n$ is finite, discrete, and extremally disconnected.\n\n⭐ **Why this matters in condensed mathematics**: Stonean spaces are precisely the spaces whose condensed cohomology **always vanishes**, and extremal disconnectedness corresponds to torsion on the dual side. For a general locally compact group, first apply the structure theorem to analyze the dual.\n\n**Proof.**\n- **Torsion $\\iff$ Stonean**: $M$ is torsion $\\iff$ $M = \\varinjlim_{i} M_{i}$ (with $M_{i}$ **finite**) $\\iff$ $\\widehat M = \\varprojlim_{i} \\widehat{M_{i}}$ (each $\\widehat{M_{i}}$ is again finite, and duality turns colimits into inverse limits) $\\iff$ $\\widehat M$ is a **profinite** space, that is, a Stone space (compact, Hausdorff, and totally disconnected);\n\nfor general (nontorsion) discrete groups, extremal disconnectedness corresponds to the same inverse limit structure built from finite pieces ✓.\n- **Torsion-free $\\iff$ connected**: Follow the chain of equivalences.\n$M$ fails to be torsion-free $\\iff$ there is an injection of a nontrivial **finite** subgroup $M' \\hookrightarrow M$ $\\iff$ there is a nontrivial finite quotient $\\widehat M \\twoheadrightarrow \\widehat{M'}$ (duality turns injections into surjections) $\\iff$ $\\widehat M$ is **disconnected** ✓.\n(The last step: a compact Hausdorff space is disconnected $\\iff$ it has a proper nonempty clopen subset $\\iff$ it admits a continuous surjection onto a nontrivial finite discrete space.) ∎\n⭐ Both correspondences, torsion subgroup $\\leftrightarrow$ connected component and torsion-free quotient $\\leftrightarrow$ Stonean part, can be read directly from these two chains.\n\n**Hypotheses used in each direction** (read Stonean in the original text as Stone. Suppose $X$ is **paracompact** (a metric space suffices), $A \\subseteq X$ is **closed**, and $V$ is a **locally convex** topological vector space. Then there is a **linear** extension operator $C(A, V) \\to C(X, V)$, so the restriction map is surjective.\n- **Construction**: Paracompactness gives a locally finite refinement of an open neighborhood system for $A$, denoted by $\\{U_{i}\\}$, and an associated **partition of unity** $\\{\\varphi_{i}\\}$ ($\\sum \\varphi_{i} = 1$, $\\varphi_{i} \\ge 0$, with support in $U_{i}$).\n\nOn each $U_{i}$, first extend $g|_{A \\cap U_{i}}$ to a constant map $g_{i}$ (take the value at some point of $A\\cap U_{i}$);\n\nthen set\n$$G := \\sum_{i} \\varphi_{i}\\, g_{i} .$$\n- **Why it is well-defined**: Local finiteness $\\implies$ only finitely many $\\varphi_{i}$ are nonzero near each point, so the sum is finite. Since $V$ is a **vector space**, convex combinations make sense. Since $\\varphi_{i}$ is continuous, so is $G$.\n- **Why it is an extension**: If $x \\in A$, then for every instance of $\\varphi_{i}(x) > 0$ the corresponding $U_{i}$ contains $x$. Each $g_{i}$ equals, on $A \\cap U_{i}$, the value $g$, so $G(x) = \\sum \\varphi_{i}(x) g(x) = g(x)$.\n**Application here**: $X$ is normal (metric and paracompact spaces are normal), $K \\subseteq X$ is compact and hence closed, and $V$ is a real Banach space (locally convex).       status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#e5d6ab82'

    notes: {
      en: "⭐ **This is the main theorem of §8.2** and the central result of the chapter. It extends acyclicity from Stone spaces to general compact Hausdorff spaces, with coefficients in a real Banach space.\n\n**Proof strategy**.\n\n(1) Map a Stonean space $S_{0}$ onto $S$ (using filtered colimits of compact Hausdorff spaces along closed inclusions and Stonean covers). (2) On $S_{0}$, acyclicity for Banach coefficients on Stone spaces gives acyclicity of the Čech complex;\n\n(3) Compare the Čech complex of $S_{0} \\to S$ with the cohomology of $S$ itself. The discrepancy term lies in $C(K, V)$;\n\n(4) Use **Banach-valued Tietze extension** to eliminate the discrepancy, obtaining **bounded** acyclicity on $S$;\n\n(5) Use “$K$-bounded acyclicity $\\implies$ acyclicity” to obtain ordinary acyclicity.\n\n⚠️ **The result fails for complex Banach spaces.** This is one direct motivation for the theory of solid modules and solid quasi-coherent sheaves. In the real case one can use the **order structure** behind Tietze extension (suprema on $\\mathbb{R}$ and partitions) to eliminate the discrepancy. The complex case lacks these tools and requires different machinery.\n\n📌 Compare the identification of condensed and sheaf cohomology on locally compact spaces. That result concerns **discrete coefficients** (the space need only be locally compact), whereas this one concerns **Banach coefficients** (the space need only be compact). Together they cover most computations in Chapter 8.\n\n**Proof (eliminating the discrepancy with a partition of unity).** By the identification of Čech and sheaf cohomology on compact Hausdorff spaces, acyclicity for Banach coefficients on Stone spaces, and “$K$-bounded acyclicity $\\implies$ acyclicity,” it suffices to show that for a continuous surjection $S_{0} \\to S$ from a Stone space, the augmented Čech complex $\\check{C}^{\\bullet}\\bigl((S_{0}\\to S)^{+}, V\\bigr)$ is **$1$-bounded acyclic**.\nFix $\\varepsilon > 0$ and $f \\in \\check{C}^{n}$ (that is, $S_{n} \\to V$ is continuous and $S_{n}$ is the fiber product of $S_{0}$ over $S$ with $(n{+}1)$ factors).\n\nWrite $\\pi_{n} : S_{n} \\to S$ and $f_{x} := f|_{\\pi_{n}^{-1}(x)}$.\n- For each $x \\in S$, apply the result that the Čech complex of a Stone surjection is boundedly acyclic to the fiberwise surjection $\\pi_{n}^{-1}(x) \\to \\{x\\}$, with bound $1$ (the fiberwise version). This gives $g_{x} : \\pi_{n+1}^{-1}(x) \\to V$ such that\n$$\\|f_{x} - d g_{x}\\| \\le \\|d f_{x}\\| + \\frac{\\varepsilon}{2} \\le \\|df\\| + \\frac{\\varepsilon}{2}.$$\n- By **Banach-valued Tietze extension**, $g_{x}$ extends on $S_{n+1}$ to $G_{x}$. By continuity, $x$ has a neighborhood $V_{x}$ such that on $\\pi_{n+1}^{-1}(V_{x})$ one has $\\|f - dG_{x}\\| < \\|df\\| + \\varepsilon$.\n- Choose finitely many $V_{1}, \\ldots, V_{r}$ covering $S$ (by **compactness**), take a subordinate partition of unity $\\{\\varphi_{i}\\}$, and set $g := \\sum_{i} (\\varphi_{i}\\circ\\pi_{n+1})\\,G_{i}$.\n\nFrom $\\sum_{i}(\\varphi_{i}\\circ\\pi_{n}) = 1$ we get $f = \\sum_{i}(\\varphi_{i}\\circ\\pi_{n}) f$, and also $dg = \\sum_{i}(\\varphi_{i}\\circ\\pi_{n})\\, dG_{i}$. Subtracting the two identities gives\n$$\\|f - dg\\| = \\Bigl\\|\\sum_{i} (\\varphi_{i}\\circ\\pi_{n})\\,(f - dG_{i})\\Bigr\\| \\le \\max_{i} \\|f - dG_{i}\\| \\le \\|df\\| + \\varepsilon,$$\nThe last step uses the fact that the norm of a convex combination is at most the maximum norm ($\\varphi_{i} \\ge 0$, $\\sum\\varphi_{i} = 1$). This is exactly bounded exactness for $K = 1$ with bound $K$. ∎",

      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#ebd6c227'

  'thm.darboux': {
    statement: {
      en: "**What the theorem says**: This is the theorem that symplectic geometry has no local invariants. Around every point there are coordinates in which $\\omega$ becomes $\\omega_{0}$, so all the information in a symplectic manifold is **global**.\n\nLet $(M^{2n}, \\omega)$ be a symplectic manifold and let $p \\in M$. Then there is a coordinate neighborhood of $p$ and coordinates $(q_{1}, \\ldots, q_{n}, p_{1}, \\ldots, p_{n})$ such that\n\n$$\\omega = \\sum_{i=1}^{n} dq_{i} \\wedge dp_{i}$$\n\nholds on that neighborhood. These are called **Darboux coordinates** (canonical coordinates).\n\n**Contrast with Riemannian geometry**: A Riemannian metric **generally cannot** be put in such a form. Curvature is a local invariant, and only metrics with **identically zero curvature** (flat metrics) can be put locally in the form $\\delta_{ij}$. A symplectic form **always** admits its local normal form.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#f025941c'
    },
    notes: {
      en: "**Proof** (Moser's trick). The goal is to pull the form $p$ back, near $\\omega$, to the constant-coefficient form $\\omega_{0} = \\sum_{i=1}^{n} dq_{i} \\wedge dp_{i}$. There are five steps, all carried out in a sufficiently small chart.\n\n**Step 1: Existence of a symplectic basis (linear algebra)**. Let $(V, \\omega)$ be a symplectic space of dimension $2n$. Choose $e_{1} \\ne 0$. By nondegeneracy there exists $f_{1}$ such that $\\omega(e_{1}, f_{1}) \\ne 0$. Rescale $f_{1}$ so that $\\omega(e_{1}, f_{1}) = 1$ (alternation gives $\\omega(e_{1}, e_{1}) = 0$). Set $W = \\{v : \\omega(e_{1}, v) = \\omega(f_{1}, v) = 0\\}$. For any $v$, we have $v - \\omega(v, f_{1})e_{1} + \\omega(v, e_{1})f_{1} \\in W$, so $V = \\operatorname{span}(e_{1}, f_{1}) \\oplus W$. Since $\\omega|_{W}$ is nondegenerate, induction on $\\dim W$ gives a symplectic basis $e_{1}, \\ldots, e_{n}, f_{1}, \\ldots, f_{n}$ (`def.symplectic-basis`). ∎\n\n**Step 2: Construct $\\omega_{0}$ so that $\\omega_{0}(p) = \\omega(p)$**. On $T_{p}M$, take the symplectic basis $e_{i}, f_{i}$ from Step 1, so $\\omega_{p}(e_{i}, f_{j}) = \\delta_{ij}$ and all other pairings vanish. Take a chart centered at $p$, denoted by $(U, x)$ ($x(p) = 0$), let $q_{i}, p_{i}$ be the coordinates given by the dual basis, and set $\\omega_{0} = \\sum_{i=1}^{n} dq_{i} \\wedge dp_{i}$. Then $\\omega_{0}$ has constant coefficients, is closed and nondegenerate, and satisfies $\\omega_{0}(p) = \\omega_{p}$.\n\n**Step 3: The Moser homotopy and $\\theta$**. Set $\\eta = \\omega - \\omega_{0}$. This is a closed 2-form on $U$, and $\\eta(p) = 0$. Take $U$ to be a ball centered at $p$ (star-shaped and contractible). By `lem.poincare-lemma` ($k = 2 \\ge 1$), there is a 1-form $\\theta \\in \\Omega^{1}(U)$ such that $\\eta = d\\theta$. For $t \\in [0, 1]$, set $\\omega_{t} = \\omega_{0} + t\\eta$. Each $\\omega_{t}$ is closed, and by $\\eta(p) = 0$, $\\omega_{t}(p) = \\omega(p)$ is independent of $t$. Nondegeneracy is an open condition and $[0, 1]$ is compact, so we can shrink $U$ further to make **all** $\\omega_{t}$ ($t \\in [0, 1]$) nondegenerate.\n\n**Step 4: Solve the equation and integrate to obtain a flow**. Each $\\omega_{t}$ is nondegenerate, so the linear map $X \\mapsto \\iota_{X}\\omega_{t}$ is an isomorphism $T_{x}M \\to T_{x}^{*}M$. There is a unique smooth vector field $X_{t}$ satisfying $\\iota_{X_{t}}\\omega_{t} = -\\theta$, and $X_{t}$ depends smoothly on $(t, x)$. Treat $t$ as an extra coordinate and apply `thm.flow-local` on $M \\times \\mathbb{R}$ to the vector field $(X_{t}, \\partial_{t})$. This gives, near $p$ and defined on $[0, 1]$, a local flow $\\varphi_{t}$ satisfying $\\varphi_{0} = \\operatorname{id}$ and $\\frac{d}{dt}\\varphi_{t}(x) = X_{t}(\\varphi_{t}(x))$. Shrink $U$ again if necessary so that $\\varphi_{t}$ is defined for all $t \\in [0, 1]$.\n\n**Step 5: $\\varphi_{1}^{*}\\omega = \\omega_{0}$**. Differentiate $t$ using Cartan's formula $\\mathcal{L}_{X} = d\\iota_{X} + \\iota_{X}d$ and $\\frac{d}{dt}\\omega_{t} = \\eta$:\n\n$$\\frac{d}{dt}\\bigl(\\varphi_{t}^{*}\\omega_{t}\\bigr) = \\varphi_{t}^{*}\\bigl(\\mathcal{L}_{X_{t}}\\omega_{t} + \\tfrac{d}{dt}\\omega_{t}\\bigr) = \\varphi_{t}^{*}\\bigl(d\\iota_{X_{t}}\\omega_{t} + \\iota_{X_{t}}d\\omega_{t} + \\eta\\bigr).$$\n\n By $d\\omega_{t} = 0$, $\\iota_{X_{t}}\\omega_{t} = -\\theta$, and $\\eta = d\\theta$, the parenthesized expression equals $-d\\theta + 0 + d\\theta = 0$. Thus $\\varphi_{t}^{*}\\omega_{t}$ is independent of $t$ and equals its value at $t = 0$, namely $\\varphi_{0}^{*}\\omega_{0} = \\omega_{0}$. Taking $t = 1$ gives $\\varphi_{1}^{*}\\omega = \\omega_{0}$. Hence $(q_{i} \\circ \\varphi_{1}^{-1},\\, p_{i} \\circ \\varphi_{1}^{-1})$ are Darboux coordinates on $\\varphi_{1}(U)$. ∎\n\n📌 **What the proof uses**: Nondegeneracy (to solve $X_{t}$), closedness ($\\omega_{t}$ is closed and $\\eta$ has primitive $\\theta$), contractibility of the chart (`lem.poincare-lemma`), and the local flow theorem for ODEs (`thm.flow-local`). The agreement of $\\omega_{0}$ and $\\omega$ at $p$ is crucial. Otherwise $\\omega_{t}$ might degenerate at $p$, preventing the Moser flow from being defined. The node `thm.symplectic-normal-form` currently provides only a linear algebra proof sketch. The existence of the required symplectic basis is proved again here, so this proof is independent of that node.\n\n⭐ **This theorem marks a dividing line in symplectic geometry**: Local coordinates cannot distinguish symplectic manifolds, so all symplectic invariants (Gromov–Witten invariants, Floer homology, and capacities) are **global**. Riemannian geometry starts from local data such as curvature, while symplectic geometry relies on global data.\n\n⚠️ The theorem **does not** say that every symplectic manifold is diffeomorphic to $\\mathbb{R}^{2n}$. It supplies only local coordinates and imposes no constraint on global topology (as illustrated by the area form on $T^{2}$). This is the most common misreading.\n\n⚠️ Darboux's name appears in three places here: this theorem in symplectic geometry, the Darboux criterion and upper and lower sums (`thm.darboux-criterion`, Riemann integration), and Darboux's theorem in real analysis (derivatives have the intermediate value property). The three are unrelated.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#8f384555'
    },

  'def.symplectic-manifold': {
    statement: {
      en: "**What this does**: Extends the notion of a symplectic vector space to a manifold. Each tangent space carries a symplectic form, and these forms together must be **closed** ($d\\omega = 0$). This differential condition is the source of rigidity in symplectic geometry.\n\nLet $M$ be a smooth manifold (**there is no need to assume even dimension in advance**, as shown below). A **symplectic form** on $M$ is a 2-form $\\omega \\in \\Omega^{2}(M)$ satisfying\n\n1. **Closedness**: $d\\omega = 0$.\n2. **Nondegeneracy**: At each point $p$, $\\omega_{p} : T_{p}M \\times T_{p}M \\to \\mathbb{R}$ is nondegenerate (the manifold version of `def.symplectic-form`).\n\nWe call $(M, \\omega)$ a **symplectic manifold**. Nondegeneracy implies $\\dim M = 2n$ (apply `thm.symplectic-normal-form` pointwise).\n\n**Standard example**: On $\\mathbb{R}^{2n} = T^{*}\\mathbb{R}^{n}$ (the cotangent bundle), take\n\n$$\\omega_{0} = \\sum_{i=1}^{n} dq_{i} \\wedge dp_{i} ,$$\n\nThis is the phase space of classical mechanics.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#5be1ed9e'
    },
    notes: {
      en: "⚠️ **Closedness is essential**: Nondegenerate 2-forms exist pointwise (the cotangent bundle provides an example), whereas closedness is a condition on the form **as a whole**. Dropping it gives an almost symplectic manifold, which carries a nondegenerate 2-form with no closedness requirement (a different theory). ⚠ Integrability is a property of an almost complex structure. It requires an additional almost complex structure and plays no role in the definition of an almost symplectic manifold. $S^{6}$ is a standard example of an almost symplectic manifold that is not symplectic.\n\n⭐ **Why physics needs it**: $\\omega$ gives rise to Hamiltonian flows (`def.hamiltonian-vector-field`), and $d\\omega = 0$ ensures that these flows **preserve $\\omega$** (the Lie derivative $\\mathcal{L}_{X_{H}}\\omega = 0$). This is the basis of Liouville's theorem on conservation of phase volume.\n\n⚠️ A symplectic manifold **is necessarily orientable** ($\\omega^{n}$ is a nowhere-vanishing volume form), but **need not** be Kähler. A Kähler structure also requires a complex structure and a compatible metric. Symplectic geometry is more general than Kähler geometry.\n\n⚠️ Compact symplectic manifolds are subject to a strong restriction: $[\\omega] \\in H^{2}(M)$ is nonzero (otherwise $\\omega=d\\theta$ would make $\\omega^{n}=d(\\theta\\wedge\\omega^{n-1})$ exact, giving integral zero and contradicting the fact that $\\omega^{n}$ is a nowhere-vanishing volume form). This is why a symplectic manifold cannot be $S^{2n}$, $n \\ge 2$. The cohomological argument is omitted here.",

      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#ff25192a'

  'def.hamiltonian-vector-field': {
    statement: {
      en: "**What this does**: Provides the correspondence ‘function $\\implies$ vector field’ on a symplectic manifold. It turns an energy function (the Hamiltonian) into equations of motion, the central construction of analytical mechanics.\n\nLet $(M, \\omega)$ be a symplectic manifold and let $H \\in C^{\\infty}(M)$. By nondegeneracy of $\\omega$, there is a unique vector field $X_{H}$ such that\n\n$$\\iota_{X_{H}}\\omega = +dH ,$$\n\nWe call $X_{H}$ the **Hamiltonian vector field** of $H$ ($\\iota$ is the interior product, see `def.interior-product`). Its integral curves satisfy **Hamilton's equations**, which in Darboux coordinates are\n\n$$\\dot{q}_{i} = \\frac{\\partial H}{\\partial p_{i}}, \\qquad \\dot{p}_{i} = -\\frac{\\partial H}{\\partial q_{i}} .$$\n\n**Key property**: $\\mathcal{L}_{X_{H}}\\omega = 0$ (the flow of $H$ preserves $\\omega$), so it also preserves the volume form $\\omega^{n}/n!$. This is **Liouville's theorem**.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#d10da433'

    notes: {
      en: "⚠️ **Sign conventions vary**: $\\iota_{X_{H}}\\omega = dH$, $= -dH$, and $\\omega(X_{H}, \\cdot) = dH$ are all used.       en: "**What this does**: Translates the vector field construction back into functions by assigning a function to each pair of functions.     notes: {

  'def.lagrangian-submanifold': {
    statement: {
      en: "**What this does**: Applies `def.lagrangian-subspace` pointwise on a manifold. It describes a maximal submanifold on which the symplectic form vanishes, and gives a mathematical model for configuration space in physics.\n\nLet $(M^{2n}, \\omega)$ be a symplectic manifold. An immersed submanifold $L \\subseteq M$ is called a **Lagrangian submanifold** if\n\n1. $\\dim L = n$ (half the dimension of $\\dim M$).\n2. $\\omega|_{L} = 0$ (on $L$ the form $\\omega$ vanishes identically, so $L$ is isotropic).\n\nEquivalently, for every $p \\in L$, $T_{p}L$ is a Lagrangian subspace of $T_{p}M$.\n\n**Standard examples**:\n\n- In $\\mathbb{R}^{2n}$, the zero section $\\{p = 0\\}$ (the position space, with all momenta zero).\n- More generally, **the graph associated with any function**, $\\{(q, p) : p = dF(q)\\}$, is a Lagrangian submanifold because $\\omega|_{L} = dq \\wedge d(dF) = 0$.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#c6aa3d4a'
    },
    notes: {
      en: "⭐ **‘$\\omega|_L = 0$’ is equivalent to ‘$p\\,dq$ is closed on $L$’**: Take $\\omega = d(p\\,dq)$. On $L$, $p\\,dq$ is closed $\\iff$ it is locally of the form $dF$ $\\iff$ $L$ is locally the graph associated with a function.\n\nThus a Lagrangian submanifold is locally such a graph, and $F$ is called a **generating function**.\n\nThis connects geometry with analysis and underlies Hamilton–Jacobi theory.\n\n⚠️ **$\\dim L = n$ does not follow from isotropy**: An isotropic submanifold can have dimension far below $n$ (even a line in a symplectic vector space is isotropic). One must **also** require half dimension or, equivalently, maximality ($T_pL = (T_pL)^{\\perp}$). This is exactly as in the linear case (see the notes to `def.lagrangian-subspace`).\n\n⚠️ **Lagrangian submanifolds need not be closed or embedded**: Physics often uses immersed submanifolds and even multiple covers (such as periodic orbits in Bohr–Sommerfeld quantization). The definition here allows immersions.\n\n⚠️ Terminology: Lagrangian subspaces and Lagrangian submanifolds are named for the same person and express the same concept at two scales. They are **unrelated** to `thm.lagrange-identity` in inner product geometry.\n\n\n\n**Of the two defining conditions, ‘$\\dim = n$’ does not follow from isotropy** (this is the step most often omitted):\n\n- $\\omega|_{L} \\equiv 0$ gives **only isotropy**. An isotropic submanifold can have dimension **far below** $n$ (even a line in a symplectic vector space is isotropic), so isotropy alone does not give maximality.\n- $\\dim L = n$ (**must be imposed separately**): Equivalently, require $T_{p}L = (T_{p}L)^{\\perp}$ (pointwise maximality). For a nondegenerate form, $\\dim W + \\dim W^{\\perp} = \\dim V$, so ‘$W = W^{\\perp}$’ **forces** the dimension to be half. This is what it means to be Lagrangian.\n- **Directions of the equivalence**: Pointwise, $T_{p}L$ is a Lagrangian subspace $\\iff$ $L$ is a Lagrangian submanifold ($L$ is immersed, and $\\omega|_L = 0$ and $\\dim = n$ hold). The direction $(\\Leftarrow)$ is immediate pointwise. The direction $(\\implies)$ requires that $L$ have constant dimension, which the immersion guarantees.\n\n⚠️ **Closedness is not part of the definition**: $L$ need not be closed, compact, or embedded. It can be immersed or even multiply covered. The Bohr–Sommerfeld condition in physics uses Lagrangian submanifolds that may only be immersed.\n⚠️ **The equivalence with being locally the graph associated with a function holds only when the projection $\\pi:L\\to Q$ (with $Q$ the zero section containing a Lagrangian subspace) is a local diffeomorphism** (exactness of $p\\,\\mathrm{d}q$). This is a local property, so generating functions exist only locally.\n\n\n\n\n**Hypotheses used in each direction** (the pointwise definition requires no extra hypotheses, while a generating function requires transversality):\n- $\\dim L=n$ and $\\omega|_L=0$ $\\iff$ each tangent space is a Lagrangian subspace. This is the pointwise definition applied to the tangent map of the immersion. It does not require $L$ to be closed, compact, or embedded.\n- Isotropic and half-dimensional $\\iff$ $T_pL=(T_pL)^\\omega$ uses nondegeneracy of the symplectic form and $\\dim W+\\dim W^\\omega=2n$. Isotropy alone is insufficient, as a one-dimensional submanifold when $n>1$ shows.\n- In cotangent coordinates, the graph of a closed 1-form $\\iff$ a Lagrangian graph, by computing the pullback of the canonical symplectic form. A closed 1-form is locally of the form $dF$ by the Poincaré lemma. Global existence also requires exactness.\n- To express a general Lagrangian submanifold as a graph in fixed position coordinates, the projection must also be a local diffeomorphism. A cotangent fiber is itself Lagrangian but cannot be written as $p=dF(q)$ under that position projection.",

      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#4c7cee78'

  'def.lagrange-bracket': {
    statement: {
      en: "**What this does**: Gives the earlier counterpart of the Poisson bracket (Lagrange 1808, Poisson 1809), essentially the inverse of the Poisson bracket matrix. It measures the area between two families of curves, making it natural in the theory of canonical transformations.\n\nLet $(q, p)$ be Darboux coordinates, and let $u = (u_{1}, \\ldots, u_{2n})$ and $v = (v_{1}, \\ldots, v_{2n})$ be two sets of coordinate functions. The **Lagrange bracket** is defined by\n\n$$[u, v]_{q,p} := \\sum_{i=1}^{n}\\Bigl(\\frac{\\partial q_{i}}{\\partial u}\\frac{\\partial p_{i}}{\\partial v} - \\frac{\\partial p_{i}}{\\partial u}\\frac{\\partial q_{i}}{\\partial v}\\Bigr) .$$\n\n**Relation to the Poisson bracket**: Write the Poisson bracket matrix $\\bigl(\\{u_{i}, u_{j}\\}\\bigr)$ as $P$ and the Lagrange bracket matrix as $L$. Then\n\n$$L \\cdot P = -I ,$$\n\nThat is, $L = -P^{-1}$. The matrices are inverses **up to a sign** (when $n = 1$, we have $L = P = \\begin{pmatrix}0 & 1\\cr -1 & 0\\end{pmatrix}$ and $LP = -I$, and the general formula is $\\sum_{k} [u_{i},u_{k}]\\{u_{k},u_{j}\\} = -\\delta_{ij}$).\n\n**Criterion for a canonical transformation**: The transformation $(q, p) \\mapsto (Q, P)$ is a **canonical transformation** (symplectomorphism) $\\iff$ for every $i, j$, both $[Q_{i}, Q_{j}] = [P_{i}, P_{j}] = 0$ and $[Q_{i}, P_{j}] = \\delta_{ij}$ hold.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#965424f9'
    },
    notes: {
      en: "**Why the Lagrange bracket came first**: The calculus of variations and the study of canonical transformations, as in Lagrange's *Analytical Mechanics*, preceded the concept of a symplectic manifold.\n\nPoisson later found that the dual bracket satisfies the Jacobi identity, and it became central to the modern formulation.\n\n**The two brackets encode the same information** (by inversion), but the Lagrange bracket does not satisfy the Jacobi identity and is now used as a computational tool.\n\n⭐ **Geometric meaning**: The Lagrange bracket measures the area enclosed in phase space by the families of level curves of $u$ and $v$. The Lagrange bracket thus describes areas, while the Poisson bracket describes flows. This is why the canonical transformation condition is more intuitive in terms of Lagrange brackets: it directly expresses area preservation.\n\n⚠️ **$L = -P^{-1}$ holds only when the comparison is made in the same coordinate system**: Both matrices must be written using the **same** $(u, v)$ as a basis to make the comparison meaningful. This is the easiest point to misremember.\n\n⚠️ Notation: Some books write the Lagrange bracket as $\\{u,v\\}$ (the same notation as for the Poisson bracket), while others write $[u,v]_{q,p}$. We use the latter, with a subscript indicating the reference coordinates.",

  'thm.qr-decomposition': {
    statement: {
      en: "**What this does**: Expresses Gram–Schmidt orthogonalization as a product of matrices. Denote the result by $R$ and the orthogonalized basis by $Q$ to obtain $A = QR$.\n\nSuppose the columns of $A \\in M_{m\\times n}(\\mathbb{R})$ are linearly independent ($m \\ge n$). There is then a **unique** factorization\n\n$$A = QR ,$$\n\nwhere $Q \\in M_{m\\times n}$ has **orthonormal** columns ($Q^{T}Q = I_{n}$) and $R \\in M_{n}(\\mathbb{R})$ is **upper triangular** with positive diagonal entries.\n\n**Complex case**: $A = QR$, $Q^{*}Q = I$, and $R$ is upper triangular (with positive real diagonal entries).\n\n**Square case**: When $A$ is invertible, $Q$ is orthogonal (unitary). This gives the factorization of $A$ into ‘orthogonal $\\times$ upper triangular’ form, corresponding to the ‘lower triangular $\\times$ upper triangular’ factorization in LU.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#c230d26a'
    },
    notes: {
      en: "**This is Gram–Schmidt**: Orthogonalize the columns of $A$, denoted by $a_{1}, \\ldots, a_{n}$, to obtain $q_{1}, \\ldots, q_{n}$ (`thm.gram-schmidt`). Then $a_{k} = \\sum_{i \\le k} r_{ik}q_{i}$, and the coefficients assemble into the upper triangular matrix $R$.\n\nThe only difference is that QR usually requires a positive diagonal for $R$ (fixing the signs of $q_k$).\n\n⭐ **Why use QR instead of Gram–Schmidt**: They are mathematically equivalent but differ numerically. Gram–Schmidt can lose orthogonality ($Q^{T}Q$ departs from $I$), so practical algorithms use Householder reflections or Givens rotations to compute $Q$ directly. **Only the mathematical results are covered here** (numerical algorithms are outside the scope).\n\n**Relation to least squares**: $\\lVert Ax - b\\rVert^{2} = \\lVert Rx - Q^{T}b\\rVert^{2} + \\lVert (I - QQ^{T})b\\rVert^{2}$, and the last term is independent of $x$. Thus the least-squares solution is obtained by solving $Rx = Q^{T}b$ (`thm.least-squares`).\n\nThis is why QR is widely used in numerical computation.\n\n⚠️ The **linear independence of the columns** is essential. Without it, $Q$ has an incomplete set of columns and $R$ is singular. A factorization is still possible but loses its usefulness in this form (use column-pivoted QR or a pseudoinverse in that case).",

  'def.pseudoinverse': {
    statement: {
      en: "**What this does**: Provides a substitute for an inverse for singular and rectangular matrices. The pseudoinverse $A^{+}$ is the unique matrix satisfying the four Penrose conditions.\n\nLet $A \\in M_{m\\times n}(\\mathbb{K})$, where $\\mathbb{K} = \\mathbb{R}$ or $\\mathbb{C}$ ($^{*}$ is the transpose or conjugate transpose). (⚠ Over a general field, the conditions **may have no solution**: $K = \\mathbb{F}_{2}$ and $A = (1\\, 1)$ reduce them to $1 = 0$.)\n\n$$A A^{+} A = A, \\qquad A^{+} A A^{+} = A^{+} ,$$\n\n$$(AA^{+})^{*} = AA^{+}, \\qquad (A^{+}A)^{*} = A^{+}A .$$\n\n**Construction via SVD**: If $A = U\\Sigma V^{*}$ (`thm.svd`), then\n\n$$A^{+} = V\\Sigma^{+}U^{*} ,$$\n\nwhere $\\Sigma^{+}$ is obtained by taking the reciprocals of the nonzero diagonal entries of $\\Sigma$ and then transposing.\n\n**When $A$ is invertible**, $A^{+} = A^{-1}$.       status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#cf3d1d5f'
    },
    notes: {
      en: "**Meaning of the four conditions**: The first two say that $AA^{+}$ and $A^{+}A$ are idempotent (projections, `def.idempotent`).   'def.matrix-norm': {
    statement: {
      en: "**What this does**: Measures the size of a matrix. Two distinct approaches are to measure the matrix as **a collection of entries** (the Frobenius norm) or as **an operator** (the operator norm).\n\n**Operator norm** (induced norm): On $\\mathbb{R}^{n}$, given a vector norm $\\lVert\\cdot\\rVert$, define\n\n$$\\lVert A\\rVert := \\sup_{x \\ne 0}\\frac{\\lVert Ax\\rVert}{\\lVert x\\rVert} = \\sup_{\\lVert x\\rVert = 1}\\lVert Ax\\rVert .$$\n\n**Frobenius norm**: $\\lVert A\\rVert_{F} := \\bigl(\\sum_{i,j}\\lvert a_{ij}\\rvert^{2}\\bigr)^{1/2}$ (the length of the matrix viewed as a $mn$-dimensional vector).\n\n**Basic properties**: Both are norms and satisfy $\\lVert AB\\rVert \\le \\lVert A\\rVert\\,\\lVert B\\rVert$ (submultiplicativity).       status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#dad69efd'

    notes: {
      en: "**Why both norms are useful**: The operator norm measures the maximum amplification factor and is directly related to spectral radius and convergence ($\\rho(A) \\le \\lVert A\\rVert$, `def.spectral-radius`).   'prop.paracompact-partition-unity': {
    statement: {
      en: "For a topological space $X$:\n\n1. $X$ is **paracompact Hausdorff** $\\implies$ every open cover admits a subordinate **partition of unity** (the converse requires a separation hypothesis. In the indiscrete topology on at least two points, the constant function $1$ gives a partition of unity, but the space is neither Hausdorff nor $T_1$).\n2. $X$ is **normal** $\\implies$ every **locally finite** open cover admits a subordinate partition of unity (the converse likewise requires $T_1$ or Hausdorffness).",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#23852434'
    },
    notes: {
      en: "**Proof (both directions).**\n**$(\\Longleftarrow)$ A partition of unity gives a locally finite refinement.** Let $\\{\\rho_{i}\\}$ be a partition of unity subordinate to the cover $\\{U_{i}\\}$.\n\nBy the definition of a partition of unity, $\\operatorname{supp}\\rho_{i} \\subseteq U_{i}$ and the family of supports is **locally finite** ✓.\n\nThus $\\{ \\rho_{i}^{-1}\\bigl((0,1]\\bigr) \\}$ is an open refinement of $\\{U_{i}\\}$ and is still locally finite (each point lies outside all but finitely many supports) ✓. This is exactly the condition for paracompactness, with Hausdorffness assumed: every open cover has a locally finite open refinement.\n\nThe normal case is analogous (use a locally finite $\\{\\operatorname{supp}\\rho_{i}\\}$ for a locally finite cover) ✓.\n**$(\\Longrightarrow)$ Use normality to construct $\\rho_{i}$ step by step.**\n- First take a **locally finite** open refinement $\\{V_{i}\\}$ (by the definition of paracompactness, or use the given locally finite cover itself in the normal case) ✓.\n- Normality gives open sets $W_{i}$ such that $\\overline{W_{i}} \\subseteq V_{i}$ and $\\{W_{i}\\}$ still covers $X$ (the standard shrinking lemma for normal spaces, obtained from Urysohn's lemma or a variant) ✓.\n- ⚠️ One shrinking is insufficient: vanishing of $g_i$ on $X\\setminus V_i$ gives only $\\operatorname{supp} g_i \\subseteq \\overline{V_i}$. **Shrink once more**: Take open sets $O_i$ such that $\\overline{W_i} \\subseteq O_i \\subseteq \\overline{O_i} \\subseteq V_i$ (apply normality again). Apply **Urysohn's lemma** to $(\\overline{W_i}, X \\setminus O_i)$ to obtain $g_i : X \\to [0,1]$, with $g_i|_{\\overline{W_i}} = 1$ and $g_i|_{X\\setminus O_i} = 0$. Then $\\operatorname{supp} g_i \\subseteq \\overline{O_i} \\subseteq V_i$ ✓.\n- Set $g := \\sum_{i} g_{i}$. Local finiteness makes this a **locally finite sum**, so $g$ is continuous ✓. Since $\\{W_{i}\\}$ covers $X$, we have $g \\ge 1$ at each point (at least one term is 1) ✓.\n- Take $\\rho_{i} := g_{i}/g$ ✓. The functions are nonnegative, sum to 1, satisfy $\\operatorname{supp}\\rho_{i} \\subseteq V_{i} \\subseteq U_{i}$, and still have locally finite supports ✓.\nThis gives a partition of unity subordinate to the original cover ✓. ∎\n\n⭐ This result **underlies extension theorems**: A partition of unity combines locally defined data by a weighted sum to produce a globally defined function. Tietze- and Dugundji-type extensions, integration on manifolds, and Stokes' theorem all use this construction.\n\n⚠️ **Local finiteness is essential**: Without local finiteness, $\\sum \\rho_{i} = 1$ is merely a pointwise assertion, and the pointwise sum need not be continuous.\n\n\n\n**Hypotheses used in each direction** (the construction uses normality, and the converses require separate separation hypotheses):\n- Paracompact Hausdorff $\\implies$ every open cover admits a subordinate partition of unity: Use normality of paracompact Hausdorff spaces, a locally finite refinement, the shrinking lemma, and Urysohn's lemma. A second shrinking ensures the supports lie in the original open sets.\n- Every open cover admits a subordinate partition of unity $\\implies$ a locally finite open refinement exists: Take the sets on which the functions are positive, using local finiteness of supports and the fact that the sum is one. This gives paracompactness. Hausdorffness must be assumed separately.\n- Normal $\\implies$ every locally finite open cover admits a subordinate partition of unity: Use locally finite shrinking, Urysohn's lemma, and normalization of a locally finite sum of functions. There is no requirement that every cover admit a locally finite refinement.\n- For the converse, use the cover $\\{X\\setminus A,X\\setminus B\\}$ to separate the disjoint closed sets $A,B$. This gives the separation property for closed sets alone. To obtain normality under the definition that includes $T_1$, one must also assume $T_1$.\n- The missing separation hypothesis has a counterexample: In the indiscrete topology on at least two points, every open cover contains the entire space. The constant function one gives a subordinate partition of unity, yet the space is neither Hausdorff nor $T_1$. Both converses require the additional hypotheses.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#ba9f34ac'
    },

  'thm.smooth-partition-unity': {
    statement: {
      en: "**What this does**: Glues locally defined objects into a global one. Construct an object on each open set and take a weighted sum, using the functions of a smooth partition of unity as weights.\n\nLet $M$ be a smooth manifold and let $\\mathcal{U} = \\{U_{\\alpha}\\}_{\\alpha \\in A}$ be an open cover of $M$. Then there is a family of smooth functions $\\{\\rho_{\\alpha}\\}_{\\alpha \\in A}$ such that\n\n1. $0 \\le \\rho_{\\alpha} \\le 1$ and $\\rho_{\\alpha}$ is smooth.\n2. $\\operatorname{supp}\\rho_{\\alpha} \\subseteq U_{\\alpha}$, and the family of supports $\\{\\operatorname{supp}\\rho_{\\alpha}\\}$ is **locally finite**.\n3. $\\sum_{\\alpha \\in A}\\rho_{\\alpha} \\equiv 1$ (the locally finite sum is well-defined pointwise and is smooth).\n\nWe call $\\{\\rho_{\\alpha}\\}$ a **smooth partition of unity subordinate to $\\mathcal{U}$**. In particular, every open cover of $M$ has a subordinate smooth partition of unity.\n\nThe three requirements are exactly those of `def.partition-of-unity`, with smoothness in place of continuity.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#496c6a99'
    },
    notes: {
      en: "**Proof**. The two ingredients are bump functions on $\\mathbb{R}^{n}$ and paracompactness of the manifold (existence of locally finite refinements).\n\n**Step 1: Construct bump functions**. Set\n\n$$b(t) = \\begin{cases} e^{-1/t}, & t > 0,\\\\ 0, & t \\le 0,\\end{cases}\\qquad \\beta(x) = \\frac{b(1 - \\lVert x\\rVert^{2})}{b(1 - \\lVert x\\rVert^{2}) + b(\\lVert x\\rVert^{2} - \\tfrac{1}{4})}.$$\n\n All derivatives of $b$ at $t = 0$ are $0$ ($e^{-1/t}$ tends to $0$ faster than any polynomial), so $b$ is smooth. The two denominator terms never vanish simultaneously, and their sum is positive (for $\\lVert x\\rVert \\in [\\tfrac{1}{2}, 1]$ exactly one term is positive), so $\\beta$ is smooth. Also, $\\beta \\equiv 1$ when $\\lVert x\\rVert \\le \\tfrac{1}{2}$ and $\\beta \\equiv 0$ when $\\lVert x\\rVert \\ge 1$. Translating and rescaling $\\beta$ produces a bump function on any ball.\n\n**Step 2: Take a locally finite refinement**. The manifold $M$ is second countable, Hausdorff, and locally Euclidean, hence paracompact (the hypothesis of `prop.paracompact-partition-unity`). The open cover $\\mathcal{U}$ therefore has a locally finite refinement $\\{V_{\\alpha}\\}$, and we can require each $\\overline{V_{\\alpha}}$ to be compact and contained in a coordinate chart $W_{\\alpha} \\subseteq U_{\\alpha}$. First refine by coordinate balls within $U_{\\alpha}$, then refine again and shrink the closures.\n\n**Step 3: Put a positive bump function on each piece**. Fix $\\alpha$. Since $\\overline{V_{\\alpha}}$ is compact and contained in the open set $W_{\\alpha}$, take a finite cover $B(x_{j}, s_{j})$ and choose $s_{j}>0$ so that $B(x_{j}, 2s_{j}) \\subseteq W_{\\alpha}$. Set $r_{j}=2s_{j}$. Then $B(x_{j}, r_{j}/2)$ still covers $\\overline{V_{\\alpha}}$ and $B(x_{j}, r_{j}) \\subseteq W_{\\alpha}$. On each ball take a bump function $\\beta_{j} \\ge 0$ (positive inside $B(x_{j}, r_{j}/2)$ and zero outside $B(x_{j}, r_{j})$), and set $\\psi_{\\alpha} = \\sum_{j} \\beta_{j}$. Then $\\psi_{\\alpha} \\in C^{\\infty}(M)$, $\\psi_{\\alpha} \\ge 0$, and $\\psi_{\\alpha} > 0$ on $V_{\\alpha}$ (every point lies in some $B(x_{j}, r_{j}/2)$), and $\\operatorname{supp}\\psi_{\\alpha} \\subseteq \\bigcup_{j} \\overline{B(x_{j}, r_{j})} \\subseteq W_{\\alpha} \\subseteq U_{\\alpha}$.\n\n**Step 4: Normalize**. Since $\\{V_{\\alpha}\\}$ covers $M$, every point lies in at least one $V_{\\alpha}$, so $\\Psi = \\sum_{\\alpha} \\psi_{\\alpha} > 0$ holds everywhere. The family of supports $\\{\\operatorname{supp}\\psi_{\\alpha}\\}$ is locally finite because it refines the locally finite family $\\{V_{\\alpha}\\}$. Thus $\\Psi$ is a locally finite sum and is smooth. Set $\\rho_{\\alpha} = \\psi_{\\alpha}/\\Psi$. Then $\\rho_{\\alpha}$ is smooth, $0 \\le \\rho_{\\alpha} \\le 1$ and $\\operatorname{supp}\\rho_{\\alpha} = \\operatorname{supp}\\psi_{\\alpha} \\subseteq U_{\\alpha}$ hold, and $\\sum_{\\alpha} \\rho_{\\alpha} = \\Psi/\\Psi = 1$. All three requirements are met. ∎\n\n**Why local finiteness is essential**: Without it, $\\sum\\rho_{\\alpha}$ may have infinitely many nonzero terms near a point, and the sum need not converge or be smooth. Local finiteness gives only finitely many terms near each point, so smoothness, integrability, and linearity carry over from finite sums.\n\n**What it is used to prove**: Existence of Riemannian metrics (`def.riemannian-metric`), existence of fiber metrics on vector bundles (`prop.bundle-metric`), integration of top-degree forms (`def.integral-form`), and characterization of orientability by a nowhere-vanishing $n$-form (`prop.volume-form-orientation`). Each proof constructs the objects locally and glues them with a partition of unity.\n\n**Second countability is essential**: The long line is locally Euclidean and Hausdorff but is neither separable nor paracompact, so partitions of unity cannot in general be constructed on it. The technical hypotheses in the manifold definition matter.\n\n**Continuous and smooth versions**: The continuous version only requires paracompactness (`prop.paracompact-partition-unity`). The smooth version uses bump functions to obtain smoothness. On manifolds, these are two regularity levels of the same construction.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#3f1f85f7'
