  'thm.chebyshev-estimate': {
    statement: {
      en: "There exist constants $0 < c_{1} < c_{2}$ such that for every $x \\ge 2$,\n\n$$c_{1}\\,\\frac{x}{\\log x} < \\pi(x) < c_{2}\\,\\frac{x}{\\log x} .$$\n\n($\\pi(x) \\asymp x/\\log x$. Examples of constants from the elementary proof are $0.28$ and $6$.)",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#1397c7e8'
    },
    notes: {
      en: "**Proof**: The proof uses only elementary binomial coefficient estimates. Write $\\theta(x) := \\sum_{p \\le x} \\log p$ (the sum runs only over primes $p \\le x$). First prove, for integers $x$, the estimate $0.3\\,x/\\log x < \\pi(x) < 6\\,x/\\log x$. For real arguments the lower bound is weakened to $0.28\\,x/\\log x$, while the upper bound stays the same.\n\n**Lower bound.** For an integer $n \\ge 1$, set $N := \\binom{2n}{n}$. Since $\\sum_{k=0}^{2n}\\binom{2n}{k} = 4^{n}$ and $\\binom{2n}{n}$ is the largest term, we obtain\n\n$$\\frac{4^{n}}{2n+1} \\le N \\le 4^{n}.$$\n\nOn the other hand, factor $N$ into primes. By Legendre's formula,\n\n$$v_{p}(N) = \\sum_{k \\ge 1}\\left(\\left\\lfloor\\frac{2n}{p^{k}}\\right\\rfloor - 2\\left\\lfloor\\frac{n}{p^{k}}\\right\\rfloor\\right),$$\n\nEach term is either $0$ or $1$, and given $p^{k} \\le 2n$, the number of admissible $k$ is at most $\\log(2n)/\\log p$. Hence $v_{p}(N) \\le \\log(2n)/\\log p$, that is, $p^{v_{p}(N)} \\le 2n$. Thus\n\n$$N = \\prod_{p \\le 2n} p^{v_{p}(N)} \\le (2n)^{\\pi(2n)}.$$\n\nCombine this with $N \\ge 4^{n}/(2n+1)$ and take logarithms to get\n\n$$\\pi(2n) \\ge \\frac{n\\log 4 - \\log(2n+1)}{\\log(2n)} = \\frac{\\log 4}{2}\\cdot\\frac{2n}{\\log(2n)} - \\frac{\\log(2n+1)}{\\log(2n)}.$$\n\nFor $x := 2n \\ge 16$, we have $\\log(2n+1)/\\log(2n) \\le 2$ and $2\\log x/x \\le 2\\log 16/16 < 0.7$, so $\\pi(x) \\ge 1.386\\,x/\\log x - 2 \\ge (1.386 - 0.693)\\,x/\\log x > 0.3\\,x/\\log x$. For odd $x = 2n+1 \\ge 17$, we have $\\pi(x) \\ge \\pi(2n) \\ge 0.3\\cdot 2n/\\log(2n)$. Also, $x/\\log x \\le \\frac{x}{2n}\\cdot\\frac{2n}{\\log(2n)}$ and $\\frac{x}{2n} = 1+\\frac{1}{2n} < 1.07$, giving $\\pi(x) > \\frac{0.3}{1.07}\\,x/\\log x > 0.28\\,x/\\log x$.\n\n**Upper bound.** First estimate $\\theta$. For even $n = 2r$, the product of the primes in $(r, 2r]$ divides $\\binom{2r}{r} \\le 4^{r}$, so\n\n$$\\theta(2r) - \\theta(r) \\le 2r\\log 2.$$\n\nFor odd $n = 2r+1$, the product of the primes in $(r+1, 2r+1]$ divides $\\binom{2r+1}{r}$. Since binomial coefficients of odd degree occur in equal pairs, $2\\binom{2r+1}{r} \\le 2^{2r+1}$, so $\\binom{2r+1}{r} \\le 2^{2r}$, that is,\n\n$$\\theta(2r+1) - \\theta(r+1) \\le 2r\\log 2.$$\n\nTogether these give $\\theta(n) \\le n\\log 4$ (induct on $n$, checking the base case $n = 1, 2$ directly). Now count the primes: $\\pi(\\sqrt{x}) \\le \\sqrt{x}$. Each $\\sqrt{x} < p \\le x$ satisfies $\\log p \\ge \\tfrac12\\log x$, and the number of such $p$ is at most $(\\theta(x) - \\theta(\\sqrt{x}))/\\tfrac12\\log x$. Thus for integers $x \\ge 2$,\n\n$$\\pi(x) \\le \\sqrt{x} + \\frac{2\\theta(x)}{\\log x} \\le \\sqrt{x} + \\frac{4x\\log 2}{\\log x}.$$\n\nFor $x \\ge 16$, we have $\\log x \\le \\sqrt{x}$, so $\\sqrt{x} \\le x/\\log x$ and hence $\\pi(x) \\le (1 + 4\\log 2)\\,x/\\log x < 6\\,x/\\log x$. For $2 \\le x < 16$, we have $6x/\\log x > 6$ and $\\pi(x) \\le 6$, so the conclusion holds immediately.\n\n**From integers to reals.** Let $x \\ge 2$ be real and set $m := \\lfloor x \\rfloor$. Then $\\pi(x) = \\pi(m)$. When $m \\ge 16$, the function $t/\\log t$ is increasing on $[16, \\infty)$, so $m/\\log m \\le x/\\log x$ and the upper bound carries over directly. This only gives $\\pi(x) \\ge 0.3\\,m/\\log m$ for the lower bound, so use $x/\\log x \\le (1+1/m)\\,m/\\log m$ instead (by $x < m+1$). When $m \\ge 16$, the factor satisfies $1+1/m < 1.07$, so $\\pi(x) > \\frac{0.3}{1.07}\\,x/\\log x > 0.28\\,x/\\log x$. When $m < 16$, check the integers individually ($\\pi$ is piecewise constant and $t/\\log t$ increases on $[3, \\infty)$). ∎\n\n**Why these bounds suffice**: The constants are only used for the qualitative conclusion that $\\pi(x)$ and $x/\\log x$ have the same order of growth. Improving the constants to arbitrary precision is the work of Chebyshev's original paper, while sharpening the upper bound to $\\pi(x) \\sim x/\\log x$ requires complex analysis (the prime number theorem, see `thm.prime-number-theorem`).",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#772b257b'
    },

  'thm.radical-solvable': {
    statement: {
      en: "Let $F$ be a field of **characteristic zero**, let $f \\in F[x]$ be nonzero, and let $K$ be the splitting field of $f$. Then\n\nThe roots of $f$ can be expressed by **radicals** (arithmetic operations and extraction of $n$th roots) from elements of $F$ $\\iff$ $\\operatorname{Gal}(K/F)$ is a **solvable group**.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#66154e45'

    notes: {
      en: "**Proof**. First make precise what it means to be solvable by radicals: There is a tower of fields $F=F_{0}\\subseteq F_{1}\\subseteq\\cdots\\subseteq F_{m}$ with $F_{i}=F_{i-1}(\\alpha_{i})$ and $\\alpha_{i}^{n_{i}}\\in F_{i-1}$ ($1\\le i\\le m$), and $K\\subseteq F_{m}$. In characteristic zero, $K/F$ is Galois (a separable splitting field). We prove the two directions separately.\n\n**Lemma (one Kummer step)**: Suppose $F$ contains a primitive $m$th root of unity $\\zeta$ and $E/F$ is a cyclic extension of degree $m$. Then $E=F(\\beta)$ and $\\beta^{m}\\in F$. To prove this, choose a generator $\\sigma$ and regard $\\sigma$ on $E$ as a $F$-linear transformation. We have $\\sigma^{m}=1$, and $x^{m}-1$ splits over $F$ with no repeated roots, so $\\sigma$ is diagonalizable and all its eigenvalues are $m$th roots of unity. **The key is that the minimal polynomial of $\\sigma$ is exactly $x^{m}-1$**. By Dedekind's linear independence theorem (Lemma 1 in the Galois correspondence proof: pairwise distinct automorphisms are linearly independent as functions), $1,\\sigma,\\ldots,\\sigma^{m-1}$ is linearly independent over $E$. Thus no nonzero polynomial of degree $<m$ annihilates $\\sigma$, and the minimal polynomial has degree $\\ge m$. Also, $\\sigma^{m}=1$ implies that it divides $x^{m}-1$ (of degree $m$), so the minimal polynomial is $=x^{m}-1$. The minimal polynomial also divides the characteristic polynomial $\\chi_{\\sigma}$ (by Cayley–Hamilton, $\\chi_{\\sigma}$ annihilates $\\sigma$ and hence is divisible by the minimal polynomial). Since $\\deg\\chi_{\\sigma}=\\dim_{F}E=m=\\deg\\mu_{\\sigma}$ and both polynomials are monic of the same degree, $\\chi_{\\sigma}=\\mu_{\\sigma}=x^{m}-1$. Thus the $m$ eigenvalues are **all** the $m$th roots of unity, including the primitive root $\\zeta^{k}$. Take a corresponding eigenvector $\\beta$ with $\\sigma(\\beta)=\\zeta^{k}\\beta$. Then $\\sigma(\\beta^{m})=\\beta^{m}$, so $\\beta^{m}$ lies in the fixed field of $\\sigma$. The fixed field of the cyclic extension is precisely $F$, so $\\beta^{m}\\in F$. Also, $\\beta\\notin F$ (otherwise $\\sigma(\\beta)=\\beta$). Since $\\zeta^{k}$ is primitive, $\\sigma^{d}(\\beta)=\\beta$ if and only if $m\\mid d$. Thus the subgroup fixing $F(\\beta)$ contains only the identity, so $[F(\\beta):F]=m$ and hence $E=F(\\beta)$. ∎\n\n**$\\Leftarrow$ (solvable group $\\implies$ solvable by radicals)**: Suppose $G=\\operatorname{Gal}(K/F)$ is solvable. First assume that $F$ contains a primitive $n$th root of unity, with $n=|G|$. Induct on $|G|$. When $|G|=1$, we have $K=F$ and there is nothing to prove. Suppose $|G|>1$. The solvable group has a maximal proper normal subgroup $H$, so $G/H$ is simple and abelian, hence cyclic of prime order $p$. Set $E=K^{H}$. The Galois correspondence gives $[E:F]=p$, with $\\operatorname{Gal}(E/F)=G/H$ cyclic. Since $F$ contains $\\zeta_{p}$, the Kummer step gives $E=F(\\beta)$ and $\\beta^{p}\\in F$. Also, $K/E$ is Galois with group $H$, and $H$ is solvable with $|H|<|G|$. Since $E$ contains a primitive $|H|$th root of unity ($|H|$ divides $n$), the induction hypothesis gives a radical tower from $E$ to $K$. Appending it to the step $F\\subseteq E$ gives a radical tower for $K$ over $F$.\n\n**Removing the roots-of-unity hypothesis**: In general, $F$ need not contain $\\zeta_{n}$. Choose $N$ to be a common multiple of $1,2,\\ldots,n$ (for example, $n!$), and set $F_{1}=F(\\zeta_{N})$ and $K_{1}=K(\\zeta_{N})$. The extension $K_{1}/F_{1}$ is Galois, and the restriction map $\\operatorname{Gal}(K_{1}/F_{1})\\to\\operatorname{Gal}(K/F)$ is injective, so $G_{1}=\\operatorname{Gal}(K_{1}/F_{1})$ is solvable and $|G_{1}|\\le n$. The field $F_{1}$ contains all roots of unity of orders $\\le n$, in particular $\\zeta_{|G_{1}|}$. The case already proved gives a radical tower for $K_{1}$ over $F_{1}$. Also, $F\\subseteq F_{1}=F(\\zeta_{N})$ is itself a one-step radical extension ($\\zeta_{N}^{N}=1\\in F$). Combining these towers places $K\\subseteq K_{1}$ inside a radical tower over $F$, so the roots of $f$ are solvable by radicals.\n\n**$\\implies$ (solvable by radicals $\\implies$ solvable group)**: Let $F=F_{0}\\subseteq\\cdots\\subseteq F_{m}$ be a radical tower with $K\\subseteq F_{m}$ and $\\alpha_{i}^{n_{i}}\\in F_{i-1}$. Choose $N$ to be a common multiple of all the $n_{i}$ and set $F^{\\prime}=F(\\zeta_{N})$ and $E_{i}=F_{i}(\\zeta_{N})$. Then $E_{i}=E_{i-1}(\\alpha_{i})$ and $\\alpha_{i}^{n_{i}}\\in E_{i-1}$, and this is still a radical tower, with base field $E_{0}=F^{\\prime}$ containing $\\zeta_{n_{i}}$. Let $M$ be the Galois closure of $E_{m}$ over $F^{\\prime}$ (the splitting field of the product of the minimal polynomials of the generators). Then $M/F^{\\prime}$ is Galois and $K^{\\prime}=K(\\zeta_{N})\\subseteq M$.\n\n**Taking closures at each level**: Let $M_{i}$ be the compositum of all conjugates of $E_{i}$ generated under $F^{\\prime}$. Then $M_{0}=F^{\\prime}$ and $M_{m}=M$, and each $M_{i}/F^{\\prime}$ is Galois. The key is that $M_{i}$ is obtained from $M_{i-1}$ by adjoining several conjugates of $\\alpha_{i}$, denoted by $\\beta$, with $\\beta^{n_{i}}\\in M_{i-1}$. Write $\\beta=\\sigma(\\alpha_{i})$. Then $\\beta^{n_{i}}=\\sigma(\\alpha_{i}^{n_{i}})\\in\\sigma(F_{i-1})\\subseteq M_{i-1}$. Thus $M_{i}/M_{i-1}$ is generated by several $n_{i}$th roots, and $\\zeta_{n_{i}}\\in F^{\\prime}\\subseteq M_{i-1}$, so it is a Kummer extension. Any $\\tau\\in\\operatorname{Gal}(M_{i}/M_{i-1})$ sends each generator $\\beta$ to $\\zeta_{n_{i}}^{k_{\\beta}}\\beta$, and $\\tau\\mapsto(k_{\\beta})_{\\beta}$ is an injective homomorphism into $(\\mathbb{Z}/n_{i})^{r}$. Hence $\\operatorname{Gal}(M_{i}/M_{i-1})$ is abelian.\n\n**Solvable series**: Since $M_{i}/M_{i-1}$ is Galois, $\\operatorname{Gal}(M/M_{i})\\trianglelefteq\\operatorname{Gal}(M/M_{i-1})$, and the quotient is isomorphic to $\\operatorname{Gal}(M_{i}/M_{i-1})$, which is abelian. Thus $\\operatorname{Gal}(M/F^{\\prime})\\ \\trianglerighteq\\ \\operatorname{Gal}(M/M_{1})\\ \\trianglerighteq\\ \\cdots\\ \\trianglerighteq\\ \\operatorname{Gal}(M/M_{m})=1$ is a normal series with abelian quotients, so $\\operatorname{Gal}(M/F^{\\prime})$ is solvable.\n\n**Returning to $K$**: The extension $K^{\\prime}/F$ is Galois (as a compositum of two normal extensions). The restriction map $\\operatorname{Gal}(M/F^{\\prime})\\to\\operatorname{Gal}(K^{\\prime}/F^{\\prime})$ is surjective, so $\\operatorname{Gal}(K^{\\prime}/F^{\\prime})$ is solvable. We also have the exact sequence $1\\to\\operatorname{Gal}(K^{\\prime}/F^{\\prime})\\to\\operatorname{Gal}(K^{\\prime}/F)\\to\\operatorname{Gal}(F^{\\prime}/F)\\to1$ (the cyclotomic extension is Galois and restriction is surjective). Both ends are solvable and $\\operatorname{Gal}(F^{\\prime}/F)$ is abelian, so $\\operatorname{Gal}(K^{\\prime}/F)$ is solvable. Finally, $\\operatorname{Gal}(K/F)$ is a quotient of $\\operatorname{Gal}(K^{\\prime}/F)$. The extension $K^{\\prime}/F$ is normal, and every automorphism of $K$ over $F$ extends to $K^{\\prime}$. A quotient of a solvable group is solvable, so $\\operatorname{Gal}(K/F)$ is solvable. ∎\n\n⭐ **Why characteristic zero is required**: Both directions use that $x^{m}-1$ has no repeated roots (so $\\sigma$ is diagonalizable), as well as separability. In characteristic $p$, pure radical extensions must be replaced by Artin–Schreier extensions $x^{p}-x-a$, and the conclusion must be reformulated.\n\n📌 **This theorem is the entry point to the quintic problem**: It turns the question of whether a radical formula exists into a purely group-theoretic question ($S_{n}$ is solvable $\\iff n\\le4$, see the solvability of symmetric groups). When applying it in reverse, distinguish the absence of a radical solution from the absence of any solution: $\\operatorname{Gal}(f)$ is not solvable $\\implies$ has no solution by radicals.",

  'lem.shrink': {
    statement: {
      en: "Let $X$ be a **normal** space and let $\\mathcal{U} = \\{ U_{i} \\}_{i \\in I}$ be a **locally finite open cover** of $X$. Then there is an open cover of $X$, denoted by $\\mathcal{V} = \\{ V_{i} \\}_{i \\in I}$ (with **the same index set**), such that\n\n$$V_{i} \\subseteq \\overline{V_{i}} \\subseteq U_{i}\\qquad (i \\in I),$$\n\nand $\\mathcal{V}$ is still locally finite. Such a family $\\mathcal{V}$ is called a **shrinking** of $\\mathcal{U}$.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#e90ee2c4'
    },
    notes: {
      en: "**Proof (well-order the index set and shrink one member at a time)**. Choose a well-order on $I$ (using the axiom of choice). Construct open sets $V_{i}$ such that $\\overline{V_{i}} \\subseteq U_{i}$ while preserving coverage. Maintain the invariant that for every $i$, the family\n$$\\mathcal{W}_{i} := \\{\\, V_{j} : j < i \\,\\} \\cup \\{\\, U_{k} : k \\ge i \\,\\}$$\nis a cover of $X$.\n**Successor step** ($i \\to i^{+}$): Set $A_{i} := X \\setminus \\bigcup \\mathcal{W}_{i^{+}}$.\n\nThe set $A_{i}$ is **closed**.\n\nMoreover, $x \\notin \\mathcal{W}_{i^{+}}$ implies $x \\notin V_{j}$ ($j < i$) and $x \\notin U_{k}$ ($k > i$), so the invariant forces $x$ to lie in $U_{i}$. Hence $A_{i} \\subseteq U_{i}$.\n\nApply normality to the disjoint closed sets $A_{i}$ and $X \\setminus U_{i}$ to obtain an open set $V_{i}$ such that $A_{i} \\subseteq V_{i}$ and $\\overline{V_{i}} \\subseteq U_{i}$. This is the standard reformulation of normality: between a closed set and an open set containing it, there is an open set whose closure still lies in the larger open set.\n\nThus $\\mathcal{W}_{i^{+}}$ still covers $X$.\n**Limit step** ($i$ is a limit ordinal): Let $x \\in X$.\n\nIf $x \\in U_{k}$ for some $k \\ge i$, the point is already covered.\n\nOtherwise, $x \\notin U_{k}$ for every $k \\ge i$, and the cover $\\mathcal{W}_{i}$ gives some $j < i$ such that $x \\in U_{j}$.\n\nBy **point finiteness** (locally finite $\\implies$ point finite), $J := \\{\\, j < i : x \\in U_{j} \\,\\}$ is finite and nonempty.\n\nTake $j := \\max J$. Then for every $k > j$ we have $x \\notin U_{k}$, so $x \\in A_{j} \\subseteq V_{j}$.\n**Completion**: By $V_{i} \\subseteq U_{i}$, an open neighborhood meeting only finitely many $U_{i}$ also meets only finitely many $V_{i}$. Hence $\\mathcal{V}$ is locally finite. To check coverage, given $x$, point finiteness gives an index $i$ greater than all indices of members containing $x$ among the $U_{j}$. Applying the covering property to $\\mathcal{W}_{i}$ then gives $x \\in V_{j}$ (for some $j < i$).\n\n∎\n\n**Why local finiteness is essential**: The limit ordinal step uses the fact that only finitely many members contain $x$. A general open cover need not have this property and may admit no shrinking. This explains why normality does not imply paracompactness.\n\n⭐ There are two uses: turning a locally finite open refinement from paracompactness into a **locally finite closed refinement** by taking closures, and turning a locally finite open refinement into a **partition of unity** by using Urysohn's lemma between $A_{i}$ and $X \\setminus U_{i}$ and then normalizing.\n\n⚠️ After shrinking, **the closures also lie in the corresponding original members**. This is stronger than refining again, which only preserves coverage and local finiteness and does not guarantee containment of closures in the corresponding members.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#6c2ec690'
    },

  'thm.paracompact-hausdorff-normal': {
    statement: {
      en: "Let $X$ be a **paracompact Hausdorff** space. Then $X$ is **normal**. The proof first establishes the stronger intermediate conclusion that $X$ is already **regular**.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#a2106696'
    },
    notes: {
      en: "**Step 1: Paracompact Hausdorff $\\implies$ regular.** Suppose $x \\in X$, with $F \\subseteq X$ closed and $x \\notin F$.\n\nFor each $y \\in F$, Hausdorffness gives disjoint open sets $O_{1} \\ni y$ and $O_{2} \\ni x$.\n\nTake $G_{y} := O_{1}$. From $O_{1} \\subseteq X \\setminus O_{2}$ we obtain $\\overline{G_{y}} \\subseteq X \\setminus O_{2}$, so $x \\notin \\overline{G_{y}}$.\n\nThus\n$$\\mathcal{A} := \\{\\, X \\setminus F \\,\\} \\cup \\{\\, G_{y} : y \\in F \\,\\}$$\nis an open cover.\n\nTake a locally finite open refinement $\\mathcal{C}$ and set $W := \\bigcup \\{\\, C \\in \\mathcal{C} : C \\cap F \\ne \\emptyset \\,\\}$.\n\nThen $F \\subseteq W$. A point of $F$, say $y$, lies in some $C$. The case $C \\subseteq X \\setminus F$ is impossible because it contains $y$. Hence $C \\subseteq G_{y}$, so $C$ is included in $W$.\n\nOn the other hand, each member included in $W$, say $C$, lies in some $G_{y}$, so $x \\notin \\overline{C}$.\n\nSince the closure of the union of a locally finite family equals the union of the closures, $\\overline{W} = \\bigcup \\{\\, \\overline{C} : C \\cap F \\ne \\emptyset \\,\\}$ does not contain $x$.\n\nThus $W \\ni F$ and $X \\setminus \\overline{W} \\ni x$ are disjoint open sets separating $F$ and $x$.\n**Step 2: Regular $+$ paracompact $\\implies$ normal.** Let $A, B$ be disjoint closed sets.\n\nFor every $x \\in A$, regularity ($x \\notin B$) gives an open set $O_{x} \\ni x$ such that $\\overline{O_{x}} \\cap B = \\emptyset$.\n\nThen $\\mathcal{A} := \\{\\, X \\setminus A \\,\\} \\cup \\{\\, O_{x} : x \\in A \\,\\}$ is an open cover. Take a locally finite open refinement $\\mathcal{D}$ and set $U := \\bigcup \\{\\, D \\in \\mathcal{D} : D \\cap A \\ne \\emptyset \\,\\}$.\n\nAs above, $A \\subseteq U$, and each such $D$ lies in some $O_{x}$, giving $\\overline{D} \\cap B = \\emptyset$. Therefore $\\overline{U} \\cap B = \\emptyset$.\n\nThus $U \\supseteq A$ and $X \\setminus \\overline{U} \\supseteq B$ are disjoint open sets separating $A, B$.\n\n∎\n\n⭐ This result places paracompactness in the hierarchy of separation axioms: **compact Hausdorff $\\implies$ paracompact Hausdorff $\\implies$ normal $\\implies$ regular $\\implies$ Hausdorff**.\n\nThis supplies the normality used with Urysohn's lemma in Pack 40. Compact Hausdorff spaces ($[0,1]$, $\\beta I$) are paracompact.\n\n⚠️ **Hausdorffness is essential**: The two-point Sierpiński space is paracompact (its open covers are finite and hence locally finite), but it fails to be $T_{1}$ and therefore is not normal.\n\n⚠️ **The converse also fails**: A normal space need not be paracompact ($[0, \\omega_{1})$). Thus paracompactness is a separate condition from normality.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#0a80e695'
    },

  'thm.brouwer-fixed-point': {
    statement: {
      en: "Suppose $f : \\overline{\\mathbb{D}} \\to \\overline{\\mathbb{D}}$ is continuous ($\\overline{\\mathbb{D}} = \\{\\, |z| \\le 1 \\,\\}$). Then there exists $z \\in \\overline{\\mathbb{D}}$ such that $f(z) = z$.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#099e41c0'
    },
    notes: {
      en: "**Proof (continuous case)**: Suppose for contradiction that $f$ has no fixed point, so $f(z) \\ne z$ for every $z \\in \\overline{\\mathbb{D}}$. For each $z$ define\n\n$$r(z) := z + t(z)\\,(z - f(z)),$$\n\nwhere\n\n$$a(z) := \\langle z,\\ z - f(z)\\rangle,\\qquad b(z) := |z - f(z)|^{2} > 0,$$\n\n$$t(z) := \\frac{-a(z) + \\sqrt{a(z)^{2} + b(z)\\,(1 - |z|^{2})}}{b(z)}.$$\n\n**Why $r$ lies on $S^{1}$**: Expand $|z + t(z - f(z))|^{2} = 1$ to obtain\n\n$$b(z)\\,t^{2} + 2a(z)\\,t + (|z|^{2} - 1) = 0,$$\n\nHere $t(z)$ is precisely the nonnegative root of this quadratic equation ($b > 0$. Since $1 - |z|^{2} \\ge 0$, the radicand satisfies $\\ge a^{2}$, so the square root satisfies $\\ge |a| \\ge -a$ and hence $t \\ge 0$). Therefore $|r(z)| = 1$.\n\n**$r$ is continuous**: $f$ is continuous, and $a, b$ is computed from inner products and norms and is therefore continuous as well. Since $b(z) \\ge |z - f(z)|^{2}$ is everywhere $> 0$, taking the square root and dividing are both well-defined.\n\n**$r$ is the identity on the boundary**: If $|z| = 1$, then $a(z) = \\langle z, z - f(z)\\rangle = 1 - \\langle z, f(z)\\rangle \\ge 0$ (because $|f(z)| \\le 1$). Substituting into $t(z)$ gives $t(z) = 0$, hence $r(z) = z$.\n\nThus $r : \\overline{\\mathbb{D}} \\to S^{1}$ is a continuous retraction ($r|_{S^{1}} = \\operatorname{id}$), contradicting `thm.no-retraction-disk`. Therefore $f$ has a fixed point. ∎\n\n**Why complex analysis alone does not suffice**: The construction of $r$ above uses only continuity of $f$. The topological input is $\\pi_{1}(S^{1}) \\cong \\mathbb{Z}$. The proof of `thm.no-retraction-disk` is an immediate application of functoriality: A retraction $r$ would force $r_{*} \\circ i_{*} = \\operatorname{id}_{\\mathbb{Z}}$ to factor through $\\pi_{1}(\\overline{\\mathbb{D}}) = 1$.\n\n**An alternative for the holomorphic case (Rouché)**: Suppose $f$ is holomorphic on $\\mathbb{D}$, continuous up to the boundary, and $|f| \\le 1$. Then on $|z| = 1$, $|f(z) - z| \\ge |z| - |f(z)| \\ge 0$, with equality only when $f(z) = z$ (already a fixed point). Otherwise $f - z$ has no boundary zeros. By `thm.rouche`, compare $f - z$ with $-z$ ($|-z| = 1 > |f|$). The latter has exactly one zero inside $\\mathbb{D}$, so $f - z$ also has exactly one zero. This argument covers only the holomorphic case.\n\n💡 **Equivalent formulation**: There is no continuous retraction of $\\overline{\\mathbb{D}}$ onto $S^{1}$. Brouwer's theorem is equivalent to saying that $S^{1}$ is not a retract of the disk (see the edge involving `thm.borsuk-ulam` and `thm.no-retraction-disk`).",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#ea8b6bb2'
    },

  'thm.simplicial-approximation': {
    statement: {
      en: "Let $K, L$ be simplicial complexes, with $K$ **finite**, and let $f : |K| \\to |L|$ be continuous. Write $K^{(m)}$ for the result of applying **barycentric subdivision** to $K$ a total of $m$ times ($|K^{(m)}| = |K|$, with simplex diameters shrinking by a fixed factor). Then there exist $m \\ge 1$ and a **simplicial map**\n\n$$g : K^{(m)} \\longrightarrow L$$\n\nsuch that $|g| \\simeq f$ ($|g|$ and $f$ are homotopic). Such a map $g$ is called a **simplicial approximation** to $f$.\n\n**Corollary**: Let $K$ be a finite simplicial complex. The inclusion $C_{*}(K) \\hookrightarrow S_{*}(|K|)$ induces an isomorphism\n\n$$H_{p}(K) \\;\\cong\\; H_{p}(|K|) \\qquad \\forall p.$$\n\nThus the singular homology of a polyhedron can be computed as the simplicial homology of a triangulation, which is a **finite matrix** calculation.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#7f025703'
    },
    notes: {
      en: "**The proof has two steps**: First prove that the star condition $\\Longrightarrow$ gives a simplicial map and a homotopy, then prove that sufficiently fine subdivision makes the star condition hold.\n\n**Lemma 1 (star condition)**: Suppose $g : \\operatorname{Vert}(K) \\to \\operatorname{Vert}(L)$ satisfies\n\n$$f\\big(\\operatorname{st}_{K}(v)\\big) \\;\\subseteq\\; \\operatorname{st}_{L}\\big(g(v)\\big) \\qquad \\forall v .$$\n\nThen (i) $g$ sends every simplex of $K$ to a simplex of $L$, hence is simplicial, and (ii) $|g| \\simeq f$.\n\nProof of (i): Suppose $v_{0}, \\ldots, v_{p}$ span, in $K$, a simplex $\\sigma$. Choose an interior point $x \\in \\sigma^{\\circ}$. For each $i$ we have $x \\in \\operatorname{st}_{K}(v_{i})$, so $f(x) \\in \\operatorname{st}_{L}(g(v_{i}))$. Hence\n\n$$f(x) \\;\\in\\; \\bigcap_{i=0}^{p} \\operatorname{st}_{L}\\big(g(v_{i})\\big).$$\n\nThe stars of a collection of vertices in $L$ have nonempty intersection if and only if the vertices span a simplex ($\\bigcap \\operatorname{st}(w_{i})$ is precisely the interior of the simplex with vertex set $\\{w_{i}\\}$, and if it is nonempty, that simplex exists). Thus $\\{g(v_{0}), \\ldots, g(v_{p})\\}$ span a simplex of $L$, and $g$ is simplicial.\n\n(ii): Suppose $x \\in \\sigma^{\\circ}$. Set $\\tau = g(\\sigma)$, the simplex with vertices $g(v_{0}), \\ldots, g(v_{p})$. By the argument in (i), $f(x) \\in \\operatorname{st}_{L}(\\tau)$. That is, $f(x)$ lies in the interior of a simplex **containing $\\tau$**, denoted by $\\rho$. Also, $g(x) \\in \\tau \\subseteq \\rho$. The simplex $\\rho$ is convex, so the line segment satisfies $[f(x), g(x)] \\subseteq \\rho \\subseteq |L|$. Define $H(x, t) = (1 - t)\\,g(x) + t\\,f(x)$. It is continuous on each $\\sigma^{\\circ}$, and the formulas agree on boundaries of adjacent simplices (for a given point $x$, the choice of $\\rho$ gives the same convex hull regardless of the simplex used). Thus $H : |K| \\times I \\to |L|$ is continuous and is a homotopy from $g$ to $f$. ∎\n\n**Lemma 2 (sufficiently fine subdivision)**: $\\{\\operatorname{st}_{L}(w)\\}_{w \\in \\operatorname{Vert}(L)}$ is an open cover of $|L|$. By continuity of $f$, $\\{f^{-1}(\\operatorname{st}_{L}(w))\\}$ is an open cover of $|K|$. Since $|K|$ is compact, choose a Lebesgue number $\\delta > 0$ (`def.lebesgue-number`). Every subset of $|K|$ with diameter $< \\delta$ lies in some $f^{-1}(\\operatorname{st}_{L}(w))$, so its image lies in some $\\operatorname{st}_{L}(w)$.\n\nRepeated barycentric subdivision makes the diameters tend to zero. If $\\dim K = n$, then $\\operatorname{mesh}(K^{(m)}) \\le \\big(\\tfrac{n}{n+1}\\big)^{m}\\,\\operatorname{mesh}(K)$ (every subdivided simplex lies in an original simplex and has diameter at most $n/(n+1)$ times the original diameter). Choose $m$ such that $\\operatorname{mesh}(K^{(m)}) < \\delta/2$.\n\nFor each vertex of $K^{(m)}$, say $v$, the set $\\operatorname{st}(v)$ has diameter $\\le 2\\,\\operatorname{mesh}(K^{(m)}) < \\delta$, so $f(\\operatorname{st}(v)) \\subseteq \\operatorname{st}_{L}(w)$ for some $w$. Set $g(v) := w$. By Lemma 1, $g$ is simplicial and $|g| \\simeq f$. ∎\n\n**Proof of the corollary (at the chain level)**: Write $\\iota_{m} : C_{*}(K^{(m)}) \\hookrightarrow S_{*}(|K|)$ and $\\iota : C_{*}(K) \\hookrightarrow S_{*}(|K|)$ for the inclusions. Apply the theorem to the identity map $\\operatorname{id} : |K| \\to |K|$ to obtain $m$ and a simplicial map $g : K^{(m)} \\to K$ such that $|g| \\simeq \\operatorname{id}$.\n\n- $\\iota\\,g_{*} = (|g|)_{*}\\,\\iota_{m}$ ($g$ is simplicial, and both sides give the same image on each simplex).\n\n- By `thm.homotopy-invariance`, $|g| \\simeq \\operatorname{id}$ gives a chain homotopy $(|g|)_{*} \\simeq \\operatorname{id}$, so $\\iota\\,g_{*} \\simeq \\iota_{m}$.\n\n- The barycentric subdivision chain maps $\\operatorname{sd}^{m} : C_{*}(K) \\to C_{*}(K^{(m)})$ and $\\iota_{m}$ satisfy $\\iota_{m}\\,\\operatorname{sd}^{m} = \\iota$ (subdivision leaves the geometric realization unchanged and subdivides the chains), and $\\operatorname{sd}^{m}$ induces a homology isomorphism (for the reverse chain homotopy, each subdivided simplex lies in an original simplex, so the cone construction applies).\n\n⚠️ **A step is missing here (omitted from the graph)**: $\\iota\\,g\\,\\mathrm{sd}^{m} = \\iota$ gives only a candidate right inverse for $\\iota$. It does not establish invertibility unless $g\\,\\mathrm{sd}^{m}$ is already known to be invertible, which is precisely what must be proved (the subscripts denoting induced maps have been suppressed in the formulas). To complete the proof, explicitly construct a chain map $r : S_{*}(|K|) \\to C_{*}(K)$ taking sufficiently fine singular chains to simplicial chains, and chain homotopies on both sides, $r\\,\\iota = \\operatorname{id}$ and $\\iota\\,r \\simeq \\operatorname{id}$. **These two chain homotopies are the missing steps at the chain level**. The lemmas on the star condition and subdivision are complete.\n\n**⚠️ Technical detail**: The proof uses two standard constructions, the chain map induced by a simplicial map and the prism construction for chain homotopies (see `def.chain-homotopy` and `thm.homotopy-invariance`). If $C_{*}$ is taken to be an **ordered** chain group, $g$ may collapse a simplex in degenerate cases, and the signs in the chain map must respect the vertex order. Homology here is defined using the quotient of ordered chain groups by degenerate chains. The sign details are omitted.\n\n**Limits and counterexamples**:\n\n- The **finiteness** of $K$ is essential. For infinite complexes, repeated barycentric subdivision need not make the mesh tend to zero (an infinite sequence of subdivisions can remain coarse), so the star condition may fail.\n\n- $L$ need not be finite. The theorem only uses that the vertex stars of $L$ form an open cover.\n\n- The open star $\\operatorname{st}(w)$ is generally not convex, so one cannot justify the homotopy by joining points in $\\operatorname{st}(w)$. Part (ii) of Lemma 1 uses that $f(x), g(x)$ lie in a common **closed simplex** $\\rho$, and $\\rho$ is convex.\n\n⭐ **Why the corollary matters**: It gives three conclusions at once. (i) Simplicial homology is a topological invariant independent of triangulation. (ii) Singular homology can be computed from ranks of finite matrices. (iii) The Euler characteristic formula carries over to singular homology (see the edge for `thm.euler-characteristic`).\n\n💡 In short: **continuous map $\\approx$ simplicial map after subdivision**. This gives a combinatorial description of the topology.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#63974b9d'

  'thm.schur-triangularization': {
    statement: {
      en: "**What this does**: Unitary diagonalization is available only for normal matrices (`thm.spectral-matrix`). If the requirement is relaxed to **upper triangular** form, every complex square matrix qualifies.\n\nLet $A \\in M_{n}(\\mathbb{C})$. There is a **unitary** matrix $U$ such that\n\n$$U^{*}AU = T ,$$\n\nwhere $T$ is **upper triangular** and the diagonal entries of $T$ are exactly the eigenvalues of $A$ (in any prescribed order).\n\nReal case: Every real square matrix is orthogonally similar to a **real Schur form**, which is block upper triangular with diagonal blocks of size $1\\times 1$ or $2\\times 2$ (the latter corresponding to conjugate pairs of complex eigenvalues).\n\n**Special case**: When $A$ is normal, $T$ must be diagonal. This is the spectral theorem (`thm.spectral-matrix`).",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#f38a08d0'
    },
    notes: {
      en: "**Proof** (induction on $n$). When $n = 1$, take $U = (1)$. Suppose $n \\ge 2$.\n\n\n\n**Choose an eigenvector**: The characteristic polynomial $\\chi_{A}(t) = \\det(tI - A)$ has degree $n$ and complex coefficients. By the **fundamental theorem of algebra**, it has a root $\\lambda$. Thus there exists $0 \\ne x \\in \\mathbb{C}^{n}$ such that $Ax = \\lambda x$. Normalize $x$, keeping the notation $x$, so that $\\lVert x\\rVert = 1$.\n\n\n\n**Extend to an orthonormal basis**: By **Gram–Schmidt**, extend $x$ to an orthonormal basis of $\\mathbb{C}^{n}$, denoted by $u_{1} = x, u_{2}, \\ldots, u_{n}$. Set $U_{1} := (u_{1}, \\ldots, u_{n})$ (with these basis vectors as columns). Then $U_{1}$ is unitary and $U_{1}^{*}u_{j} = e_{j}$. Thus the first column of $U_{1}^{*}AU_{1}$ is $U_{1}^{*}Ax = \\lambda U_{1}^{*}x = \\lambda e_{1}$. The matrix has $\\lambda$ in the upper left, all $0$ in the lower left, a lower-right block of order $(n-1)$ denoted by $A_{1}$, and a row vector $w^{*}$ in the upper right.\n\n\n\n**Induction**: Apply the induction hypothesis to $A_{1}$. There is a unitary matrix of order $(n-1)$, denoted by $V$, such that $V^{*}A_{1}V = T_{1}$ is upper triangular. Set $U_{2} := \\operatorname{diag}(1, V)$ and $U := U_{1}U_{2}$. The matrix $U_{2}$ is unitary (it is block diagonal with two unitary blocks), so $U$ is unitary. Applying $U_{2}$ on both sides of $U_{1}^{*}AU_{1}$ leaves the upper-left entry $\\lambda$ unchanged, changes the upper-right block to $w^{*}V$, leaves the lower-left block equal to $0$, and changes the lower-right block to $V^{*}A_{1}V = T_{1}$. Thus $U^{*}AU$ is upper triangular ✓.\n\n\n\n**The diagonal entries are the eigenvalues**: $U^{*}AU$ and $A$ are similar, so they have the same characteristic polynomial. The characteristic polynomial of the block upper triangular matrix (upper left $\\lambda$, lower right $A_{1}$, lower left $0$) is $(t - \\lambda)\\chi_{A_{1}}(t)$. Therefore the multiset of diagonal entries of $U^{*}AU$ is exactly $\\operatorname{spec}(A)$. Here the diagonal entries of $T_{1}$ are the eigenvalues of $A_{1}$ by induction, and $\\operatorname{spec}(A) = \\{\\lambda\\} \\cup \\operatorname{spec}(A_{1})$.\n\n\n\n**Prescribing the order**: At each step, choose the first eigenvalue in the prescribed order that has yet to be placed, $\\lambda$ (it occurs in $\\operatorname{spec}(A)$ and hence has an eigenvector). When applying induction to $A_{1}$, use the order induced on $A_{1}$ by the prescribed order. Thus the diagonal order can be specified in advance ✓.\n\n\n\n**Note on the real case**: Nonreal eigenvalues occur in conjugate pairs. Taking real and imaginary parts gives an invariant two-dimensional real subspace. The same induction gives the real Schur form (with diagonal block sizes $1\\times 1$ or $2\\times 2$). The details are omitted. ∎\n\n⭐ **Why it is used more often than Jordan normal form**: Jordan form uses arbitrary similarity transformations ($P^{-1}AP$). It is useful in theory, but $P$ may be too ill-conditioned for computation. Schur form uses unitary similarity ($U^{*}AU$), whose condition number is always 1. Thus **numerical linear algebra uses Schur form instead of Jordan form**.\n\n**A shortcut to the spectral theorem**: If $A$ is normal and $U^{*}AU = T$ is upper triangular, then $T$ is also normal. An upper triangular normal matrix $\\implies$ must be diagonal (compare the two sides of $(TT^{*})_{11}$ to see that the off-diagonal entries of the first row are zero, then use induction).\n\n⚠️ In the real case, **one cannot always obtain a real diagonal or triangular form** with only $1\\times1$ blocks. The rotation example $90^\\circ$ shows why $2\\times 2$ blocks are necessary.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#74970084'

  'thm.riesz-perfect-pairing': {
    statement: {
      en: "Let $(X, Y, \\langle\\cdot,\\cdot\\rangle)$ be a perfect pairing (`def.perfect-pairing`). Then\n\n(a) $x \\mapsto \\langle x, \\cdot\\rangle$ is the image of the isometric isomorphism $X \\to Y^*$, and $y \\mapsto \\langle \\cdot, y\\rangle$ is an isometric embedding of $Y \\to X^*$.\n\n(b) If $T : X \\to Y^*$ is surjective and $Y$ is reflexive, then $S : Y \\to X^*$ is also surjective. Symmetrically, if $S$ is surjective and $X$ is reflexive, then $T$ is surjective. In particular, when $X,Y$ are both reflexive, $T$ is surjective if and only if $S$ is surjective, and then $X \\cong Y^*$ and $Y \\cong X^*$ are isometric isomorphisms.\n\n(c) The pairings for Hilbert spaces $H$ and for $L^p$ ($1 < p < \\infty$, $1/p + 1/q = 1$) are surjective (by the projection theorem in the Hilbert case and Radon–Nikodym in the $L^p$ case). Thus $H^* \\cong H$ and $(L^p)^* \\cong L^q$.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#2f1b6cac'

    notes: {
      en: "**Proof of (a)**: The norming condition gives $\\|x\\| = \\sup_{\\|y\\| \\le 1}|\\langle x, y\\rangle| = \\|T x\\|$, where $T x := \\langle x, \\cdot\\rangle$. Nondegeneracy makes $T$ injective, so $T$ is an isometric embedding. The argument for $y$ is analogous. $\\blacksquare$\n\n\n\n**Proof of (b)**: Suppose $T$ is surjective and $Y$ is reflexive. The map $T$ is an isometric isomorphism $X \\to Y^*$, so $T^{*} : Y^{**} \\to X^{*}$ is an isometric isomorphism. Also, $S = T^{*} \\circ J_Y$ (to check this, use $T^{*} J_Y y (x) = J_Y y(T x) = \\langle x, y\\rangle = S y(x)$). Reflexivity of $Y$ means that $J_Y$ is surjective (an isometric isomorphism), so $S = T^{*} \\circ J_Y$ is surjective. Dualizing gives the symmetric case. $\\blacksquare$\n\n\n\n**Proof of (c)**: **Hilbert case (projection theorem)**: Take $\\varphi \\in H^{*}$. If $\\varphi = 0$, take $y = 0$. Otherwise, $E := \\ker\\varphi$ is a closed proper subspace. By the orthogonal decomposition (`thm.projection-subspace`) $H = E \\oplus E^{\\perp}$, choose $0 \\ne z \\in E^{\\perp}$. Since $z \\notin E$, we have $\\varphi(z) \\ne 0$. Set $y := \\overline{\\varphi(z)}\\, z / \\|z\\|^{2}$. For $x = u + \\lambda z$ ($u \\in E$), we have $\\varphi(x) = \\lambda\\varphi(z)$, and since $\\langle x, y\\rangle = \\lambda\\varphi(z)$, we obtain $\\varphi(x) = \\langle x, y\\rangle$. Also, $\\|y\\| = |\\varphi(z)|/\\|z\\| = \\sup_{\\|x\\| \\le 1}|\\langle x, y\\rangle| = \\|\\varphi\\|$. **$L^{p}$ case (Radon–Nikodym)**: Let $\\Phi \\in (L^{p})^{*}$ and first assume that $\\mu$ is finite (in the general case use a $\\sigma$-finite decomposition). Set $\\nu(E) := \\Phi(\\chi_{E})$. Then $\\nu \\ll \\mu$, so by Radon–Nikodym (`thm.lebesgue-radon-nikodym`) there exists $g \\in L^{1}(\\mu)$ such that $\\nu(E) = \\int_{E} g\\,d\\mu$. Hence $\\Phi(f) = \\int f g\\,d\\mu$ (first for simple functions, then by approximation for $f \\in L^{p}$). By Hölder, $|\\Phi(f)| \\le \\|g\\|_{q}\\|f\\|_{p}$. Taking $f = |g|^{q-1}\\overline{\\operatorname{sgn} g}/\\|g\\|_{q}^{q-1}$ gives $\\|g\\|_{q} \\le \\|\\Phi\\|$, so $g \\in L^{q}$ and $\\|g\\|_{q} = \\|\\Phi\\|$ ($g$ is unique $\\mu$-a.e.). $\\blacksquare$\n\n\n\n⭐ The two Riesz theorems are instances of the same framework: a perfect pairing together with surjectivity. The proofs in (c) use the projection theorem and Radon–Nikodym independently, so there is no circularity.\n\n⚠ **A perfect pairing alone does not imply surjectivity**: In the pairing of $\\ell^1$ with $c_0$, $T : X \\to Y^*$ is surjective but $S : Y \\to X^*$ is not (see the notes to `def.perfect-pairing`). Thus (b) requires reflexivity. Without it, ‘one is surjective $\\implies$ the other is surjective’ is false.",

  'def.split-exact': {
    statement: {
      en: "A short exact sequence\n\n$$0 \\longrightarrow M_{1} \\xrightarrow{\\;i_{1}\\;} M \\xrightarrow{\\;i_{2}\\;} M_{2} \\longrightarrow 0$$\nis called **split** if it is isomorphic to\n\n$$0 \\longrightarrow M_{1} \\xrightarrow{\\;\\mathrm{pr}_{1}\\;} M_{1} \\oplus M_{2} \\xrightarrow{\\;\\mathrm{pr}_{2}\\;} M_{2} \\longrightarrow 0$$\n\n(that is, $i_{1}$ is the inclusion into the first summand and $i_{2}$ is the projection onto the second summand).",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#a8fee1c7'
    },
    notes: {
      en: "**Equivalent criteria** (in an abelian category, the three are equivalent):\n\n\n\n- $i_{2}$ has a **section** $s : M_{2} \\to M$ ($i_{2} \\circ s = \\mathrm{id}$).\n- $i_{1}$ has a **retraction** $r : M \\to M_{1}$ ($r \\circ i_{1} = \\mathrm{id}$).\n- $M \\cong M_{1} \\oplus M_{2}$ is compatible with the sequence.\n\n**Proof.** Write $i := i_{1}$ and $p := i_{2}$. Then $i$ is monic, $p$ is epic, and $\\operatorname{im} i = \\ker p$.\n- **Has a section $\\implies$ splits**: Suppose $p s = 1_{M_{2}}$. Then $p(1_{M} - s p) = p - p s p = 0$, so $1_{M} - sp$ lies in $\\ker p = \\operatorname{im} i$. Thus there exists $r : M \\to M_{1}$ such that\n$$i r = 1_{M} - s p.$$\nSince $i$ is monic, $i(r i) = (1_{M} - sp)\\,i = i - s(p i) = i$ (using $p i = 0$), so $r i = 1_{M_{1}}$ ✓.\n\nTogether with $p i = 0$, the maps $p_{1} := r$, $i_{1} := i$, $p_{2} := p$, and $i_{2} := s$ satisfy the three biproduct identities ($r i = 1$, $p s = 1$, $i r + s p = 1_{M}$, see ‘The direct sum is a product’). Thus $M$ is precisely $M_{1} \\oplus M_{2}$ and the sequence splits ✓.\n- **Splits $\\implies$ has a section**: If the sequence splits, it is isomorphic to $0 \\to M_{1} \\xrightarrow{\\ \\mathrm{pr}_{1}\\ } M_{1} \\oplus M_{2} \\xrightarrow{\\ \\mathrm{pr}_{2}\\ } M_{2} \\to 0$, and $\\mathrm{pr}_{2}$ has an evident section (the inclusion into the second summand). Transport it along the isomorphism to obtain a section of $p$ ✓.\n- **Duality**: Apply the same argument in $\\mathcal{C}^{\\mathrm{op}}$ to obtain ‘splits $\\iff$ $i$ has a retraction’ ✓. ∎\n\n⭐ **This is the splitting lemma**: In an additive category, splitting of a short exact sequence, existence of a section, and a compatible direct-sum decomposition are equivalent. It **reduces questions about sequences to questions about direct sums**, a standard simplification in homological algebra.\n\n⚠️ Short exact sequences **need not** split ($0 \\to \\mathbb{Z} \\xrightarrow{2} \\mathbb{Z} \\to \\mathbb{Z}/2 \\to 0$ is one that does not). Splitting is a special case, and $\\operatorname{Ext}^{1}$ addresses whether a given sequence splits.",

  'thm.lambek': {
    statement: {
      en: "Suppose $R$ is a **unital Noetherian ring** and $M$ is a **left** $R$-module. (The criterion holds for arbitrary unital rings, but the induction below uses the fact that submodules are still finitely generated. The non-Noetherian case requires a filtered colimit argument, which is omitted here.) Then $M$ is flat if and only if, for every **finitely generated right ideal** $I \\subseteq R$, the multiplication map\n\n$$I \\otimes_{R} M \\longrightarrow M, \\qquad a \\otimes m \\longmapsto a m$$\n\nis injective. Equivalently, $\\operatorname{Tor}_{1}^{R}(R/I,\\ M) = 0$ holds for every finitely generated right ideal $I$.\n\n(Over a commutative ring, there is no need to distinguish left and right ideals.)",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#b3e45dba'
    },
    notes: {
      en: "**Why this is a strengthening**: The definition of flatness requires testing **all** injections. Item 4 of `prop.flat-equivalences` already reduces the test to ideals. Lambek reduces it further to **finitely generated** ideals, which often form a countable family and can be checked individually.\n\n**Proof (converse, in three steps)**:\n\n**Step 1 (reduce the** objects being tested **to finitely generated ones)**: Keep the module $M$ being tested **fixed**. The fact that $M$ is a filtered colimit of its finitely generated submodules does not let us reduce $M$ to the finitely generated case. Finite generation does not pass to submodules ($R = k[x,y]$ is flat, but the ideal $(x,y)$ is not). The reduction concerns the maps being tested. It suffices to verify that for every **finitely generated** submodule $K \\subseteq R^{n}$, the map $K \\otimes M \\to R^{n} \\otimes M$ is injective.\n\n**Step 2 (reduce to submodules of free modules)**: Take a surjection $R^{n} \\twoheadrightarrow M_{1}$ (where $M_{1}$ is a finitely generated submodule of $M$). Let $K$ be the preimage of $M_{1}$, giving the short exact sequence $0 \\to K \\to R^{n} \\to M_{1} \\to 0$.\n\nTensor both sequences and compare them. Since $K \\otimes M \\to R^{n} \\otimes M \\to M_{1} \\otimes M \\to 0$ is exact, $K \\otimes M \\to R^{n} \\otimes M$ is injective if and only if $R^{n} \\otimes M \\to M_{1} \\otimes M$ is injective, by the snake lemma.\n\nThus it suffices to show that for every **finitely generated** submodule $K \\subseteq R^{n}$, the map $K \\otimes M \\to R^{n} \\otimes M$ is injective.\n\n**Step 3 (induct on $n$)**: When $n = 1$, the submodule $K$ is a finitely generated right ideal, so the hypothesis applies. When $n > 1$, write $R^{n} = R^{n-1} \\oplus R$ and define $K_{1} := K \\cap R^{n-1}$, with $K_{2}$ the projection of $K$ onto the second component (a finitely generated **right** ideal of $R$, on the same side as the ideals in the criterion. Regard $K \\subseteq R^{n}$ as a right submodule). By **Noetherianity**, $K_{1}$ is **finitely generated** as a submodule of $R^{n-1}$, so the induction hypothesis applies directly (the non-Noetherian case requires a separate filtered colimit argument, omitted here). This gives the short exact sequence\n\n$$0 \\to K_{1} \\to K \\to K_{2} \\to 0, \\qquad 0 \\to R^{n-1} \\to R^{n} \\to R \\to 0 .$$\n\nAfter tensoring, we obtain a $3 \\times 3$ diagram. The vertical maps in the left and right columns are injective by the induction hypothesis and the case $n = 1$, so the vertical map in the middle column is injective by the snake lemma ✓. ∎\n\n⚠️ Testing injectivity only on principal ideals $(a)$ gives **torsion-freeness** over an **integral domain**. This characterization holds only over integral domains: when $R = \\mathbb{Z}/4$ and $M = R/(2)$, non-zero-divisors act injectively, but $(2) \\otimes M \\to M$ is the zero map. Torsion-freeness is weaker than flatness. See `prop.flat-domain-torsionfree` for the counterexample $(x, y) \\subseteq k[x, y]$.\n\n⭐ **Practical use**: To test whether a given $R$-module is flat, it suffices to do one division computation for each finitely generated ideal. Over a commutative Noetherian ring, every ideal is finitely generated, so the criterion simply applies to all ideals.",
      status: 'draft',

  'prop.pd-vanishing': {
    statement: {
      en: "Let $M$ be an $R$-module and let $n \\ge 0$. The following conditions are **equivalent**:\n\n1. $\\operatorname{pd}_{R} M \\le n$.\n2. The $n$th syzygy $\\Omega^{n}M$ is **projective** (equivalently, $M$ has a projective resolution of length $n$).\n3. For every $R$-module $N$ and every $k > n$, we have $\\operatorname{Ext}^{k}_{R}(M, N) = 0$.\n4. For every $R$-module $N$, we have $\\operatorname{Ext}^{n+1}_{R}(M, N) = 0$.\n\nDually, $\\operatorname{id}_{R} M \\le n$ if and only if $\\operatorname{Ext}^{k}_{R}(N, M) = 0$ holds for every $N$ and every $k > n$.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#3046979f'
    },
    notes: {
      en: "**Proof.**\n$(1) \\implies (3)$: Take a length-$n$ projective resolution $P_{\\bullet}$. The truncated resolution is zero at $k > n$, so $\\operatorname{Hom}(P_{\\bullet}, N)$ is also zero in degree $k > n$, and hence its cohomology vanishes ✓ (this uses the fact that projective and injective resolutions compute the same group, as in the two ways of computing Ext).\n$(3) \\implies (4)$: Take $k = n+1$ ✓.\n$(4) \\implies (1)$: Take any projective resolution and compute $K := \\Omega^{n}M$. Applying **dimension shifting** $n$ times gives $\\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{R}(K, N) \\cong \\operatorname{Ext}^{n+1}_{R}(M, N)$, which vanishes for every $N$ ✓.\nThe vanishing of $\\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{R}(K, -)$ in every case forces $K$ to be projective. If $K$ were not projective, a surjection $F \\twoheadrightarrow K$ with $F$ free would fail to split. The classification of first extensions then gives $\\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{R}(K, \\ker) \\ne 0$, a contradiction ✓.\n\nThus $M$ has a projective resolution of length $n$ ✓, giving $(1)$.\n$(1) \\iff (2)$: For a fixed resolution, this restates the definition. Passing to a different resolution requires Schanuel's lemma (stability of syzygies) to show that projectivity of the $n$th syzygy is independent of the resolution ✓. ∎\n\n**Of the four conditions, 2 $\\implies$ 3 is the only substantive step, and it requires a long exact sequence and induction**:\n\n- (1) $\\iff$ (2): This unpacks the definition **for a fixed resolution** ($\\mathrm{pd} \\le n$ means there is a projective resolution of length $n$). Projectivity of the $n$th syzygy is a statement **independent of the resolution** by **Schanuel's lemma / stability of syzygies** (`prop.syzygy-stability`).\n- (2) $\\implies$ (3): **This is the substantive step**. Use the projective resolution $\\cdots \\to P_{1} \\to P_{0} \\to M \\to 0$ to compute $\\operatorname{Ext}$. The group $\\operatorname{Ext}^{k}(M,N)$ is the cohomology computed from the resolution, and a resolution of length $n$ has no term at $k > n$, so the group vanishes. ⚠️ This uses the fact that **projective resolutions compute $\\operatorname{Ext}$** (dimension shifting / balancedness), which itself requires proof.\n- (3) $\\implies$ (4): **Special case** ($k = n+1$).\n- (4) $\\implies$ (2): **This step uses dimension shifting in reverse**. If $\\operatorname{Ext}^{n+1}(M, -)$ always vanishes, take a partial resolution of length $n$ whose $n$th syzygy $K$ satisfies $\\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(K, -) = 0$. Then $K$ is projective (the converse in `prop.ext-projective-vanishes`). **⚠️ This step uses the projectivity criterion for $\\operatorname{Ext}^{1} = 0 \\implies$ in the module category** (which has enough projectives, so no further hypothesis is needed). Care is needed in more general abelian categories.\n\n⚠️ **The case $n = 0$**: $\\mathrm{pd} M \\le 0$ $\\iff$ $M$ is projective $\\iff$ $\\operatorname{Ext}^{k}(M,-) = 0$ for all $k \\ge 1$. The assertion that $\\operatorname{Ext}^{1} = 0$ implies projectivity holds for arbitrary $n$: only the case $n = 0$ (that is, $\\operatorname{Ext}^{1}$) behaves this way.\n\n\n**Hypotheses used in each direction** (the dimension-shifting criterion in module categories):\n- (1) $\\iff$ existence of a projective resolution of length $n$ is the definition. If (2) refers to the $n$th syzygy in an arbitrarily chosen resolution, independence of the resolution also requires Schanuel's lemma or dimension shifting.\n- (2) $\\implies$ (3): Truncating the projective resolution and using it to compute $\\operatorname{Ext}$ requires the theorem on computation by resolutions. For (3) $\\implies$ (4), simply take $k=n+1$.\n- (4) $\\implies$ (2): Dimension shifting reduces the condition to $\\operatorname{Ext}^1(\\Omega^nM,N)=0$ for all $N$, and splitting short exact sequences then gives projectivity. Module categories have enough projectives, with no requirement that the ring be a PID.\n- The quantifier over all $N$ cannot be replaced by a single $N$. Taking $N=0$ gives vanishing for arbitrary $M$. For example, $\\mathbb Z/2$ is not a projective $\\mathbb Z$-module.\n\n\n⭐ **Practical form**: To test the dimension, one generally computes just **one** group for each coefficient module. It suffices that $\\operatorname{Ext}^{n+1}_{R}(M, N)$ vanish for every $N$, without checking every degree.   'thm.ext1-extensions': {
    statement: {
      en: "Let $M, N$ be $R$-modules. An **extension** of $N$ by $M$ is a short exact sequence\n\n$$0 \\to N \\to E \\xrightarrow{\\ \\pi\\ } M \\to 0 .$$\n\nTwo extensions are **equivalent** if there is an isomorphism between them making both squares commute and inducing the identity on $N$ and $M$. The equivalence classes are in **bijection** with $\\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{R}(M, N)$. The **Baer sum** makes these classes into an abelian group, and the bijection is a group isomorphism.\n\nThe **split extension** ($E \\cong N\\oplus M$) corresponds precisely to the zero element.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#4e71b1a6'

    notes: {
      en: "**Proof (construct the map using a projective precover).** Choose a projective precover $0 \\to K \\to P \\xrightarrow{\\ p\\ } M \\to 0$.\n\nBy dimension shifting, $\\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{R}(M, N) \\cong \\operatorname{coker}\\bigl(\\operatorname{Hom}(P, N) \\to \\operatorname{Hom}(K, N)\\bigr)$ ✓.\n**From an extension to a class.** Given an extension $0 \\to N \\to E \\to M \\to 0$, pull it back along $p$ to get $0 \\to N \\to E_{0} \\to P \\to 0$, where $E_{0} = \\{\\, (e, x) : \\pi(e) = p(x) \\,\\}$.\n\nSince $P$ is projective, the sequence **splits**. Choose a splitting $\\sigma : P \\to E_{0}$.\n\nThe composite $K \\hookrightarrow P \\xrightarrow{\\sigma} E_{0} \\to N$ gives $\\theta \\in \\operatorname{Hom}(K, N)$ ✓.\n\nAny two choices of splitting differ by an element of $\\operatorname{Hom}(P, N)$, so the **class** of $\\theta$ in the cokernel is well-defined ✓.\n**From a class to an extension.** Given $\\theta : K \\to N$, form the pushout $E := (P \\oplus N)/\\{\\, (k, -\\theta k) : k \\in K \\,\\}$ to obtain a commutative diagram and a short exact sequence $0 \\to N \\to E \\to M \\to 0$ ✓.\n**The two constructions are inverse.** By construction, the two steps undo one another. Pulling back and then pushing out yields an extension isomorphic to the original, and in the other direction the class of $\\theta$ is recovered ✓. This uses the fact that any two splittings differ by an element of $\\operatorname{Hom}(P,N)$.\n**Baer sum.** Given two extensions $E_{1}, E_{2}$, take their fiber product over $M$, denoted $E_{1}\\times_{M}E_{2}$ (it combines the two middle terms over the same $M$). Then quotient by the image of the antidiagonal $\\{\\, (n, -n) : n \\in N \\,\\}$. The resulting middle term, denoted $E_{1} + E_{2}$, gives the Baer sum ✓.\n\nAssociativity and commutativity follow from the fiber product and quotient constructions.\n\nThe zero element is $N\\oplus M$ (the split extension).\n\nThe inverse of $E$ is obtained by replacing $E\\to M$ with the negative of $N\\to E$, that is, in $E$ reverse the sign of $N$ ✓.\n\nThus the equivalence classes form an abelian group ✓.\n**Agreement with the group structure on $\\operatorname{Ext}^{1}$.** The bijection above sends the Baer sum to addition in $\\operatorname{Ext}^{1}$. When adding the classes of two extensions, the chosen splittings of the pullbacks can be combined, so the corresponding $\\theta$ add ✓. ∎\n\n⭐ **This gives an interpretation of the elements of $\\operatorname{Ext}^{1}$**: The group $\\operatorname{Ext}^{1}$ classifies **actual middle terms**. Thus $\\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(M, N) = 0$ has a concrete meaning that can be checked by hand: **every extension splits**.\n\n📌 Higher $\\operatorname{Ext}$ groups also have an interpretation in terms of their elements. The group $\\operatorname{Ext}^{n}(M, N)$ classifies extensions $Y$ of the form $N \\to E_{1}\\to \\cdots \\to E_{n}\\to M$ and of degree $n$ (homotopy classes of exact sequences of length $n$).\n\nThe result for $\\operatorname{Ext}^{1}$ is the simplest case of $n = 1$.\n\n⚠️ In the strict definition of equivalence, the isomorphism must **be the identity at both ends**. Allowing arbitrary isomorphisms at the ends gives a coarser classification by orbits of invertible elements in $\\operatorname{Hom}$, so the original correspondence is no longer one-to-one.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#79ce3619'

  'prop.tor-flat-vanishing': {
    statement: {
      en: "Let $R$ be a ring and $F$ a right $R$-module. The following conditions are equivalent:\n\n1. $F$ is **flat**;\n2. For every left $R$-module $N$, $\\operatorname{Tor}_{1}^{R}(F, N) = 0$;\n3. For every left $R$-module $N$ and every $n \\ge 1$, $\\operatorname{Tor}_{n}^{R}(F, N) = 0$.\n\nThus the flat modules are precisely those for which all higher Tor groups vanish. This is exactly parallel to the characterization of projective modules by the vanishing of all higher $\\operatorname{Ext}$ groups.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#58c6a52b'
    },
    notes: {
      en: "**Proof.**\n$(1) \\implies (3)$: For $F$ take a projective resolution $P_{\\bullet} \\to F$.\n\nWhen $F$ is flat, $\\operatorname{Tor}_{n}$ can also be computed using a resolution of $N$. Take a projective resolution of $N$, denoted by $Q_{\\bullet}$. The resolution on the $N$ side gives $\\operatorname{Tor}_{n}(F, N) = H_{n}(F\\otimes Q_{\\bullet})$.\n\nSince $F\\otimes -$ is exact and $Q_{\\bullet} \\to N$ is exact, $F\\otimes Q_{\\bullet}$ has homology only in degree $0$, and its higher homology vanishes ✓.\n$(3) \\implies (2)$: Take $n = 1$ ✓.\n$(2) \\implies (1)$: Take any injection $f : M_{1} \\rightarrowtail M$. We must show that $F\\otimes M_{1} \\to F\\otimes M$ is injective. Include it in the short exact sequence $0 \\to M_{1} \\to M \\to M/M_{1} \\to 0$, tensor with $F$, and take the long exact sequence\n$$\\operatorname{Tor}_{1}^{R}(F,\\ M/M_{1}) \\to F\\otimes M_{1} \\to F\\otimes M \\to F\\otimes M/M_{1} \\to 0 ,$$\nThe leftmost term is zero by condition 2, so $F\\otimes M_{1} \\to F\\otimes M$ is **injective** ✓, which gives exactness of $F\\otimes -$ ✓. ∎\n\n**Of the three conditions, (2)$\\implies$(3) is the only step that requires induction. The equivalence (1)$\\iff$(2) uses a long exact sequence**:\n\n- (1) $\\iff$ (2): **A long exact sequence is needed** (flatness means that $(-)\\otimes F$ is exact. Its equivalence to the universal vanishing of $\\operatorname{Tor}_{1}$ follows from the long exact sequence for $\\operatorname{Tor}$).\n- (2) $\\implies$ (3): **This step uses dimension shifting**. If $\\operatorname{Tor}_{1}(F, N) = 0$ for all $N$, take the short exact sequence for $N$ and use the long exact sequence to obtain $\\operatorname{Tor}_{n} = 0$ by induction. ⚠️ This requires the long exact sequence (the Tor version of `thm.long-exact`).\n- (3) $\\implies$ (2): **A special case** ($n = 1$).\n\n⚠️ **Comparison with projectivity**: Projective $\\iff$ $\\operatorname{Ext}^{n}(M,-) = 0$ for all $n \\ge 1$. Flat $\\iff$ $\\operatorname{Tor}_{n}(-,N) = 0$ for all $n \\ge 1$. The criteria are parallel in form, but **flatness is weaker than projectivity** (projective $\\implies$ flat, and the converse fails. For example, $\\mathbb{Q}$ is flat over $\\mathbb{Z}$ but is not projective).\n⚠️ $\\operatorname{Tor}$ can be computed in **either variable**. Here $F$ is on the left and $N$ is on the right. Reversing the order gives isomorphic results ($\\operatorname{Tor}$ is balanced), but over a noncommutative ring one must also pass to the opposite ring: $\\operatorname{Tor}_{n}^{R}(M,N) \\cong \\operatorname{Tor}_{n}^{R^{\\mathrm{op}}}(N,M)$ (for example, when $R = M_{2}(k)$, the original structures put $N$ and $M$ on different sides).\n\n\n**Assumptions used in each direction** (flatness is defined by exactness of tensoring, and the Tor criterion uses derived functors):\n- Flatness $\\iff$ exactness of $F\\otimes_R-$ is the definition. The implication (1) $\\implies$ (3) also uses a projective resolution of the left module $N$ to compute Tor and uses the fact that exact functors preserve exactness of resolutions.\n- (3) $\\implies$ (2): Take $n=1$, a direct specialization. (2) $\\implies$ (1): The Tor long exact sequence of a short exact sequence shows that tensoring preserves injections. Combine this with the right exactness of tensoring.\n- These implications hold over any unital ring, with no commutativity, Noetherian, or PID hypothesis. The pairing of $F$ as a right module with $N$ as a left module is essential. Over a noncommutative ring the two inputs cannot be interchanged freely.\n- Vanishing is required for every $N$. Testing only $N=R$ gives vanishing for any $F$, including $F=\\mathbb Z/2$, which is not flat. Flatness cannot be replaced by projectivity either. For example, $\\mathbb Q$ is a flat, nonprojective $\\mathbb Z$-module.\n\n\n⭐ **This gives a condition for flatness that can be checked one case at a time**. Instead of examining all injections, compute, for each $N$, the group $\\operatorname{Tor}_{1}$. In practice this is often used with Lambek's criterion, which requires testing only finitely generated ideals.\n\n📌 **Corollaries**: **Direct summands** of flat modules are flat ($\\operatorname{Tor}$ commutes with direct sums), and direct sums of flat modules are **flat**. Projective modules are flat (vanishing in higher degrees on the $\\operatorname{Ext}$ side is the dual manifestation of the same phenomenon).\n\n⚠️ Here $\\operatorname{Tor}_{n}^{R}(F, -)$ treats $F$ as a **right** module. Over a noncommutative ring, right flatness of $F$ and left flatness of $F$ (that is, $\\operatorname{Tor}_{n}^{R}(-, F) = 0$) are **two distinct conditions**. This result concerns right flatness. Over a commutative ring the two conditions coincide ✓.",

      en: "**Ingredients of the standard proof.** Proofs of ① and ③ are given below. The comparison in ④ is still missing, so this node is marked `status: 'skeleton'`.\n\n**① The Čech complex of sheaves is a resolution.** For a cover $\\mathfrak{U} = (U_{i})_{i}$, set $\\check{\\mathcal{C}}^{p}(\\mathfrak{U}, \\mathcal{M}) := \\prod_{i_{0} < \\cdots < i_{p}} j_{*}\\bigl(\\mathcal{M}|_{U_{i_{0} \\cdots i_{p}}}\\bigr)$, where $j : U_{i_{0} \\cdots i_{p}} \\hookrightarrow X$ is the inclusion.\n\n**Proof of ①.** It suffices to check the stalks. The two formulations of fineness apply as follows. **(a) Sheaves of $\\mathcal C(X)$-modules**: A partition of unity for a locally finite cover $\\{V_{j}\\}$, denoted by $\\{\\rho_{j}\\}$ ($\\sum_{j}\\rho_{j}=1$, $\\operatorname{supp}\\rho_{j}\\subseteq V_{j}$), gives endomorphisms by multiplication by $\\rho_{j}$, so $\\rho_{j}s_{j}$ is defined pointwise. **(b) General sheaves of abelian groups**: Use a **partition by endomorphisms** $\\eta_{j}:\\mathcal F \\to \\mathcal F$, with $\\operatorname{supp}\\eta_{j}\\subseteq V_{j}$ and $\\sum_{j}\\eta_{j}=\\operatorname{id}_{\\mathcal F}$ (a locally finite sum). Each $x \\in F$ has a neighborhood $U_{x}$ and a section $s_{x} \\in \\mathcal F(U_{x})$ such that $s_{x}|_{F \\cap U_{x}}=s$. Take a locally finite refinement of $\\{U_{x}\\}_{x \\in F} \\cup \\{X \\setminus F\\}$, denoted by $\\{V_{j}\\}$, with the corresponding $\\rho_{j}$ or $\\eta_{j}$ as above. Take $s_{j} \\in \\mathcal F(V_{j})$ to be the corresponding local section (when $V_{j} \\subseteq X \\setminus F$, take $0$). In case (b), $\\operatorname{supp}\\eta_{j} \\subseteq V_{j}$ ensures that $\\eta_{j}(s_{j})$ extends outside $V_{j}$ by $0$ (both sections equal $0$ on the overlap, so they agree). Apply Zorn's lemma along a locally finite closed refinement $\\{A_{i}\\}$. Given a lift over the closed union $B$, denoted by $s$, choose $A_{i} \\not\\subseteq B$.   'lem.single-cube-stokes': {
    statement: {
      en: "**The calculation on a standard cube**: Stokes' theorem is first verified on a single standard cube. The left-hand side is a multiple integral, and the right-hand side integrates over $2k$ faces. The two sides agree term by term, checking all the sign conventions.\n\nLet $\\eta$ be defined on an open set containing $I^{k} = [0,1]^{k}$ and be a $(k-1)$-form ($k\\ge 1$). Then\n\n$$\\int_{I^{k}}\\mathrm{d}\\eta = \\int_{\\partial I^{k}}\\eta ,$$\n\nHere $I^{k}$ has the standard orientation, and $\\partial I^{k}$ is interpreted according to the sign convention in `def.boundary-operator` as a $(k-1)$-chain.\n\nWhen $k = 1$, this is the fundamental theorem of calculus: $\\int_{0}^{1}F^{\\prime}(t)\\,\\mathrm{d}t = F(1)-F(0)$.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#1b1f3aa2'
    },
    notes: {
      en: "**Proof**: Expand $\\eta$ uniquely according to which $\\mathrm{d}x^{j}$ is omitted:\n\n$$\\eta = \\sum_{j=1}^{k}(-1)^{j-1} f_{j}\\,\\mathrm{d}x^{1}\\wedge\\cdots\\wedge\\widehat{\\mathrm{d}x^{j}}\\wedge\\cdots\\wedge\\mathrm{d}x^{k},$$\n\nHere $f_{j}$ are smooth functions on an open set containing $I^{k}$ (a hat indicates that the factor is omitted).\n\n**Left-hand side.** Take the exterior derivative term by term. When $\\mathrm{d}f_{j}$ is wedged with the remaining factors, only the $\\mathrm{d}x^{j}$ term survives, and\n\n$$\\mathrm{d}\\left((-1)^{j-1} f_{j}\\,\\mathrm{d}x^{1}\\wedge\\cdots\\widehat{\\mathrm{d}x^{j}}\\cdots\\wedge\\mathrm{d}x^{k}\\right) = \\partial_{j} f_{j}\\,\\mathrm{d}x^{1}\\wedge\\cdots\\wedge\\mathrm{d}x^{k},$$\n\nHence $\\mathrm{d}\\eta = \\sum_{j=1}^{k}\\partial_{j} f_{j}\\,\\mathrm{d}x^{1}\\wedge\\cdots\\wedge\\mathrm{d}x^{k}$. By Fubini's theorem and the one-variable fundamental theorem of calculus (integrating first with respect to $x^{j}$ and denoting the remaining coordinates by $y$),\n\n$$\\int_{I^{k}}\\mathrm{d}\\eta = \\sum_{j=1}^{k}\\int_{I^{k-1}}\\left[f_{j}\\big|_{x^{j}=1} - f_{j}\\big|_{x^{j}=0}\\right]\\mathrm{d}y.$$\n\n**Right-hand side.** By `def.boundary-operator`, $\\partial I^{k} = \\sum_{i=1}^{k}\\sum_{\\alpha=0}^{1}(-1)^{i+\\alpha} I^{k}_{(i,\\alpha)}$. On face $(i,\\alpha)$, the pullback of $\\mathrm{d}x^{i}$ is $0$, so only the term with $j = i$ survives:\n\n$$I^{k}_{(i,\\alpha)}{}^{*}\\eta = (-1)^{i-1} f_{i}\\big|_{x^{i}=\\alpha}\\,\\mathrm{d}y^{1}\\wedge\\cdots\\wedge\\mathrm{d}y^{k-1},$$\n\nHere $y$ denotes the remaining $k-1$ coordinates on the face, in their original order. Thus\n\n$$\\int_{\\partial I^{k}}\\eta = \\sum_{i=1}^{k}\\sum_{\\alpha=0}^{1}(-1)^{i+\\alpha}(-1)^{i-1}\\int_{I^{k-1}} f_{i}\\big|_{x^{i}=\\alpha}\\,\\mathrm{d}y = \\sum_{i=1}^{k}\\int_{I^{k-1}}\\left[f_{i}\\big|_{x^{i}=1} - f_{i}\\big|_{x^{i}=0}\\right]\\mathrm{d}y,$$\n\nThe last step uses $(-1)^{i+\\alpha+i-1} = (-1)^{\\alpha+1}$. The case $\\alpha=1$ gives $+1$, and the case $\\alpha=0$ gives $-1$.\n\nThe two sides agree term by term, so $\\int_{I^{k}}\\mathrm{d}\\eta = \\int_{\\partial I^{k}}\\eta$. When $k = 1$, we have $\\eta = f$ and $\\mathrm{d}\\eta = f^{\\prime}\\mathrm{d}x$. The two sides give $\\int_{0}^{1} f^{\\prime} = f(1) - f(0)$ and $f(1) - f(0)$, respectively, so the sign conventions agree. ∎\n\n**Why this case comes first**: The case of a general cube $c$ follows by pullback ($c^{\\ast}\\mathrm{d} = \\mathrm{d}c^{\\ast}$), which reduces it to this lemma. This lemma therefore supplies the **only** substantive calculation in Stokes' theorem for chains. The rest is the passage to general cubes and chains.\n\n**⚠️ The most common sign error**: When $\\eta$ is written as $\\sum_{j}(-1)^{j-1}f_{j}\\mathrm{d}x^{1}\\wedge\\cdots\\widehat{\\mathrm{d}x^{j}}\\cdots\\wedge\\mathrm{d}x^{k}$ (a hat indicates that the factor is omitted), there is **no** additional sign in $\\mathrm{d}\\eta = \\sum_{j}\\partial_{j}f_{j}\\mathrm{d}x^{1}\\wedge\\cdots\\wedge\\mathrm{d}x^{k}$. Inserting an extra $(-1)^{j-1}$ here throws off the matching of the face terms on the right-hand side.\n\n**⚠️ Measure-zero sets on the boundary**: The form $\\eta$ need only be defined on an open set and continuous up to the boundary. The facewise pullbacks use the values of $\\eta$ on each face, and $\\partial I^{k}$ has measure zero, so the integrals are independent of the choice of extension.\n\n**Relation to the classical formulas**: When $k = 2$, this is Green's theorem. When $k = 3$, it is the Kelvin–Stokes theorem in circulation form. Both are special cases of this lemma.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#d9e65b0a'
    },

  'thm.stokes-chain': {
    statement: {
      en: "**What it says**: Integrating a $(k-1)$-form over the **boundary chain** gives the same result as integrating its exterior derivative over the **chain**. This is the common generalization of the fundamental theorem of calculus, Green's theorem, Gauss's theorem, and the Kelvin–Stokes theorem.\n\nLet $c$ be a chain in $M$ of dimension $k$ ($k\\ge 1$), and let $\\omega$ be defined on a neighborhood of the image of $c$ and be a $(k-1)$-form. Then\n\n$$\\int_{\\partial c}\\omega = \\int_{c}\\mathrm{d}\\omega .$$\n\n**Relation to the manifold version** (`thm.stokes`): The manifold version requires $M$ to be compact and orientable with boundary, and $\\omega$ to be defined on $M$. The chain version does not require $M$ to be orientable or compact, or $\\omega$ to have compact support. It uses a chain in place of a manifold.\n\nEach is a special case of the other. Decompose (or subdivide) $M$ into a covering $n$-chain. Summing the chain formula term by term gives the manifold version.\n\nConversely, restricting the manifold version to a single orientation-preserving parametrized cube gives the single-cube case of the chain version.\n\nWhen $k = 1$ and $c = \\gamma : [0,1]\\to\\mathbb{R}$, we have $\\partial\\gamma = \\gamma(1)-\\gamma(0)$, and the formula becomes $F(\\gamma(1))-F(\\gamma(0)) = \\int_{0}^{1}(F^{\\prime}\\circ\\gamma)\\,\\gamma^{\\prime}\\,\\mathrm{d}t$, precisely the fundamental theorem of calculus.",

  'thm.frobenius': {
    statement: {
      en: "**In plain language**: A smooth family of directions fits together into sheets if and only if it is involutive. Involutivity is the only obstruction. This is the geometric version of solvability for systems of first-order partial differential equations, and it explains how foliations arise.\n\nLet $\\mathcal{D}$ be a tangent distribution on an $n$-dimensional smooth manifold $M$, smooth and of rank $k$. The following four conditions are equivalent:\n\n1. $\\mathcal{D}$ is involutive (`def.involutive-distribution`).\n2. **Existence of flat coordinates**: Each point $p$ has a neighborhood $U$ with coordinates $(x^{1},\\ldots,x^{n})$ in which $\\mathcal{D}|_{U}$ is spanned by $\\partial_{1},\\ldots,\\partial_{k}$.\n3. **Local integrability**: Each point $p$ lies in an $k$-dimensional integral manifold (`def.integral-manifold`).\n4. **Existence of a foliation**: $M$ is covered by a family of pairwise disjoint maximal connected integral manifolds (leaves), each of which is an immersed submanifold.\n\nIn particular, involutivity holds **if and only if** there are always local coordinates that straighten $\\mathcal{D}$ into coordinate slices. When $k = n-1$ and $\\mathcal{D} = \\ker\\omega$ (with $\\omega$ nowhere zero), involutivity is equivalent to\n\n$$\\omega \\wedge \\mathrm{d}\\omega = 0,$$\n\nThis is the classical Frobenius condition. Its proof is the explicit calculation for hyperplane distributions corresponding to the flow-box argument for (1)$\\iff$(2) below.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#cb3c206c'
    },
    notes: {
      en: "**Proof structure**: Condition (2) drives the proof of the four-way equivalence. The two proof edges are `imp.mf2-frobenius-flat` (the flow-box argument and induction on rank for (1)$\\implies$(2)) and `imp.mf2-frobenius-integral` ((2)$\\implies$(3), (3)$\\implies$(1), (2)$\\implies$(4)). The main argument is presented step by step below and can be read independently.\n\n**I. (1)$\\implies$(2): Flow box and induction on rank.** Choose $p$ and, on a neighborhood of $p$, take smooth pointwise independent generators $X_{1},\\ldots,X_{k}$.\n\n1. By `thm.flow-local`, straighten $X_{1}$ along its local flow. There are coordinates $(y^{1},\\ldots,y^{n})$ such that $X_{1}=\\partial_{1}$ (the flow-box theorem).\n2. Write the remaining generators as $X_{i}=a_{i}\\,\\partial_{1}+Y_{i}$ ($i\\ge2$), where $Y_{i}$ has only $\\partial_{2},\\ldots,\\partial_{n}$ components. Since $\\mathrm{d}y^{1}$ is a closed form,\n\n$$\\mathrm{d}y^{1}\\bigl([\\partial_{1},Y_{i}]\\bigr)=\\partial_{1}\\bigl(\\mathrm{d}y^{1}(Y_{i})\\bigr)-Y_{i}\\bigl(\\mathrm{d}y^{1}(\\partial_{1})\\bigr)=0,$$\n\nso $[\\partial_{1},Y_{i}]$ also has only $\\partial_{2},\\ldots,\\partial_{n}$ components.\n3. Involutivity gives that $[X_{1},X_{i}]=[\\partial_{1},a_{i}\\partial_{1}+Y_{i}]=(\\partial_{1}a_{i})\\partial_{1}+[\\partial_{1},Y_{i}]$ lies in $\\mathcal{D}=\\operatorname{span}\\{\\partial_{1},Y_{2},\\ldots,Y_{k}\\}$. Comparing the $\\partial_{1}$ components and using the preceding step gives\n\n$$\\partial_{1}a_{i}=0,\\qquad [\\partial_{1},Y_{i}]\\in\\operatorname{span}\\{Y_{2},\\ldots,Y_{k}\\}.$$\n\nThe latter equation shows that on the transverse hypersurface $S=\\{y^{1}=0\\}$, the distribution $\\mathcal{D}_{S}=\\operatorname{span}\\{Y_{2},\\ldots,Y_{k}\\}$ is involutive (of rank $k-1$).\n4. Induct on $k$ (when $k=1$, $\\partial_{1}$ itself gives the desired coordinates). By the induction hypothesis, on $S$ there are coordinates $(x^{2},\\ldots,x^{n})$ in which $\\mathcal{D}_{S}$ is spanned by $\\partial_{2},\\ldots,\\partial_{k}$. Use the flow of $X_{1}=\\partial_{1}$ to transport the coordinates of $S$ in the $x^{1}$ direction, obtaining on a neighborhood of $p$ the coordinates $(x^{1},x^{2},\\ldots,x^{n})$.\n5. In these coordinates, $[\\partial_{1},\\partial_{a}]=0$ ($a\\ge2$). Write $Y_{i}$ as $Y_{i}=\\sum_{a\\ge2}f^{a}_{i}\\,\\partial_{a}$. Since $[\\partial_{1},Y_{i}]\\in\\operatorname{span}\\{Y_{j}\\}$ has no $\\partial_{1}$ component and $[\\partial_{1},\\partial_{a}]=0$, we obtain $\\partial_{1}f^{a}_{i}=0$. On the slice $x^{1}=0$, we have $Y_{i}=\\partial_{i}$ ($i=2,\\ldots,k$ by the induction hypothesis), so $f^{a}_{i}=\\delta^{a}_{i}$ is independent of $x^{1}$. Thus $Y_{i}=\\partial_{i}$ holds throughout the neighborhood. Hence\n\n$$\\mathcal{D}=\\operatorname{span}\\{\\partial_{1},\\ldots,\\partial_{k}\\}.\\qquad\\blacksquare$$\n\n**II. (2)$\\implies$(3):** In flat coordinates, the tangent spaces of $N=\\{x^{k+1}=\\cdots=x^{n}=0\\}$ are spanned precisely by $\\partial_{1},\\ldots,\\partial_{k}$, so $N$ is an $k$-dimensional integral manifold through $p$. $\\square$\n\n**III. (3)$\\implies$(1):** If $X,Y$ are tangent at $p$ to an integral manifold $N$, then $[X,Y]_{p}$ is also tangent to $N$ (by naturality of the bracket, $[X,Y]$ depends only on first-order information about $X,Y$, and after extending $X,Y$ to vector fields tangent to $N$, their bracket still lies in $TN$, see `def.lie-bracket`). Thus $\\mathcal{D}$ is involutive. $\\square$\n\n**IV. (2)$\\implies$(4):** Each point has a coordinate chart $N_{q}$. Order all connected integral manifolds by inclusion and apply Zorn's lemma to obtain maximal elements. The union of a chain is still a connected integral manifold, since locally it is part of a coordinate chart and smoothness is preserved along the chain. The maximal elements are pairwise disjoint, since otherwise their union would still be a connected integral manifold, and they cover $M$. We need $M$ to be second countable to ensure that the set of leaves is countable and the construction is well-defined. Second countability is already part of the definition of a manifold. See `imp.mf2-frobenius-integral` for all four steps. $\\square$\n\n**V. The calculation for $k=n-1$ and $\\omega\\wedge\\mathrm{d}\\omega=0$.** Suppose $\\mathcal{D}=\\ker\\omega$ and $\\omega$ is nowhere zero. For $X,Y\\in\\ker\\omega$,\n\n$$\\mathrm{d}\\omega(X,Y)=X\\omega(Y)-Y\\omega(X)-\\omega([X,Y])=-\\omega([X,Y]).$$\n\nThus involutivity ($[X,Y]\\in\\ker\\omega$) is equivalent to $\\mathrm{d}\\omega$ vanishing identically on $\\ker\\omega\\times\\ker\\omega$. In turn, $\\mathrm{d}\\omega$ vanishes on $\\ker\\omega$ if and only if $\\mathrm{d}\\omega=\\omega\\wedge\\alpha$ (for some $1$-form $\\alpha$), which is equivalent to $\\omega\\wedge\\mathrm{d}\\omega=0$, as one sees by expanding both sides using the same $\\alpha$. $\\square$\n\n**VI. The condition is sharp.** A contact distribution is a counterexample (see the notes to `def.involutive-distribution`). It is smooth, has rank $2$, and is nowhere zero, yet fails to be involutive, so no surface can be an integral manifold for it. Involutivity is therefore necessary as well as sufficient.\n\n**VII. The conclusion is local.** The theorem gives foliation coordinates near each point. It does **not** guarantee global coordinates straightening $\\mathcal{D}$ into coordinate slices of $\\mathbb{R}^{n}$, which would require the leaf space itself to be a product manifold. The irrational-slope foliation on the torus is a counterexample. Its leaves are locally straight lines, yet each winds densely around the torus.\n\n**VIII. Analytic formulation and smoothness.** Frobenius gives the compatibility condition for local solvability of the system of first-order linear partial differential equations $\\partial_{i}f=\\sum_{j}a^{j}_{i}\\partial_{j}f$ ($i>k$). Compatibility is precisely involutivity, which is also how to read $\\mathrm{d}^{2}=0$. Step 1 uses Picard–Lindelöf to solve the ODE, so $\\mathcal{D}$ must be smooth (at least $C^{2}$ to discuss brackets). Below $C^{1}$, there are counterexamples that are nowhere involutive yet have integral surfaces everywhere.\n\n**IX. Notation.** Here we use the language of families of $k$-dimensional subspaces. Textbooks often use integral manifolds of a differential ideal $I\\subseteq\\Omega^{\\bullet}(M)$. The two formulations are equivalent (take $I$ to be the ideal generated by forms annihilating $\\mathcal{D}$). We use only the distribution formulation here to avoid introducing a second notation. The cases $k=n$ and $k=0$ are trivial. $k=1$ is always involutive, and the nontrivial examples begin at $2\\le k\\le n-1$.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#9617c8aa'
    },

  'thm.de-rham': {
    statement: {
      en: "**In plain language**: The cohomology defined using calculus (closed forms modulo exact forms) is the same as the topological cohomology built from simplices (by dualizing singular chains). This theorem connects analysis, geometry, and topology.\n\nLet $M$ be a smooth manifold. For each $k \\ge 0$ there is an isomorphism\n\n$$\\Phi^{k} : H^{k}_{\\mathrm{dR}}(M) \\longrightarrow H^{k}_{\\mathrm{sing}}(M;\\mathbb{R}),\\qquad [\\omega] \\longmapsto \\Bigl([c] \\mapsto \\int_{c}\\omega\\Bigr),$$\n\nwhere $H^{k}_{\\mathrm{sing}}(M;\\mathbb{R}) := H^{k}\\bigl(\\operatorname{Hom}(C^{\\mathrm{sing}}_{\\bullet}(M),\\mathbb{R})\\bigr)$ is the cohomology of the dual of the singular chain complex (`def.singular-chain`), and $\\int_{c}\\omega$ is integration of forms over chains.\n\n$\\Phi^{k}$ is compatible with pullbacks, so $k \\mapsto \\Phi^{k}$ is a family of natural isomorphisms. $H^{\\bullet}_{\\mathrm{dR}}$ and $H^{\\bullet}_{\\mathrm{sing}}$ are two ways to compute the same functor.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#a08b3fdf'
