    notes: {
      en: "**This node retains skeleton status**: The graph has no full proof of the de Rham theorem. The outline below divides the proof into six steps and identifies which parts can be verified in full within the graph and which require external theorems. It names each missing result so that it can be checked individually as nodes are added.\n**Step 0: Conventions.** For smooth singular chains $C^{\\infty}_{\\bullet}(M)$, each simplex $\\sigma:\\Delta^{k}\\to M$ is smooth and extends to a prism neighborhood of $\\Delta^{k}$, so every face is smooth. $\\Omega^{q}$ is the sheaf of $q$-forms, and $\\mathrm{d}$ is the differential of the complex of sheaves. $\\Gamma(M,\\Omega^{\\bullet})=\\Omega^{\\bullet}(M)$ is the de Rham complex.\n**Step 1: The pairing is well-defined (fully verifiable within the graph).** First note that $\\int_{c}\\omega$ is undefined on **continuous** singular chains, since $c$ need not be smooth and $\\omega$ is defined only on smooth tangent vectors. We therefore use smooth singular chains. Both invariance properties follow from `thm.stokes-chain`: if $\\omega$ is closed and $c=\\partial b$, then $\\int_{c}\\omega=\\int_{b}\\mathrm{d}\\omega=0$. If $\\omega=\\mathrm{d}\\eta$, then $\\int_{c}\\mathrm{d}\\eta=\\int_{\\partial c}\\eta=0$ (with $c$ a closed chain). Thus $\\Phi^{k}([\\omega])([c])=\\int_{c}\\omega$ is well-defined and clearly $\\mathbb{R}$-bilinear.\n**Step 2: $\\Omega^{\\bullet}$ is a resolution of the constant sheaf $\\underline{\\mathbb{R}}$ (exactness is fully verifiable within the graph).** Exactness at each stalk follows from the Poincaré lemma (`lem.poincare-lemma`). $\\ker(\\mathrm{d}:\\Omega^{0}\\to\\Omega^{1})$ is the sheaf of locally constant functions $\\underline{\\mathbb{R}}$. Since $\\Omega^{q}$ is a fine sheaf and fine sheaves on paracompact manifolds have vanishing higher cohomology (Godement's lemma), we obtain $H^{k}_{\\mathrm{dR}}(M)=H^{k}\\bigl(\\Gamma(M,\\Omega^{\\bullet})\\bigr)\\cong H^{k}(M;\\underline{\\mathbb{R}}).$. The **exactness** in this step can be checked in full. **Acyclicity of fine sheaves** is an external lemma (Gap D).\n**Step 3: The Čech–de Rham double complex.** Choose a good cover $\\mathfrak{U}=\\{U_\\alpha\\}$ (each point has a basis of contractible neighborhoods). Arrange $\\check{C}^{p}(\\mathfrak{U},\\Omega^{q})$ into a double complex with the Čech coboundary $\\delta$ as the horizontal differential and $\\mathrm{d}$ as the vertical differential. The cohomology of the total complex $\\operatorname{Tot}$ can be computed in either direction. Taking cohomology first with respect to $\\mathrm{d}$ (each column $\\Omega^{\\bullet}(U_{p})$ is exact by the Poincaré lemma, leaving only, at $q=0$, the term $H^{0}_{\\mathrm{dR}}(U_{p})=\\mathbb{R}$ in the sense of the corresponding column term) gives $E_{1}^{p,0}=\\check{C}^{p}(\\mathfrak{U};\\underline{\\mathbb{R}})$, hence $H^{\\bullet}(\\operatorname{Tot})\\cong\\check{H}^{\\bullet}(\\mathfrak{U};\\underline{\\mathbb{R}})$. Taking cohomology first with respect to $\\delta$ ($\\Omega^{q}$ is fine and the Čech complex on a good cover is exact at $p>0$) gives $E_{1}^{0,q}=\\Omega^{q}(M)$, hence $H^{\\bullet}(\\operatorname{Tot})\\cong H^{\\bullet}_{\\mathrm{dR}}(M)$. Thus $H^{\\bullet}_{\\mathrm{dR}}(M)\\cong\\check{H}^{\\bullet}(\\mathfrak{U};\\underline{\\mathbb{R}})$. This requires **Gap B (existence of good covers)** and **Gap C (spectral sequences of double complexes)**.\n**Step 4: Čech cohomology equals sheaf cohomology.** On paracompact spaces, $\\check{H}^{\\bullet}(\\mathfrak{U};\\underline{\\mathbb{R}})\\cong H^{\\bullet}(M;\\underline{\\mathbb{R}})$ (`thm.paracompact-cech-equals-sheaf`). ⚠️ That node **also remains a skeleton**. It outlines the argument using the facts that fine sheaves are soft and soft sheaves are acyclic, followed by an acyclic resolution, but leaves Godement's lemma and the comparison lemma unproved. This step is therefore also unproved in the graph and is labeled **Gap E**.\n**Step 5: Sheaf cohomology equals singular cohomology.** Both functors are homotopy invariant, agree with $\\mathbb{R}$ on a point, and have long exact sequences for good covers. The Eilenberg–Steenrod uniqueness theorem gives a natural isomorphism. The graph has neither a node nor a proof for this axiomatic uniqueness result, labeled **Gap F**.\n**Step 6: $\\Phi$ agrees with these isomorphisms (naturality).** One must verify that the chain-level map $\\omega\\mapsto\\bigl(c\\mapsto\\int_{c}\\omega\\bigr)$ agrees with the composite of the isomorphisms constructed in Steps 2–3 and is natural under pullbacks ($\\int_{f_{*}c}f^{*}\\omega=\\int_{c}\\omega$). Stokes gives commutativity, but the full verification, especially the passage between smooth and continuous chains, is missing. This is **Gap G**.\n**Summary of gaps**: (A) Smooth singular chains $\\simeq$ continuous singular chains (simplicial approximation and smoothing). (B) Existence of good covers on smooth manifolds (geodesically convex neighborhoods and convexity radius). (C) Convergence of spectral sequences of double complexes (the graph has only ingredients such as `def.spectral-sequence`, `thm.filtered-complex-spectral`, and `cor.cech-cohomology-spectral`). (D) Acyclicity of fine sheaves (Godement). (E) Čech cohomology equals sheaf cohomology (`thm.paracompact-cech-equals-sheaf` remains a skeleton). (F) Sheaf cohomology equals singular cohomology (Eilenberg–Steenrod uniqueness). (G) Full verification of compatibility via Stokes and of naturality. **The well-definedness and exactness assertions in Steps 1 and 2 can be verified in full. The remaining parts rely on standard external results unproved in the graph, so the proof carries no ∎ and retains skeleton status.**\n**Why this is a theorem**: The two complexes are entirely different (smooth forms and topological simplices), so their agreement requires proof. The central step is Step 2, where the assertion that $\\Omega^{\\bullet}$ resolves the constant sheaf globalizes the Poincaré lemma.\n**Corollaries**: $H^{k}_{\\mathrm{dR}}$ is homotopy invariant. On a compact manifold, each $H^{k}_{\\mathrm{dR}}$ is finite-dimensional and vanishes when $k>\\dim M$. Thus the number of independent classes of closed forms modulo exact forms is purely topological, given by the Betti numbers.\n**Relation to the three de Rham nodes in pack 131**: $H^{0}$ counts connected components (`thm.de-rham-h0`), cohomology vanishes locally (`lem.poincare-lemma`), and combining open sets gives a long exact sequence (`thm.mayer-vietoris`).   'thm.open-mapping': {
    statement: {
      en: "Suppose $f$ is holomorphic and **nonconstant** on a domain $\\Omega$. Then $f$ is an open map: every open subset of $\\Omega$ has open image.\n\nCorollaries:\n\n1. **Another formulation of the maximum modulus principle**: $|f|$ cannot attain a maximum in the interior of $\\Omega$, since otherwise the image would fail to be open.\n2. A holomorphic **injection** is biholomorphic onto its image (its inverse is also holomorphic).\n3. If the image of $f$ is contained in an open set, then “$\\operatorname{Im} f$ has empty interior” is equivalent to “$f$ is constant.”",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#d4dde314'
    },
    notes: {
      en: "**Proof**: Take $z_{0} \\in \\Omega$ and $w_{0} := f(z_{0})$. It suffices to show that $w_{0}$ is an interior point of $f(\\Omega)$. By translation, we may assume $w_{0} = 0$.\n\nBy `prop.zero-isolated`, $z_{0}$ is an isolated zero of $f$ of order $m \\ge 1$. Thus in some neighborhood of $z_{0}$, the only zero of $f$ is $z_{0}$. Choose a small circle $|z - z_{0}| = \\rho$ on which $f$ does not vanish, and set $\\delta := \\min_{|z - z_{0}| = \\rho}|f(z)| > 0$.\n\nNow take any $|w| < \\delta$ and compare $f$ with the constant $-w$. On the circle, $|f(z)| \\ge \\delta > |w| = |{-w} - f(z) + f(z)|$… More directly, on the circle, $|f(z) - (-w)| \\ge |f(z)| - |w| > 0$. **Rouché's theorem** compares the number of zeros of $f$ and $-w$ inside the circle. When $|f| > |{-w} - f|$ holds on the circle, the two have the same number of zeros.\n\nInside the circle, $f$ has $m \\ge 1$ zeros counted with multiplicity, so $f + w$ also has a zero inside the circle, meaning that $w = -f(z)$ is attained.\n\nThus $B(0, \\delta) \\subseteq f(\\Omega)$, and $w_{0}$ is an interior point.\n\n∎\n\n⭐ **A more intuitive way to read the theorem**: A nonconstant holomorphic function cannot collapse an open set to a line or a point. The maximum modulus principle, Schwarz's lemma, and the implication univalent $\\implies$ biholomorphic are different expressions of this idea.\n\n⚠️ Nonconstancy is essential, since a constant function maps an open set to a point. ⚠️ The theorem requires **holomorphicity**. Continuous real-valued functions lack this property.\n\n**Hypotheses used in each direction** (the open mapping theorem is an analytic result, and the equivalence between constancy and empty interior is a corollary):\n- Being an open map $\\iff$ having open image for every open set follows in both directions by definition. The implication nonconstant and holomorphic $\\implies$ open uses isolated zeros and Rouché's theorem, comparing $f-f(z_0)$ with $f-f(z_0)-w$.\n- Constant $\\implies$ image with empty interior follows directly. Image with empty interior $\\implies$ constant follows by contraposition of the open mapping theorem and requires the domain to be connected. Here the image refers to the set $f(\\Omega)$ rather than the imaginary part of the function.\n- Open map $\\implies$ nonconstant follows because a constant map on a nonempty domain has a singleton image. Continuity alone is insufficient. For example, the image of $f(z)=\\operatorname{Re}z$ has empty interior in the complex plane.\n- Holomorphic injection $\\implies$ biholomorphism: The open mapping theorem first gives continuity of the inverse. The local zero-multiplicity argument then gives $f'\\ne0$, and the inverse function theorem gives holomorphicity of the inverse. This uses more than the definition of injectivity.\n- Without connectedness, global nonconstancy is insufficient. A function taking different constant values on two disjoint disks is holomorphic and nonconstant but fails to be open. The interior maximum modulus principle follows from openness of the image and the geometry of disks.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#6e80ef6d'
    },

  'thm.runge': {
    statement: {
      en: "Let $K \\subset \\mathbb{C}$ be compact, and let $f$ be holomorphic on a neighborhood of $K$. If $\\mathbb{C} \\setminus K$ is connected (that is, $K$ has no holes), then for each $\\varepsilon > 0$ there is a **polynomial** $p$ such that\n\n$$\\sup_{z \\in K} |f(z) - p(z)| < \\varepsilon.$$\n\nIn the general case (when $\\mathbb{C}\\setminus K$ has holes), $f$ can be approximated by **rational functions** whose poles are restricted to one point chosen in advance in each connected component of $\\mathbb{C}\\setminus K$.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#e557d52f'
    },
    notes: {
      en: "**Proof**: There are four steps. ① Express $f$ as a Cauchy integral over a grid boundary outside $K$. ② Discretize the integral to obtain rational functions with poles outside $K$. ③ Move the poles along paths in $\\mathbb{C}\\setminus K$ to the prescribed locations. ④ If there are no holes, move them out to infinity and expand to obtain polynomials.\n\n**Lemma A (Cauchy representation)**: Let $K$ be compact, let $V$ be an open neighborhood of $K$, and let $f$ be holomorphic on $V$. Then there is a finite union of closed squares $S$ with positively oriented boundary $\\Gamma = \\partial S$ such that $K \\subset \\operatorname{int} S,\\qquad S \\subset V,\\qquad f(z) = \\frac{1}{2\\pi i}\\int_{\\Gamma}\\frac{f(\\zeta)}{\\zeta - z}\\,\\mathrm{d}\\zeta\\quad(z\\in K).$. Proof: $d := \\operatorname{dist}(K,\\mathbb{C}\\setminus V) > 0$. Take a square grid of side length $h$, with $h\\sqrt{2} < d$. Let $S$ be the union of all closed grid squares that meet $K$. Then $K\\subset\\operatorname{int} S$, and the distance from $S$ to $K$ is at most $h\\sqrt{2} < d$, so $S\\subset V$. Orient each square boundary counterclockwise and add them. The integrals along shared edges cancel, giving $\\Gamma = \\partial S$. For $z\\in K\\subset\\operatorname{int} S$, the function $h(\\zeta) := (f(\\zeta)-f(z))/(\\zeta - z)$ (extended at $\\zeta = z$ by the value $f'(z)$) is holomorphic on a neighborhood of $S$. Applying Cauchy–Goursat (`thm.cauchy-goursat`) on each square gives $\\int_{\\partial Q} h = 0$, and summing gives $\\int_{\\Gamma} h = 0$. Hence $\\int_{\\Gamma}\\frac{f(\\zeta)}{\\zeta - z}\\,\\mathrm{d}\\zeta = f(z)\\int_{\\Gamma}\\frac{\\mathrm{d}\\zeta}{\\zeta - z} = 2\\pi i\\, f(z)\\,\\operatorname{Ind}_{\\Gamma}(z) = 2\\pi i\\,f(z),$. The last step uses the fact that the positively oriented boundary of a bounded region has index $1$ about each interior point (the index is constant on each connected component of $\\mathbb{C}\\setminus\\Gamma$ and equals $0$ on the unbounded component).\n\n**Step 1: Discretizing the integral.** $\\Gamma$ is compact and $\\Gamma\\cap K = \\emptyset$, so $g(\\zeta,z) := f(\\zeta)/(\\zeta - z)$ is continuous on $\\Gamma\\times K$ and hence uniformly continuous. Divide each edge of $\\Gamma$ into equal segments of length $<\\delta$. Take midpoints $\\zeta_{j}$ and increments $\\Delta\\zeta_{j}$, and form the Riemann sum $R(z) := \\frac{1}{2\\pi i}\\sum_{j}\\frac{f(\\zeta_{j})}{\\zeta_{j} - z}\\,\\Delta\\zeta_{j}.$. By uniform continuity, taking $\\delta$ sufficiently small ensures that $\\sup_{z\\in K}\\Bigl|\\frac{1}{2\\pi i}\\int_{\\Gamma} g(\\zeta,z)\\,\\mathrm{d}\\zeta - R(z)\\Bigr| < \\frac{\\varepsilon}{2}.$. Here $R$ is a rational function whose poles lie only at the midpoints $\\zeta_{j}\\in\\Gamma\\subset\\mathbb{C}\\setminus K$.\n\n**Step 2: Moving the poles.** Suppose $b,b^{\\prime}$ lie in the same component of $\\mathbb{C}\\setminus K$, denoted by $\\Omega$. Take a polygonal path in $\\Omega$ from $b$ to $b^{\\prime}$, and choose points $b = b_{0}, b_{1}, \\ldots, b_{N} = b^{\\prime}$ along it such that $|b_{i+1} - b_{i}| < \\operatorname{dist}(b_{i},K)$. For $z\\in K$, we have $|b_{i}-z| \\ge \\operatorname{dist}(b_{i},K) > |b_{i+1}-b_{i}|$, so the geometric series $\\frac{1}{b_{i+1} - z} = \\frac{1}{b_{i} - z}\\cdot\\frac{1}{1 - \\frac{b_{i}-b_{i+1}}{b_{i}-z}} = \\sum_{k=0}^{\\infty}\\frac{(b_{i}-b_{i+1})^{k}}{(b_{i}-z)^{k+1}}$ converges uniformly on $K$ (with common ratio $\\le q_{i} < 1$). Truncating at $k\\le N_{i}$ uniformly approximates $1/(b_{i+1}-z)$ to arbitrary accuracy by a rational function with poles only at $b_{i}$. Repeating this step shows that $1/(b-z)$ can be uniformly approximated by rational functions with poles only at $b^{\\prime}$. Taking linear combinations of these basic terms gives uniform approximation of any rational function on $K$, moving its poles one at a time.\n\n**Step 3: Obtaining polynomials when there are no holes.** If $\\mathbb{C}\\setminus K$ is connected, it is an unbounded connected open set. Choose $b^{\\prime}$ such that $|b^{\\prime}| > 2\\max_{z\\in K}|z|$. For $z\\in K$, we have $|z/b^{\\prime}| \\le 1/2$, so $\\frac{1}{b^{\\prime} - z} = \\frac{1}{b^{\\prime}}\\sum_{k=0}^{\\infty}\\Bigl(\\frac{z}{b^{\\prime}}\\Bigr)^{k}$ converges uniformly on $K$, and its truncations are polynomials. Thus $f$ is uniformly approximated on $K$ by polynomials to within $\\varepsilon$.\n\n**General case**: In $\\mathbb{C}\\setminus K$, choose in advance, for each connected component $\\Omega$, one point $b_{\\Omega}$. Step 1 gives poles in $\\Gamma$ for a rational function $R$. Since $\\Gamma\\subset\\mathbb{C}\\setminus K$, each pole lies in some component $\\Omega$, and Step 2 moves it to $b_{\\Omega}$. There are finitely many poles and the error in each step can be made arbitrarily small, so $R$ is uniformly approximated by a rational function with poles at $\\{b_{\\Omega}\\}$ to within $\\varepsilon/2$. Combining this with the error $\\varepsilon/2$ from Step 1 proves the result. ∎\n\n⭐ **Runge's theorem, Mittag-Leffler's theorem, and the Weierstrass product theorem (`thm.weierstrass-product`) belong together**: Runge concerns approximation, Mittag-Leffler constructs functions with prescribed principal parts, and Weierstrass constructs functions with prescribed zeros. All three answer the same question: **What kinds of holomorphic or meromorphic functions can be constructed on a domain?**\n\n⚠️ Connectedness of $\\mathbb{C}\\setminus K$ is essential. When $K = $ is the unit circle, $1/z$ is holomorphic near the circle, but cannot be uniformly approximated by polynomials on $K$. Otherwise the maximum modulus principle would imply that $1/z$ extends holomorphically to $\\{|z|\\le 1\\}$, contradicting the singularity at $z=0$.\n\n⚠️ The graph has no separate nodes on path integrals as Riemann sums or on the index (winding number) being 1 for a positively oriented boundary. Lemma A therefore spells out these points. `thm.cauchy-goursat` is already in the graph.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#64243440'

  'thm.weierstrass-product': {
    statement: {
      en: "Let $\\{a_{n}\\}$ be a sequence of points in $\\mathbb{C}$ tending to $\\infty$ (repetitions are allowed, and the number of occurrences gives the multiplicity of the zero). Then there exists an entire function $f$ whose zero set is **exactly** $\\{a_{n}\\}$ and which can be written as\n\n$$f(z) = z^{m} e^{g(z)} \\prod_{n=1}^{\\infty} E_{p_{n}}\\left(\\frac{z}{a_{n}}\\right),$$\n\nHere $m$ is the multiplicity of $0$ as a zero, and $p_{n}$ are suitably chosen nonnegative integers that ensure uniform convergence of the product on compact subsets.\n\nThus **the zeros of an entire function, together with their multiplicities, can be prescribed arbitrarily**. The only remaining freedom is a zero-free entire factor $e^{g}$.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#494a9f40'

    notes: {
      en: "**Proof.** First remove the terms of the sequence equal to $0$ (they contribute only the factor $z^{m}$). Continue to denote the remaining sequence by $a_{n}\\ne 0$ and take the elementary factors to be $E_{p}(z) = (1-z)\\exp\\Bigl(z + \\frac{z^{2}}{2} + \\cdots + \\frac{z^{p}}{p}\\Bigr),\\qquad E_{0}(z) = 1-z .$.\n\n**Lemma 1 (estimate for elementary factors).** If $|z|\\le \\frac{1}{2}$, then $|1 - E_{p}(z)| \\le 4|z|^{p+1}$. To prove this, observe that when $|z|<1$, we have $\\log E_{p}(z) = -\\sum_{k>p} z^{k}/k$, so $|\\log E_{p}(z)| \\le \\sum_{k>p}\\frac{|z|^{k}}{k} \\le \\frac{|z|^{p+1}}{(p+1)(1-|z|)} \\le 2|z|^{p+1};$. Also, $|1-e^{w}|\\le 2|w|$ ($|w|\\le 1$). Taking $w = \\log E_{p}(z)$ gives the result (when $p=0$, we obtain $1-E_{0}(z) = z$ directly).\n\n**Lemma 2 (convergence of the product).** If $u_{n}$ are holomorphic on a domain and $\\sum_{n}|u_{n}|$ converges uniformly on every compact subset, then $\\prod_{n}(1+u_{n})$ converges uniformly on every compact subset to a holomorphic function $F$, and the zeros of $F$ are exactly the zeros of the factors $1+u_{n}$, with multiplicities added. The partial products are uniformly Cauchy by the telescoping estimate $|\\prod_{n\\le N}-\\prod_{n\\le M}| \\le \\prod(1+|u_{n}|)\\bigl(e^{\\sum_{n>\\min}|u_{n}|}-1\\bigr)$, and the limit is holomorphic by `thm.weierstrass-convergence`. At a point where no factor vanishes, eventually $|u_{n}|\\le 1/2$, hence $|1+u_{n}|\\ge 1/2$, so the product is nonzero.\n\n**Step 1. Choose $p_{n}$ so that the product converges.** Take $p_{n} = n$. Given any $R>0$, since $a_{n}\\to\\infty$, there exists $N$ such that whenever $n\\ge N$, we have $|a_{n}|\\ge 2R$; then for $|z|\\le R$ and $n\\ge N$, we have $|z/a_{n}|\\le 1/2$. By Lemma 1, $\\bigl|1 - E_{n}(z/a_{n})\\bigr| \\le 4\\Bigl(\\frac{R}{|a_{n}|}\\Bigr)^{n+1} \\le 4\\cdot 2^{-(n+1)},$, so $\\sum_{n}\\bigl|1 - E_{n}(z/a_{n})\\bigr|$ converges uniformly on $|z|\\le R$. By Lemma 2, $F(z) := \\prod_{n=1}^{\\infty} E_{n}(z/a_{n})$ is entire, its zeros are exactly the points $a_{n}$, and their multiplicities equal the multiplicities of $a_{n}$ in the sequence.\n\n**Step 2. Restore $z^{m}$.** Set $f_{0}(z) := z^{m}F(z)$. Then $f_{0}$ is entire with zeros exactly $\\{a_{n}\\}$, counting multiplicities. The multiplicity at $0$ is $m$, and the other zeros are given by $F$.\n\n**Step 3. Remove the zero-free factor.** Since $F$ is nowhere zero, $F'/F$ is entire. Set $g_{0}(z) := \\int_{0}^{1}\\frac{F'(tz)}{F(tz)}\\,z\\,\\mathrm{d}t .$. The integrand is continuous, $g_{0}$ is entire, and $g_{0}'(z) = F'(z)/F(z)$ (differentiate under the integral sign, or use $g_{0}(z) = \\int_{0}^{z}F'(\\zeta)/F(\\zeta)\\,\\mathrm{d}\\zeta$ along a line segment, with path independence ensured by Cauchy's theorem). Thus $(F e^{-g_{0}})' = 0$, so $F = C e^{g_{0}}$ and $C\\ne 0$. Writing $C = e^{c}$ and $g := g_{0} + c$ gives $F = e^{g}$. Combining these gives the desired $f_{0}(z) = z^{m}e^{g(z)}\\prod_{n=1}^{\\infty}E_{n}(z/a_{n}),$. ∎\n\n⭐ **This is the factorization “entire function = zeros $\\times$ zero-free factor.”** In comparison, Weierstrass handles **zeros** multiplicatively, while Mittag-Leffler handles **principal parts and poles** additively.\n\n⚠️ The product representation **is not unique** ($p_{n}$ can be chosen larger), so the theorem asserts existence rather than uniqueness.\n\n⚠️ The node `def.elementary-factor` contains a stronger estimate for elementary factors. Hurwitz's theorem has no separate node, so Lemma 2 is given here in full.",
      status: 'draft',

  'thm.hadamard-factorization': {
    statement: {
      en: "Let $f$ be an entire function of **finite order** $\\rho$ with zeros $\\{a_{n}\\}$, counted with multiplicity. Then\n\n$$f(z) = z^{m} e^{P(z)} \\prod_{n} E_{p}\\left(\\frac{z}{a_{n}}\\right),$$\n\nwhere $P$ is a polynomial of degree $\\le \\rho$ and $p \\le \\rho$ is a **single** fixed integer used for all factors (the Weierstrass construction chooses it separately for each factor).\n\nIn particular, when $\\rho < 1$, we have $p = 0$, hence $\\sum |a_{n}|^{-1} < \\infty$ (there may still be infinitely many zeros. For example, $\\prod_{n}(1 - z/n^{2})$ has order $\\frac{1}{2}$ and infinitely many zeros).",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#fde82eb2'
    },
    notes: {
      en: "**Notation.** Let $f$ be entire of finite order $\\rho$, with zeros $a_{n}\\ne 0$ counted with multiplicity. The zero at $z=0$ has multiplicity $m$. Let $n(r)$ count the zeros with modulus $\\le r$, and let $M(r)=\\max_{|z|=r}|f(z)|$ and $E_{p}(w)=(1-w)e^{w+w^{2}/2+\\cdots+w^{p}/p}$. Take $p=\\lfloor\\rho\\rfloor$ (when $\\rho<1$, we have $p=0$), so $p+1>\\rho$. Fix $\\varepsilon>0$ such that $p+1>\\rho+\\varepsilon$.\n\n**Step 1. Density of zeros.** Apply Jensen's formula (`prop.jensen`) to $f/z^{m}$, whose value at the origin is nonzero, to obtain $n(R)\\log 2\\le\\log M(2R)-\\log|f(0)|+O(1)$. Finite order gives $\\log M(2R)\\le C\\,R^{\\rho+\\varepsilon}$, hence $n(R)\\le C\\,R^{\\rho+\\varepsilon}$. Thus for any $\\sigma>\\rho$, summation by parts gives $\\sum_{n}|a_{n}|^{-\\sigma}=\\sigma\\int_{0}^{\\infty}n(r)\\,r^{-\\sigma-1}\\,\\mathrm{d}r<\\infty$ ($n(r)$, when $r<|a_{1}|$, equals $0$, so there is no boundary term). In particular, $\\sum_{n}|a_{n}|^{-(p+1)}<\\infty$.\n\n**Step 2. Convergence of the product.** Write $\\mathcal{P}(z):=\\prod_{n}E_{p}(z/a_{n})$. For $|w|\\le1/2$, we have $\\log E_{p}(w)=-\\sum_{k>p}w^{k}/k$, hence $|E_{p}(w)-1|\\le C_{p}|w|^{p+1}$. Given $R$, there are only finitely many $|a_{n}|\\le2R$. For the remaining terms, $|z/a_{n}|\\le1/2$, so $\\sum_{|a_{n}|>2R}|z/a_{n}|^{p+1}\\le R^{p+1}\\sum_{n}|a_{n}|^{-(p+1)}<\\infty$. Therefore $\\mathcal{P}$ converges uniformly on $|z|\\le R$ and defines an entire function.\n\n**Step 3. The quotient is zero-free.** Set $\\Phi(z):=f(z)\\big/\\bigl(z^{m}\\mathcal{P}(z)\\bigr)$. At each $a_{n}$, $E_{p}(z/a_{n})$ has a simple zero that cancels a zero of $f$. At all other points, $f\\ne0$ and the product is nonzero. Thus $\\Phi$ is entire and nowhere zero.\n\n**Step 4. The order of $\\Phi$ is $\\le\\rho$.** Let $h$ be an entire logarithm of $\\Phi$. Its construction in Step 5 uses only that $\\Phi$ is nowhere zero, so we may use it here independently of the estimates in this step. Then $\\operatorname{Re}h=\\log|\\Phi|$. It suffices to show that for every sufficiently large $r$ there is an $R\\in[r,2r]$ such that on $|z|=R$ we have $\\log|\\Phi(z)|\\le C\\,r^{\\rho+\\varepsilon}$. The maximum principle ($\\operatorname{Re}h$ is harmonic) and Borel–Carathéodory from Step 6 then give $|h|\\le C\\,R^{\\rho+\\varepsilon}$, and Cauchy's estimates show that $h$ is a polynomial.\n\n**(i) Two elementary estimates for $E_{p}$.** For $|w|\\le1/2$, we have $\\log|E_{p}(w)|=\\operatorname{Re}\\bigl(-\\sum_{k>p}w^{k}/k\\bigr)$, hence $|\\log|E_{p}(w)||\\le2|w|^{p+1}$. For $|w|\\ge1/2$, we have $\\log|E_{p}(w)|=\\log|1-w|+\\operatorname{Re}\\sum_{j\\le p}w^{j}/j\\ge\\log|1-w|-C_{p}|w|^{p}$.\n\n**Step 4 (continued). The good-radius lemma.** Take $M:=\\lceil\\rho\\rceil+3$ and $\\delta:=r^{-M}$. The number of zeros in $[r/2,4r]$ is $N\\le n(4r)\\le C\\,r^{\\rho+\\varepsilon}$. Each such zero excludes an interval of radii of length $2\\delta$. Since $2\\delta N\\le2C\\,r^{\\rho+\\varepsilon-M}<r$ for large $r$, there exists $R\\in[r,2r]$ such that every $|a_{n}|\\in[r/2,4r]$ satisfies $\\bigl||a_{n}|-R\\bigr|\\ge\\delta$. Fix such an $R$.\n\nFor $|z|=R$, split the terms according to $|a_{n}|\\le4r$ and $|a_{n}|>4r$ in $\\log|\\mathcal{P}(z)|=\\sum\\log|E_{p}(z/a_{n})|$.\n\n• For $|a_{n}|>4r$, we have $|z/a_{n}|<1/2$, and by (i) each term satisfies $\\ge-C(R/|a_{n}|)^{p+1}$. Summation by parts and $n(t)\\le C t^{\\rho+\\varepsilon}$ give $\\sum_{|a_{n}|>4r}(R/|a_{n}|)^{p+1}\\le C\\,r^{\\rho+\\varepsilon}$.\n\n• For $|a_{n}|\\le4r$, we have $|z-a_{n}|\\ge\\delta$ (when $|a_{n}|\\in[r/2,4r]$, this follows from the choice of $R$, and when $|a_{n}|<r/2$, we have $|z-a_{n}|\\ge R-r/2\\ge r/2$). Thus $\\log|1-z/a_{n}|=\\log|z-a_{n}|-\\log|a_{n}|\\ge\\log(\\delta/(4r))$, and by (i) each term satisfies $\\ge\\log(\\delta/(4r))-C(R/|a_{n}|)^{p}$. Summing over at most $N\\le C r^{\\rho+\\varepsilon}$ terms and using $\\sum_{|a_{n}|\\le4r}(R/|a_{n}|)^{p}\\le C r^{\\rho+\\varepsilon}$ (by summation by parts) gives $\\sum_{|a_{n}|\\le4r}\\log|E_{p}(z/a_{n})|\\ge-C\\,r^{\\rho+\\varepsilon}\\log r$.\n\nCombining these gives $\\log|\\mathcal{P}(z)|\\ge-C\\,r^{\\rho+\\varepsilon}\\log r$. Thus $\\log|\\Phi(z)|=\\log|f(z)|-m\\log R-\\log|\\mathcal{P}(z)|\\le C\\,r^{\\rho+\\varepsilon}+C\\,r^{\\rho+\\varepsilon}\\log r\\le C_{1}\\,r^{\\rho+\\varepsilon_{1}}$ for any $\\varepsilon_{1}>\\varepsilon$.\n\n**Step 5. Zero-free $\\implies$ exponential.** Since $\\Phi$ is entire and zero-free and $\\mathbb{C}$ is simply connected, `prop.single-valued-branch` gives an entire function $h$ such that $\\Phi=e^{h}$. Also, $\\operatorname{Re}h=\\log|\\Phi|$.\n\n**Step 6. Borel–Carathéodory.** The lemma states that if $g$ is holomorphic on $|z|\\le R$, $g(0)=0$, and $\\operatorname{Re}g\\le A$ ($A>0$), then for $|z|=r<R$ we have $|g(z)|\\le2Ar/(R-r)$. For the proof, $\\varphi:=g/(2A-g)$ is holomorphic on $|z|\\le R$ (the real part of the denominator is $\\ge A$), and $|\\varphi|\\le1$ ($|g|^{2}\\le(2A-\\operatorname{Re}g)^{2}+(\\operatorname{Im}g)^{2}$ is equivalent to $\\operatorname{Re}g\\le A$), with $\\varphi(0)=0$. Schwarz's lemma (`lem.schwarz`) gives $|\\varphi(z)|\\le r/R$, hence $|g|=\\bigl|2A\\varphi/(1+\\varphi)\\bigr|\\le2A|\\varphi|/(1-|\\varphi|)\\le2Ar/(R-r)$.\n\n**Step 7. $h$ is a polynomial.** By Step 4, on $|z|\\le R$ we have $\\operatorname{Re}(h-h(0))\\le C_{1}R^{\\rho+\\varepsilon_{1}}+|\\operatorname{Re}h(0)|=:A$. Apply Step 6 to $g=h-h(0)$ with $r=R/2$ to obtain $|h(z)-h(0)|\\le2A$ ($|z|\\le R/2$). Cauchy's estimates (`thm.cauchy-inequality`) then give, for $k>\\rho+\\varepsilon_{1}$, the bound $|h^{(k)}(0)|\\le k!\\,2A/(R/2)^{k}\\to0$ ($R\\to\\infty$). Hence $h^{(k)}(0)=0$, so $h$ is a polynomial of degree $\\le\\lfloor\\rho\\rfloor$.\n\n**Conclusion.** $f=z^{m}e^{h}\\prod_{n}E_{p}(z/a_{n})=z^{m}e^{P}\\prod_{n}E_{p}(z/a_{n})$ gives the Hadamard factorization. ∎ Steps 1–3 establish convergence and cancel the zeros. Step 4 uses the good-radius lemma, elementary estimates, and Borel–Carathéodory. Steps 5–7 express the zero-free quotient as the exponential of a polynomial.\n\n⭐ **This is a quantitative version of the Weierstrass product.** Knowing the order of growth lets us use the same $p$ for every factor and reduces the remaining freedom to a polynomial $P$.   'lem.average-continuous': {
    statement: {
      en: "Suppose $f \\in L^1_{loc}$. Then $A_r f(x)$ is **jointly continuous** as a function of $(r, x)$.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#370b8ea4'

    notes: {
      en: "**Proof.** Let $m$ denote Lebesgue measure and set $\\omega_n := m(B(0,1))$. Then $m(B(x,r)) = \\omega_n r^n$. To prove joint continuity, it suffices to show that for every sequence satisfying $r_k > 0$ and $(x_k, r_k) \\to (x_0, r_0)$, we have $A_{r_k} f(x_k) \\to A_{r_0} f(x_0)$.\n\n**Step 1. Split the difference.** Write the difference as\n\n$$A_r f(x) - A_{r_0} f(x_0) = \\frac{1}{m(B(x,r))}\\int_{\\mathbb{R}^n} \\bigl(\\chi_{B(x,r)} - \\chi_{B(x_0,r_0)}\\bigr) f + \\Bigl(\\frac{1}{m(B(x,r))} - \\frac{1}{m(B(x_0,r_0))}\\Bigr)\\int_{B(x_0,r_0)} f .$$\n\nThe second term tends to $0$. Indeed, $\\omega_n r_k^n \\to \\omega_n r_0^n > 0$, so the coefficient tends to $0$, while $\\int_{B(x_0,r_0)} f$ is constant.\n\n**Step 2. Find a dominating function.** Choose $\\delta = 1/2$. If $|x - x_0| < 1/2$ and $|r - r_0| < 1/2$, then $r < r_0 + 1/2$, and $|y - x| < r$ implies\n\n$$|y - x_0| \\le |y - x| + |x - x_0| < r + 1/2 < r_0 + 1,$$\n\nthat is, $B(x,r) \\subseteq B(x_0, r_0 + 1)$. Thus for such $(x,r)$ and every $y$,\n\n$$|\\chi_{B(x,r)}(y) - \\chi_{B(x_0,r_0)}(y)|\\,|f(y)| \\le \\chi_{B(x_0, r_0+1)}(y)\\,|f(y)| \\in L^1.$$\n\n**Step 3. Almost-everywhere pointwise convergence.** The sphere $\\partial B(x_0, r_0)$ has measure zero. Suppose $y \\notin \\partial B(x_0, r_0)$.\n\n- If $|y - x_0| < r_0$, choose $\\varepsilon = r_0 - |y - x_0| > 0$. When $|x - x_0| < \\varepsilon/2$ and $|r - r_0| < \\varepsilon/2$, we have $|y - x| \\le |y - x_0| + |x - x_0| < r_0 - \\varepsilon/2 < r$, hence $\\chi_{B(x,r)}(y) = 1 = \\chi_{B(x_0,r_0)}(y)$.\n\n- If $|y - x_0| > r_0$, choose $\\varepsilon = |y - x_0| - r_0 > 0$. For the same small perturbations, $|y - x| > r_0 + \\varepsilon/2 > r$, hence $\\chi_{B(x,r)}(y) = 0 = \\chi_{B(x_0,r_0)}(y)$.\n\nThus $\\chi_{B(x_k,r_k)} \\to \\chi_{B(x_0,r_0)}$ holds for almost every $y$.\n\n**Step 4. Dominated convergence.** By Steps 2 and 3 and the dominated convergence theorem, $\\int (\\chi_{B(x_k,r_k)} - \\chi_{B(x_0,r_0)}) f \\to 0$. The coefficient $1/m(B(x_k,r_k))$ is bounded and tends to $1/m(B(x_0,r_0))$, so the first term tends to $0$. Adding the second term gives $A_{r_k} f(x_k) \\to A_{r_0} f(x_0)$. ∎",

  'thm.sphere-homology': {
    statement: {
      en: "For $n \\ge 1$,\n\n$$H_{0}(S^{n}) = \\mathbb{Z},\\qquad H_{n}(S^{n}) = \\mathbb{Z},$$\n$$H_{p}(S^{n}) = 0 \\quad (p \\ne 0, n).$$\n\nUsing **reduced** homology, we can write a single formula for all $p$ (including $n = 0$)\n\n$$\\tilde{H}_{p}(S^{n}) = \\mathbb{Z} \\ (p = n),\\qquad \\tilde{H}_{p}(S^{n}) = 0 \\ (p \\ne n).$$",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#c27fe014'

    notes: {
      en: "**Proof.** Induct on $n$ using reduced homology (`def.reduced-homology`) and the reduced Mayer–Vietoris sequence (`thm.mv-homology`). Set $S^{n} \\subset \\mathbb{R}^{n+1}$, with north pole $N := (0, \\ldots, 0, 1)$ and south pole $S := -N$.\n\n**Base case $n = 0$.** The space $S^{0} = \\{\\pm 1\\}$ consists of two points. By `prop.h0-components`, $H_{0}(S^{0}) = \\mathbb{Z}^{2}$, hence $\\tilde{H}_{0}(S^{0}) = \\mathbb{Z}$. When $p > 0$, we have $\\tilde{H}_{p}(S^{0}) = 0$.\n\n**Inductive step.** Suppose $n \\ge 1$ and set $U := S^{n} \\setminus \\{S\\}$ and $V := S^{n} \\setminus \\{N\\}$. Both are open.\n\n**$U, V$ is contractible.** For $U$, use the homotopy\n\n$$H_{s}(x) := \\frac{(1-s)x + sN}{\\lVert (1-s)x + sN \\rVert}\\qquad (x \\in U,\\ s \\in [0,1]).$$\n\nThe denominator is nonzero. Indeed, $(1-s)x + sN = 0$ forces $\\lVert x \\rVert = s/(1-s) = 1$, that is, $x = S$ (at the endpoint $s = 1$, the numerator is $N \\ne 0$), whereas $S \\notin U$. Thus $H_{0} = \\mathrm{id}_{U}$ and $H_{1} \\equiv N$, so $U$ is contractible. Similarly, $V$ is contractible. By homotopy invariance (`thm.homotopy-invariance`), all reduced homology groups of a contractible space vanish, so $\\tilde{H}_{p}(U) = \\tilde{H}_{p}(V) = 0$ for every $p$.\n\n**The intersection deformation retracts onto the equator.** We have $U \\cap V = S^{n} \\setminus \\{N, S\\}$. Write a point as $(x, t)$, where $x \\in \\mathbb{R}^{n}$, $t \\in (-1, 1)$, and $\\lVert x \\rVert^{2} + t^{2} = 1$. Since $t \\ne \\pm 1$, we have $x \\ne 0$. Define\n\n$$H_{s}(x, t) := \\frac{(x,\\, s t)}{\\sqrt{\\lVert x \\rVert^{2} + s^{2} t^{2}}}\\qquad (s \\in [0,1]).$$\n\nThis map is continuous and takes values in $S^{n}$. The point $H_{0}(x, t) = (x/\\lVert x \\rVert, 0)$ lies on the equator $S^{n-1}$, we have $H_{1} = \\mathrm{id}$, and $H_{s}$ maps the equator into itself. Thus $S^{n-1}$ is a deformation retract of $U \\cap V$, so $\\tilde{H}_{p}(U \\cap V) \\cong \\tilde{H}_{p}(S^{n-1})$ by homotopy invariance.\n\n**Reduced Mayer–Vietoris.** Since $U \\cup V = S^{n}$ and $U \\cap V \\ne \\emptyset$, we have the long exact sequence\n\n$$\\cdots \\to \\tilde{H}_{p}(U \\cap V) \\to \\tilde{H}_{p}(U) \\oplus \\tilde{H}_{p}(V) \\to \\tilde{H}_{p}(S^{n}) \\to \\tilde{H}_{p-1}(U \\cap V) \\to \\cdots$$\n\nThe middle terms all vanish, so for every $p \\ge 1$ we have $\\tilde{H}_{p}(S^{n}) \\cong \\tilde{H}_{p-1}(S^{n-1})$. For $p = 0$, the end of the sequence $\\tilde{H}_{0}(U) \\oplus \\tilde{H}_{0}(V) \\to \\tilde{H}_{0}(S^{n}) \\to 0$ gives $\\tilde{H}_{0}(S^{n}) = 0$ (when $n \\ge 1$, the space $S^{n}$ is path connected).\n\n**Conclusion of the induction.** The base case and the recurrence give $\\tilde{H}_{p}(S^{n}) = \\mathbb{Z}$ when $p = n$, and $0$ otherwise.\n\n**Return to unreduced homology.** When $n \\ge 1$, the space $S^{n}$ is path connected, so $H_{0}(S^{n}) = \\mathbb{Z}$ (that is, $\\tilde{H}_{0} = 0$). When $p \\ge 1$, we have $H_{p} = \\tilde{H}_{p}$. Thus $H_{0}(S^{n}) = \\mathbb{Z}$ and $H_{n}(S^{n}) = \\mathbb{Z}$, with $H_{p}(S^{n}) = 0$ in all other cases. ∎\n\n⭐ **Interpretation.** This makes precise the statement that homology detects a hole of dimension $n$. Compare `cor.pi1-sphere` ($\\pi_{1}(S^{n}) = 1$ for $n \\ge 2$). The group $\\pi_{1}$ detects only degree $n = 1$, while $H_{n}$ detects every degree ($\\pi_{n}$ can also detect these holes, through a different theory).",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#16d39143'

  'thm.long-exact-pair': {
    statement: {
      en: "A **topological pair** is a pair of spaces $A \\subseteq X$ with one a subspace of the other. Since every singular simplex in $A$ is also a singular simplex in $X$, $S_{*}(A) \\subseteq S_{*}(X)$ is a **subcomplex**. Define the **relative chain groups** and **relative homology groups** by\n\n$$S_{p}(X, A) := S_{p}(X) \\big/ S_{p}(A),$$\n$$H_{p}(X, A) := \\ker\\bar\\partial \\big/ \\operatorname{im}\\bar\\partial.$$\n\nThen there is a **long exact sequence** (at each $\\cdot$, the exactness condition is $\\operatorname{im} = \\ker$)\n\n$$\\cdots \\to H_{p}(A) \\to H_{p}(X) \\to H_{p}(X, A)$$\n$$\\xrightarrow{\\ \\partial_{*}\\ } H_{p-1}(A) \\to \\cdots$$\n\nwhere $\\partial_{*}$ is called the **connecting homomorphism**. The sequence holds for every pair of spaces. It **requires no assumption** that $A$ is closed, open, or otherwise well behaved, and no cofibration hypothesis is needed.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#76a24ce5'
    },
    notes: {
      en: "**Proof.** Let $i : S_{*}(A) \\hookrightarrow S_{*}(X)$ denote the inclusion and $q : S_{*}(X) \\twoheadrightarrow S_{*}(X, A) := S_{*}(X)/S_{*}(A)$ the quotient map. Both are chain maps, and for each $p$,\n\n$$0 \\to S_{p}(A) \\xrightarrow{\\ i\\ } S_{p}(X) \\xrightarrow{\\ q\\ } S_{p}(X, A) \\to 0$$\n\nis a short exact sequence. The long exact sequence in homology follows from this short exact sequence. We give the connecting homomorphism and verify exactness at all three positions below.\n\n**Connecting homomorphism.** Take $[c] \\in H_{p}(X, A)$, where $c \\in S_{p}(X)$ and $\\bar\\partial[c] = 0$, that is, $\\partial c \\in S_{p-1}(A)$. Set $\\partial_{*}[c] := [\\partial c] \\in H_{p-1}(A)$.\n\nWell-definedness follows from $\\partial\\partial c = 0$, so $\\partial c$ is a cycle in $A$. If $c^{\\prime} - c = \\partial d + a$ ($d \\in S_{p+1}(X)$, $a \\in S_{p}(A)$), then $\\partial c^{\\prime} - \\partial c = \\partial a$ is a boundary in $A$, so the homology class is unchanged.\n\n**Exactness.** Let $j_{*} : H_{p}(A) \\to H_{p}(X)$ and $q_{*} : H_{p}(X) \\to H_{p}(X, A)$ denote the induced maps.\n\n**(a) $\\operatorname{im} j_{*} = \\ker q_{*}$.** If $[c] = j_{*}[a]$ (where $a$ is a cycle in $A$), then $q_{*}[c] = [q(a)] = 0$, since $a \\in S_{*}(A)$ is $0$ in the quotient complex. Conversely, suppose $q_{*}[c] = 0$, so $q(c) = \\partial b$ for some $b \\in S_{p+1}(X)$. Then $q(c - \\partial b) = 0$, so $c - \\partial b \\in S_{p}(A)$ and $\\partial(c - \\partial b) = 0$, hence $[c] = j_{*}[c - \\partial b]$.\n\n**(b) $\\operatorname{im} q_{*} = \\ker \\partial_{*}$.** If $[c] = q_{*}[z]$ and $z$ is a cycle in $X$, then $\\partial c = \\partial z = 0$, so $\\partial_{*}[c] = 0$. Conversely, suppose $\\partial_{*}[c] = 0$, so $\\partial c = \\partial a$ for some $a \\in S_{p-1}(A)$. Set $z := c - a$. Then $\\partial z = 0$ and $q(z) = q(c)$, so $[c] = q_{*}[z]$.\n\n**(c) $\\operatorname{im} \\partial_{*} = \\ker j_{*}$.** If $[c]$ comes from relative homology, then $j_{*}(\\partial_{*}[c]) = [\\partial c]$, and $\\partial c$ is a boundary in $X$, so its class is $0$. Conversely, if $a \\in S_{p-1}(A)$ is a cycle and $j_{*}[a] = 0$, so $a = \\partial b$ for some $b \\in S_{p}(X)$, then $b$ is a relative cycle ($\\partial b = a \\in S_{*}(A)$), and $\\partial_{*}[b] = [\\partial b] = [a]$.\n\nTogether, these three exactness checks give the long exact sequence. The argument uses only that $S_{*}(A) \\subseteq S_{*}(X)$ is a subcomplex, with no closedness, openness, or other assumptions on $A$. ∎\n\n**Why this is simpler in homology than in homotopy.** The long exact sequence of **relative** homotopy groups holds for every pair $(X, A)$ and requires no fibration. The delicate step is replacing the relative groups by the absolute groups of the quotient space $X/A$; it requires a good pair hypothesis (NDR pair or cofibration) on $(X, A)$. The homology sequence applies whenever $A \\subseteq X$ is a subspace. This is one of the main reasons chain complexes are easier to work with than homotopy groups.\n\n💡 Application. Break the homology of a large space into a smaller space $+$ a relative part, and use the known groups to compute the unknown ones (`thm.mv-homology` is obtained this way).",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#4c9e3130'
    },

  'thm.mv-homology': {
    statement: {
      en: "Suppose $X = U \\cup V$ and $U, V \\subseteq X$ are **open sets** (more generally, the interiors of $U, V$ cover $X$). Let $i_{*}, j_{*}$ denote the maps induced by the inclusions of $U \\cap V \\hookrightarrow U, V$. Then there is a long exact sequence\n\n$$H_{p}(U \\cap V) \\to H_{p}(U) \\oplus H_{p}(V) \\to H_{p}(X)$$\n$$\\xrightarrow{\\ \\partial_{*}\\ } H_{p-1}(U \\cap V)$$\n\n(extending in both directions to give $\\cdots$). The two maps that require explanation are\n\n$$\\Phi[z] := (i_{*}[z],\\ -j_{*}[z]),\\qquad \\Psi[a, b] := [a + b].$$\n\nOn replacing $H$ by reduced homology $\\tilde{H}$, the same sequence holds provided $U \\cap V \\ne \\emptyset$ (otherwise exactness fails in the segment $\\tilde{H}_{0}$. When $X = \\{a, b\\}$, $U = \\{a\\}$, and $V = \\{b\\}$, we have $\\tilde{H}_{0}(U) = \\tilde{H}_{0}(V) = 0$ while $\\tilde{H}_{0}(X) = \\mathbb{Z}$).",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#e4e652a1'
    },
    notes: {
      en: "**Proof.** There are five steps. ① Take the chains whose simplices each lie entirely in $U$ or $V$ to obtain a subcomplex. ② Use barycentric subdivision to prove that the inclusion of this subcomplex into the full complex induces an isomorphism in homology (this is the main difficulty). ③ Express the subcomplex as the sum of the chains in $U$ and the chains in $V$. ④ Obtain a long exact sequence from the short exact sequence. ⑤ Substitute the isomorphism from step ②.\n\n**Small chains.** A singular simplex $\\sigma : \\Delta^{p} \\to X$ is **small** with respect to the cover $\\{U, V\\}$ if $\\sigma(\\Delta^{p}) \\subseteq U$ or $\\sigma(\\Delta^{p}) \\subseteq V$. Finite sums of small simplices form a subgroup $S^{\\mathcal{U}}_{p}(X) \\subseteq S_{p}(X)$. The boundary operator preserves smallness because the faces still lie in the same set, so $S^{\\mathcal{U}}_{*}(X)$ is a subcomplex. Write $\\iota : S^{\\mathcal{U}}_{*}(X) \\hookrightarrow S_{*}(X)$.\n\n**Step 1. The subdivision chain map.** On the standard simplex $\\Delta^{p}$, define two chains recursively:\n\n$$\\operatorname{sd}_{0} := [v_{0}],\\qquad \\operatorname{sd}_{p} := [b_{p},\\ \\operatorname{sd}_{p-1}(\\partial\\Delta^{p})],$$\n\nHere $b_{p}$ is the barycenter of $\\Delta^{p}$, $[b, c]$ denotes coning with apex $b$ over the chain $c$, and $\\partial\\Delta^{p} = \\sum_{i}(-1)^{i}\\Delta^{p}_{i}$ is the standard boundary. The cone formula $\\partial[b, c] = c - [b, \\partial c]$ gives\n\n$$\\partial\\operatorname{sd}_{p} = \\operatorname{sd}_{p-1}(\\partial\\Delta^{p}).$$\n\nNext define $t_{0} := 0$ and the recursion\n\n$$c_{p} := \\operatorname{sd}_{p} - \\operatorname{id}_{p} - t_{p-1}(\\partial\\Delta^{p}),\\qquad t_{p} := [b_{p},\\ c_{p}],$$\n\nwhere $\\operatorname{id}_{p}$ is the identity $p$-simplex. Induction on $p$ gives $\\partial c_{p} = 0$ using $\\partial\\partial = 0$ and the inductive hypothesis. Thus $\\partial t_{p} = c_{p}$, that is,\n\n$$\\partial t_{p} + t_{p-1}(\\partial\\Delta^{p}) = \\operatorname{sd}_{p} - \\operatorname{id}_{p}.$$\n\nIn the base case $p = 0$, we have $\\operatorname{sd}_{0} = \\operatorname{id}_{0}$ and $t_{0} = 0$.\n\n**Step 2. Naturality.** For a singular simplex $\\sigma$, set $\\operatorname{Sd}(\\sigma) := \\sigma_{\\#}\\operatorname{sd}_{p}$ and $T(\\sigma) := \\sigma_{\\#} t_{p}$, and extend linearly. Since $\\sigma_{\\#}$ is a chain map and is compatible with continuous maps,\n\n$$\\partial\\operatorname{Sd} = \\operatorname{Sd}\\partial,\\qquad \\partial T + T\\partial = \\operatorname{Sd} - \\operatorname{id}.$$\n\n(This follows by applying the preceding identity term by term. $T$ may produce degenerate simplices, which do not affect the chain identity.) A single $\\operatorname{Sd}(\\sigma)$ may fail to be small. Iteration ensures smallness.\n\n**Step 3. Diameter contraction.** Let $\\tau \\subseteq \\Delta^{p}$ be a simplex of diameter $\\delta$, and let $b_{S}$ denote the barycenter of the face $S$. The vertices of $\\operatorname{sd}(\\tau)$ are the barycenters of certain faces $S_{0} \\subset \\cdots \\subset S_{q}$, and for $S \\subset S^{\\prime}$ we have\n\n$$b_{S^{\\prime}} - b_{S} = \\frac{1}{\\#S^{\\prime}}\\sum_{v \\in S^{\\prime} \\setminus S}(v - b_{S}),$$\n\nHence $|b_{S^{\\prime}} - b_{S}| \\le \\left(1 - \\frac{\\#S}{\\#S^{\\prime}}\\right)\\delta \\le \\frac{p}{p+1}\\delta$. It follows that $\\operatorname{mesh}(\\operatorname{sd}^{m}\\tau) \\le (p/(p+1))^{m}\\delta$. After sufficiently many subdivisions, the smaller simplices can have arbitrarily small diameter.\n\n**Step 4. The small-chain lemma.** The inclusion $\\iota : S^{\\mathcal{U}}_{*}(X) \\hookrightarrow S_{*}(X)$ induces an isomorphism in homology.\n\nProof. For each singular $p$-simplex $\\sigma$, $\\{\\sigma^{-1}(U), \\sigma^{-1}(V)\\}$ is an open cover of the compact space $\\Delta^{p}$ and has a Lebesgue number $\\delta_{\\sigma} > 0$ (any subset of diameter $< \\delta_{\\sigma}$ lies in one member of the cover). Choose $m$ so that $(p/(p+1))^{m}\\operatorname{diam}(\\Delta^{p}) < \\delta_{\\sigma}$. Then the image of each smaller simplex of $\\operatorname{Sd}^{m}(\\sigma)$ lies in $U$ or $V$.\n\nWrite $T_{M} := \\sum_{j=0}^{M-1}\\operatorname{Sd}^{j} T$. Then $\\partial T_{M} + T_{M}\\partial = \\operatorname{Sd}^{M} - \\operatorname{id}$.\n\n**Surjectivity.** Take any cycle $z$. Since $z$ contains only finitely many simplices, choose $M$ so that each $\\operatorname{Sd}^{M}(\\sigma)$ is small as $\\sigma$ ranges over the simplices of $z$. Then $\\operatorname{Sd}^{M} z \\in S^{\\mathcal{U}}_{*}(X)$ and $\\operatorname{Sd}^{M} z - z = \\partial T_{M} z$, so $\\iota_{*}[\\operatorname{Sd}^{M} z] = [z]$.\n\n**Injectivity.** Suppose $z \\in S^{\\mathcal{U}}_{p}(X)$ is a cycle and $z = \\partial b$ ($b \\in S_{p+1}(X)$). Choose $M$ so that, for every simplex in $z$, $T z$, and $b$, the $M$-fold subdivision is small. Then $z = \\operatorname{Sd}^{M} z - \\partial T_{M} z$, with both $\\operatorname{Sd}^{M} z$ and $T_{M} z$ in the small-chain complex. Also, $\\operatorname{Sd}^{M} z = \\operatorname{Sd}^{M}\\partial b = \\partial(\\operatorname{Sd}^{M} b)$, and $\\operatorname{Sd}^{M} b$ is small as well. Thus in $H_{p}(S^{\\mathcal{U}}_{*}(X))$ we have $[z] = [\\operatorname{Sd}^{M} z] = 0$.\n\n**Step 5. Splitting into two pieces.** A chain is small if and only if it belongs to $S_{*}(U) + S_{*}(V)$. Thus there is a short exact sequence of chain complexes\n\n$$0 \\to S_{*}(U \\cap V) \\to S_{*}(U) \\oplus S_{*}(V) \\to S_{*}(U) + S_{*}(V) \\to 0,$$\n\nwith maps $a \\mapsto (a, -a)$ and $(a, b) \\mapsto a + b$. For exactness, observe that $S_{*}(U) \\cap S_{*}(V) = S_{*}(U \\cap V)$ (the image of each simplex lies in both $U$ and $V$, hence in their intersection). Surjectivity follows by definition, and the kernel follows from $a + b = 0$, that is, $b = -a$.\n\n**Step 6. Obtaining the long exact sequence.** Apply `thm.long-exact-pair` to the short exact sequence above, using the chain complex version proved by diagram chasing, to obtain the long exact sequence. Here $H_{p}(S_{*}(U) \\oplus S_{*}(V)) = H_{p}(U) \\oplus H_{p}(V)$, and $H_{p}(S_{*}(U) + S_{*}(V)) \\cong H_{p}(X)$ follows from Step 4. To identify the two maps, $(a) \\mapsto (a, -a)$ gives $\\Phi[z] = (i_{*}[z], -j_{*}[z])$, and $(a, b) \\mapsto a + b$ gives $\\Psi[a, b] = [a + b]$. ∎\n\n**Reduced version.** Apply the same argument to the augmented chain complexes. When $U \\cap V \\ne \\emptyset$, choose a basepoint $x_{0} \\in U \\cap V$. The augmentation map $\\varepsilon$ agrees on all three complexes, and the short exact sequence remains exact in the augmentation degree, giving the reduced long exact sequence. When $U \\cap V = \\emptyset$, exactness fails in the segment $\\tilde{H}_{0}$. See the statement for a counterexample.\n\n**Why no good pair hypothesis is needed.** The argument uses only the open cover and Lebesgue numbers. It requires no neighborhood deformation retraction of $A$ in $X$. This is one way singular homology is easier to work with than homotopy groups.\n\n⭐ **The main difficulty** is passing from simplices that lie locally in $U$ or $V$ to a global isomorphism in homology. The chain homotopy in Step 4 does this, and the exact sequence then follows by algebra. The signs are all in the cone construction, $\\operatorname{sd}_{p} := [b_{p}, \\operatorname{sd}_{p-1}(\\partial\\Delta^{p})]$ and $t_{p} := [b_{p}, c_{p}]$.\n\n⚠️ **Difference from `thm.mayer-vietoris`.** That node gives the **de Rham version** for smooth manifolds with real coefficients and the complex of differential forms (`def.de-rham-complex`). This is the **singular-chain version** for arbitrary spaces with $\\mathbb{Z}$ coefficients. The sequences have the same form, but only one retains torsion information. They are distinct results and should not be cited as proofs of each other.\n\n💡 Common applications include computing $\\tilde{H}_{*}(S^{n})$ (`thm.sphere-homology`), decomposing a torus into two cylinders, and decomposing a connected sum into two spaces with a small ball removed from each.",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#84dd1aa6'
    },

  'thm.mu-sum': {
    statement: {
      en: "For $n \\ge 1$,\n\n$$\\sum_{d \\mid n} \\mu(d) = \\begin{cases} 1, & n = 1, \\\\ 0, & n > 1 . \\end{cases}$$",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#175c779d'
    },

  'thm.quadratic-reciprocity': {
    statement: {
      en: "Let $p, q$ be two distinct odd primes. Then\n\n$$\\left(\\frac{p}{q}\\right)\\left(\\frac{q}{p}\\right) = (-1)^{\\frac{p-1}{2}\\cdot\\frac{q-1}{2}} .$$\n\nEquivalently, if at least one of $p, q$ is $\\equiv 1 \\pmod 4$, then $\\left(\\frac{p}{q}\\right) = \\left(\\frac{q}{p}\\right)$. If both are $\\equiv 3 \\pmod 4$, the two values have opposite signs. The supplementary formulas are\n\n$$\\left(\\frac{-1}{p}\\right) = (-1)^{\\frac{p-1}{2}}, \\qquad \\left(\\frac{2}{p}\\right) = (-1)^{\\frac{p^{2}-1}{8}} .$$",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#8cda3eb3'
    },
    notes: {
      en: "**This is the most important theorem in number theory** (Gauss gave eight proofs in his lifetime, the first in 1796 at age 19). It turns the difficult question of whether $p$ is a quadratic residue modulo $q$ into the easier question of whether $q$ is a quadratic residue modulo $p$, since one can recursively reduce $p$ to $< q$.\n\n\n\n⭐ A mainstream approach today uses **point counting on curves over finite fields**. $\\left(\\frac{p}{q}\\right)$ is related to the number of solutions of $x^{2} \\equiv p \\pmod q$, and these solutions can be counted in stages using elliptic curves or multiplicative groups over $\\mathbb{F}_{p}$ (Zolotarev's lemma and the signs of permutations). This is **one modern approach**. Elementary proofs also exist, including Gauss's third proof and proofs using Gauss's lemma or Zolotarev's lemma, all based on elementary counting.\n\n\n\n**Application.** To evaluate $\\left(\\frac{a}{p}\\right)$, factor it into primes, use reciprocity to swap the arguments for each prime factor, and recurse. This takes on the order of $O(\\log p)$ divisions. The two supplementary formulas above handle $-1$ and $2$, the two cases where the arguments cannot be swapped.\n\n\n\n**Proof (Gauss's lemma and lattice-point counting in a rectangle, elementary).** Write $P = \\frac{p-1}{2}$ and $Q = \\frac{q-1}{2}$. For $1 \\le x \\le P$, write $m_{x} := \\left\\lfloor \\frac{qx}{p} \\right\\rfloor$ and let $r_{x}$ be the least positive residue of $qx$ modulo $p$ ($1 \\le r_{x} \\le p-1$ because $p \\nmid qx$).\n\n\n\n**Step 1. Gauss's lemma.** Set $\\varepsilon_{x} = 1$ when $r_{x} < p/2$, or $-1$ when $r_{x} > p/2$. Set $s_{x} = r_{x}$ in the former case and $s_{x} = p - r_{x}$ in the latter. Then $1 \\le s_{x} \\le P$. We claim that $x \\mapsto s_{x}$ is a permutation of $\\{1, \\ldots, P\\}$. If $s_{x} = s_{y}$, then $r_{x} \\equiv \\pm r_{y} \\pmod p$, that is, $qx \\equiv \\pm qy \\pmod p$. Since $q$ is invertible modulo $p$, we have $x \\equiv \\pm y \\pmod p$. But $1 \\le x, y \\le P$ and $p$ is odd, so $x \\equiv -y$ would imply $x + y = p$, contradicting $x + y \\le 2P = p - 1$. Hence $x = y$ ✓.\n\n\n\nReplacing $\\{qx\\}_{x \\le P}$ by the least positive residues and then taking signs gives\n\n\n\n$$\\prod_{x=1}^{P} qx \\equiv \\prod_{x=1}^{P} (\\varepsilon_{x} s_{x}) = \\Bigl( \\prod_{x} \\varepsilon_{x} \\Bigr) \\Bigl( \\prod_{x} s_{x} \\Bigr) \\equiv \\Bigl( \\prod_{x} \\varepsilon_{x} \\Bigr) P! \\pmod p .$$\n\n\n\nThe left-hand side is $= q^{P} P!$. Canceling $P!$ ($p \\nmid P!$) gives $q^{P} \\equiv \\prod_{x} \\varepsilon_{x} = (-1)^{\\mu}$, where $\\mu = \\#\\{x : r_{x} > p/2\\}$. By **`lem.gauss-qr`** (Gauss's lemma), $\\left(\\frac{q}{p}\\right) = (-1)^{\\mu}$, so\n\n\n\n$$\\left(\\frac{q}{p}\\right) = (-1)^{\\mu} .$$\n\n\n\n**Step 2. $\\mu$ and $\\sum m_{x}$ have the same parity.** From $r_{x} = qx - p m_{x}$ and $\\sum_{x} s_{x} = \\sum_{x=1}^{P} x = \\frac{P(P+1)}{2}$ we obtain\n\n\n\n$$\\sum_{x} s_{x} = \\sum_{x} r_{x} + \\sum_{r_{x} > p/2} (p - 2r_{x}) ,$$\n\n\n\nWe also have $\\sum_{x} r_{x} = q\\sum_{x} x - p \\sum_{x} m_{x}$. Substitute these expressions and reduce both sides modulo $2$. Then $\\sum_{r_{x} > p/2}(p - 2r_{x}) \\equiv p\\mu \\equiv \\mu \\pmod 2$ and $q \\sum_{x} x \\equiv \\sum_{x} x \\pmod 2$, so\n\n\n\n$$\\sum_{x} x \\equiv \\sum_{x} x + \\sum_{x} m_{x} + \\mu \\pmod 2 ,$$\n\n\n\nCanceling $\\sum_{x} x$ gives $\\sum_{x} m_{x} \\equiv \\mu \\pmod 2$. Hence\n\n\n\n$$\\left(\\frac{q}{p}\\right) = (-1)^{\\mu} = (-1)^{\\sum_{x=1}^{P} \\lfloor qx/p \\rfloor} .$$\n\n\n\n**Step 3. Counting lattice points in a rectangle.** In the rectangle $R = \\{(x, y) \\in \\mathbb{Z}^{2} : 1 \\le x \\le P,\\ 1 \\le y \\le Q\\}$ (with $PQ$ lattice points in total), the line $qx = py$ contains no lattice points. Indeed, $p \\mid qx$ and $p \\nmid q$ imply $p \\mid x$, but $1 \\le x \\le P < p$, a contradiction. Thus $R$ splits into two parts $R_{-} = \\{qx - py > 0\\}$ and $R_{+} = \\{qx - py < 0\\}$, with $\\#R = \\#R_{-} + \\#R_{+} = PQ$.\n\n\n\nFor fixed $x$, $\\{y : 1 \\le y \\le Q,\\ qx - py > 0\\} = \\{y : y < qx/p\\}$ has exactly $\\min(Q, \\lfloor qx/p \\rfloor)$ elements. Since $qx/p \\le qP/p = \\frac{q(p-1)}{2p} < \\frac{q}{2}$, we have $\\lfloor qx/p \\rfloor \\le \\frac{q-1}{2} = Q$, and this count is $\\lfloor qx/p \\rfloor$. Thus $\\#R_{-} = \\sum_{x=1}^{P} \\lfloor qx/p \\rfloor$. By symmetry, interchanging $p \\leftrightarrow q$ and $x \\leftrightarrow y$ gives $\\#R_{+} = \\sum_{y=1}^{Q} \\lfloor py/q \\rfloor$. Therefore\n\n\n\n$$\\sum_{x=1}^{P} \\left\\lfloor \\frac{qx}{p} \\right\\rfloor + \\sum_{y=1}^{Q} \\left\\lfloor \\frac{py}{q} \\right\\rfloor = PQ .$$\n\n\n\n**Step 4. Combining the results.** Apply Step 2 in both directions, $(p, q)$ and $(q, p)$, and substitute the result of Step 3:\n\n\n\n$$\\left(\\frac{q}{p}\\right)\\left(\\frac{p}{q}\\right) = (-1)^{PQ} = (-1)^{\\frac{p-1}{2}\\cdot\\frac{q-1}{2}} ,$$\n\n\n\nThis is the desired quadratic reciprocity law ✓. ∎",
      status: 'draft',
      basedOn: 'v0.9.3#f977da75'
    },

    'thm.filtered-complex-spectral': {
      statement: {
        en: "Let $K^{\\bullet}$ be a **filtered** complex (with descending filtration $F^{p}K^{\\bullet}$), and suppose the filtration is **bounded on both sides** (for each $n$, if $p \\ll 0$ then $F^{p}K^{n} = K^{n}$, and if $p \\gg 0$ then $F^{p}K^{n} = 0$ — the former condition alone is insufficient, since stabilization of $Z^{p,q}_{r}$ requires the latter).         status: 'draft',

      notes: {
        en: "**Construction (how the pages approximate the limit).** For $r \\ge 0$, define two families of subobjects\n\n$$Z^{p,q}_{r} := F^{p}K^{p+q} \\cap (d^{p+q})^{-1}\\bigl(F^{p+r}K^{p+q+1}\\bigr), \\qquad B^{p,q}_{r} := F^{p}K^{p+q} \\cap d^{p+q-1}\\bigl(F^{p-r+1}K^{p+q-1}\\bigr),$$\n\nand set $E^{p,q}_{r} := Z^{p,q}_{r}/\\bigl(B^{p,q}_{r} + Z^{p+1,q-1}_{r-1}\\bigr)$ (with the conventions $Z^{p,q}_{0} = F^{p}K^{p+q}$ and $Z^{p,q}_{-1} = 0$. As $r$ increases, $Z^{p,q}_{r}$ increases and $B^{p,q}_{r}$ decreases. Because the filtration is **bounded on both sides** (both $p \\ll 0$ and $p \\gg 0$ are required), there are no further changes from larger filtration jumps of $d$ after finitely many steps. Thus for each $(p,q)$, both $Z^{p,q}_{r}$ and $B^{p,q}_{r}$ **stabilize**, and the limit is the associated graded of $H^{p+q}(K^{\\bullet})$ with its induced filtration.\n\n∎\n\n⭐ **How to read it**. The differentials then assemble these pieces page by page, converging to the **original** cohomology.\n\n⚠️ The **boundedness on both sides** is essential — the condition for $p \\ll 0$ alone is insufficient.         en: "Let $M$ be an $R$-module, and suppose there are two short exact sequences\n\n$$0 \\to K \\to P \\xrightarrow{\\ \\pi\\ } M \\to 0, \\qquad 0 \\to K_{1} \\to P_{1} \\xrightarrow{\\ \\pi_{1}\\ } M \\to 0,$$\n\nin which $P, P_{1}$ are both **projective**.       notes: {
        en: "⭐ **Syzygies agree up to projective direct summands**, and this conclusion requires **the middle terms of both precovers to be projective**. Projectivity keeps appearing throughout the theory of syzygies because it is the hypothesis that makes the splitting argument work.\n\n⚠️ The conclusion is an **isomorphism of direct sums**. The modules $K$ and $K_{1}$ need not be isomorphic. To remove this ambiguity, work in the **stable category**, where objects that differ by projective direct summands are identified up to isomorphism.\n\n**Name.** This is Schanuel's lemma. It first appeared in Schanuel's unpublished lecture notes and became widely known through texts by Rotman, Cartan–Eilenberg, and others.",

    'thm.comparison-theorem': {
      statement: {
        en: "Let $R$ be a ring, let $M, N$ be $R$-modules, and let $f : M \\to N$ be a module homomorphism. Suppose we are given\n\n$$\\cdots \\to P_{1} \\xrightarrow{\\ d_{1}\\ } P_{0} \\xrightarrow{\\ \\varepsilon_{P}\\ } M \\to 0, \\qquad \\cdots \\to Q_{1} \\xrightarrow{\\ e_{1}\\ } Q_{0} \\xrightarrow{\\ \\varepsilon_{Q}\\ } N \\to 0$$\n\nwhere $P_{\\bullet}$ is a **projective** resolution and $Q_{\\bullet}$ need only be an **exact** resolution (projectivity of $Q_{n}$ is unused, although standard textbooks often take both resolutions to be projective). Then\n\n1. There is a **chain map** $\\varphi : P_{\\bullet} \\to Q_{\\bullet}$ lifting $f$. It satisfies $\\varepsilon_{Q}\\varphi_{0} = f\\varepsilon_{P}$ and $\\varphi_{n-1}d_{n} = e_{n}\\varphi_{n}$ for every $n \\ge 1$.\n\n\n2. Any two lifts $\\varphi, \\psi$ are **chain homotopic**. There are maps $s_{n} : P_{n} \\to Q_{n+1}$ such that\n\n$$\\varphi_{n} - \\psi_{n} = e_{n+1}s_{n} + s_{n-1}d_{n},$$\n\nwhere we set $s_{-1} = 0$ and interpret $e_{0}$ as $\\varepsilon_{Q}$ and $d_{0}$ as $\\varepsilon_{P}$.",
        status: 'draft',
        basedOn: 'v0.9.3#0c49fdec',
      },
      notes: {
        en: "⭐ **Significance.** Resolutions **can vary**, but uniqueness up to chain homotopy suffices. Derived functors, including $\\operatorname{Ext}$ and $\\operatorname{Tor}$, depend only on the homotopy class of the resolution, so their definitions are independent of the choice of resolution. The construction of derived functors relies on this theorem to be well defined.\n\n⚠️ The theorem gives uniqueness **up to homotopy**. Two projective resolutions of the same module can differ by a contractible complex, as when one takes a resolution and adds a contractible summand of the form $0 \\to P \\xrightarrow{1} P \\to 0$. The two resolutions represent isomorphic objects in the derived category.\n\n**Dual version.** Lifts between injective resolutions satisfy the same two conclusions, with the direction of lifting reversed by the extension property of injective objects. See “Lifts Between Injective Resolutions.”\n\n⚠️ The argument uses termwise projectivity and exactness of the two resolutions. It **requires no choice principle beyond the axiom of choice** and applies to noncommutative rings as well.",

    'cor.gldim-pid': {
      statement: {
        en: "Let $R$ be a **principal ideal domain**. Then every $R$-module $M$ has a **free** resolution of length at most $1$, so\n\n$$\\operatorname{pd}_{R} M \\le 1 , \\qquad \\operatorname{gl.dim} R \\le 1 .$$\n\n(The preceding inequalities hold for every $R$-module $M$.) Furthermore, if $R$ is a field, then $\\operatorname{gl.dim} R = 0$. Otherwise, $\\operatorname{gl.dim} R = 1$. In particular,\n\n$$\\operatorname{gl.dim} \\mathbb{Z} = 1 .$$",
        status: 'draft',
        basedOn: 'v0.9.3#5b5cef55',
      },
      notes: {
        en: "**Proof.**\n**① A free resolution of length $2$.** Choose a generating set $I$ and a surjection $\\pi : R^{(I)} \\twoheadrightarrow M$ with free domain. Its kernel $K$ is a **submodule** of the free module $R^{(I)}$, and every submodule of a free module over a principal ideal domain is free ✓. Take a surjection $R^{(J)} \\twoheadrightarrow K$ and splice it into the sequence to obtain\n\n$$0 \\to R^{(J)} \\to R^{(I)} \\to M \\to 0 ,$$\n\nwhich is a free, hence projective, resolution of length $1$ ✓. Thus $\\operatorname{pd}_{R} M \\le 1$ for every $M$ ✓.\n**② The case $\\operatorname{gl.dim} = 0$.** If $R$ is a field, every module is free because every vector space has a basis. Every module is therefore projective, so $\\operatorname{pd} = 0$ and $\\operatorname{gl.dim} R = 0$ ✓.\n**③ If the ring is not a field, then $\\operatorname{gl.dim} = 1$.** Choose a nonzero nonunit $a \\in R$ and consider $M := R/(a)$.\n\nIf this module were projective, the short exact sequence $0 \\to (a) \\to R \\to R/(a) \\to 0$ would split by projectivity of $R/(a)$. Thus $(a)$ would be a direct summand of $R$ cut out by an idempotent.\n\nThe only idempotents in an integral domain are $0, 1$. Since $(a) \\ne 0$ and $(a) \\ne R$, this is a contradiction ✓.\n\nHence $\\operatorname{pd}_{R} M = 1$, with the upper bound supplied by ①, and therefore $\\operatorname{gl.dim} R = 1$ ✓.\n\n∎\n\n⭐ **This is equivalent to the result on Ext for abelian groups.** Expressing $\\operatorname{gl.dim}\\mathbb{Z} = 1$ in terms of $\\operatorname{Ext}$ gives “$\\operatorname{Ext}^{n}_{\\mathbb{Z}}(M, N) = 0$ for every $n \\ge 2$,” precisely the proposition on Ext for abelian groups.\n\nThe two propositions express the same fact in different ways.\n\n⚠️ **Why a PID?** The proof uses the ring hypothesis only to ensure that submodules of free modules are free. This fails over $k[x,y]$, and over $k[x,y]$ there is indeed a module with $\\operatorname{pd} = 2$ (namely $k$ itself). The global dimension rises to $2$.\n\n📌 The same argument gives **flatness of torsion-free modules over a principal ideal domain** (torsion-free with a free resolution of length $1$ $\\implies$ flat). See “Flat Modules.”",
        status: 'draft',
        basedOn: 'v0.9.3#2231f941',
      },

    'lem.horseshoe': {
      statement: {
        en: "Let $0 \\to M_{1} \\xrightarrow{\\ i\\ } M \\xrightarrow{\\ p\\ } M_{2} \\to 0$ be a short exact sequence of $R$-modules, with projective resolutions of the two end terms given by\n\n$$\\cdots \\to P_{1} \\xrightarrow{\\ d_{1}\\ } P_{0} \\xrightarrow{\\ \\varepsilon\\ } M_{1} \\to 0, \\qquad \\cdots \\to Q_{1} \\xrightarrow{\\ e_{1}\\ } Q_{0} \\xrightarrow{\\ \\eta\\ } M_{2} \\to 0 .$$\n\nThen there is a projective resolution of $M$, denoted by $R_{\\bullet} \\twoheadrightarrow M$, such that\n\n1. $R_{n} = P_{n} \\oplus Q_{n}$ (a termwise direct sum).\n2. The inclusions and projections form a **termwise split** short exact sequence of complexes $0 \\to P_{\\bullet} \\to R_{\\bullet} \\to Q_{\\bullet} \\to 0$.\n3. The augmentation $R_{0} = P_{0}\\oplus Q_{0} \\to M$ combines $i\\varepsilon$ with a lift of $\\eta$.\n\nThe diagram formed by the three resolutions is a **double complex**. The resolution of $M$ runs horizontally, and the two end terms have their respective resolutions running vertically. The diagram is shaped like a horseshoe.",
        status: 'draft',
        basedOn: 'v0.9.3#d5263255',
      },
      notes: {
        en: "**Proof (construct the differential, then check exactness with the long exact sequence).**\n**① Splitting in each degree.** The sequence of inclusions and projections $0 \\to P_{n} \\to P_{n}\\oplus Q_{n} \\to Q_{n} \\to 0$ already has the explicit section $(p, q) \\mapsto q$ and retraction $x \\mapsto (x, 0)$, independently of projectivity. This step uses the **splitting** itself and does not require projectivity of $P_{n}$ ✓.\n**② Choosing the differential.** Set\n\n$$d_{n} = \\begin{pmatrix} d_{n}^{P} & \\chi_{n} \\\\ 0 & e_{n} \\end{pmatrix} : P_{n}\\oplus Q_{n} \\longrightarrow P_{n-1}\\oplus Q_{n-1}, \\qquad \\chi_{n} : Q_{n} \\to P_{n-1}.$$\n\nBlock multiplication gives the upper-right block of $d_{n-1}d_{n}$ as follows: it $= d_{n-1}^{P}\\chi_{n} + \\chi_{n-1}e_{n}$. The other three blocks vanish.\n\nThus $d^{2} = 0$ if and only if $d_{n-1}^{P}\\chi_{n} = -\\chi_{n-1}e_{n}$.\n\nWe can choose $\\chi_{n}$ **inductively**. Given $\\chi_{n-1}$, we have\n\n$$d_{n-2}^{P}\\bigl(-\\chi_{n-1}e_{n}\\bigr) = -\\bigl(d_{n-2}^{P}\\chi_{n-1}\\bigr)e_{n} = \\chi_{n-2}e_{n-1}e_{n} = 0,$$\n\nso the image of $-\\chi_{n-1}e_{n}$ lies in $\\ker d_{n-2}^{P} = \\operatorname{im} d_{n-1}^{P}$ by exactness of $P_{\\bullet}$ ✓.\n\nLift along the surjection $P_{n-1} \\twoheadrightarrow \\operatorname{im} d_{n-1}^{P}$ using **projectivity** of $Q_{n}$ to obtain $\\chi_{n}$ ✓.\n**③ Augmentation.** Since $Q_{0}$ is projective and $p$ is surjective, $\\eta$ lifts along $p$ to $\\sigma : Q_{0} \\to M$ ($p\\sigma = \\eta$) ✓. Set $\\varepsilon_{R}(x, y) := i\\varepsilon(x) + \\sigma(y)$.\n\nTo obtain $\\varepsilon_{R}d_{0} = 0$, it suffices to impose one additional condition on $\\chi_{0} : Q_{0} \\to P_{-1} = M_{1}$. By $p\\sigma e_{0} = \\eta e_{0} = 0$, the image of $\\sigma e_{0}$ lies in $\\ker p = \\operatorname{im} i$, so the map can be written as $i\\theta$.\n\nNow use surjectivity of $\\varepsilon$ and projectivity of $Q_{0}$ to lift $\\theta : Q_{0} \\to M_{1}$ to $\\chi_{0}$ with the condition $\\varepsilon\\chi_{0} = -\\theta$ ✓.\n\nThus $\\varepsilon_{R}d_{0} = i\\varepsilon d_{0}^{P} + i\\varepsilon\\chi_{0} + \\sigma e_{0} = 0 - i\\theta + i\\theta = 0$ ✓.\n**④ Checking exactness (the key step).** Thus $R_{\\bullet}$ is a complex, and $0 \\to P_{\\bullet} \\to R_{\\bullet} \\to Q_{\\bullet} \\to 0$ is a **termwise split** short exact sequence of complexes. Take its long exact sequence in homology\n\n$$\\cdots \\to H_{n}(P) \\to H_{n}(R) \\to H_{n}(Q) \\xrightarrow{\\ \\delta\\ } H_{n-1}(P) \\to \\cdots$$\n\nwhere $H_{0}(P) \\cong M_{1}$ and $H_{0}(Q) \\cong M_{2}$, and $H_{n}(P) = H_{n}(Q) = 0$ (for $n \\ge 1$, since the two outer complexes are resolutions). Read off the following segments:\n- For $n \\ge 2$, the group $H_{n}(R)$ lies between two copies of $0$ and therefore vanishes ✓.\n- For $n = 1$, the segment $0 = H_{1}(P) \\to H_{1}(R) \\to H_{1}(Q) = 0$ gives $H_{1}(R) = 0$ ✓.\n- For $n = 0$, the exact segment $M_{1} \\xrightarrow{\\ i\\ } H_{0}(R) \\to M_{2} \\to 0$, compared with $0 \\to M_{1} \\to M \\to M_{2} \\to 0$, gives $H_{0}(R) \\cong M$ ✓.\nTogether these give $H_{0}(R) \\cong M$ and $H_{n}(R) = 0$ (for $n \\ge 1$). Each term $R_{n}$ is projective as a direct sum of projective objects, so $R_{\\bullet}$ is a projective resolution of $M$ ✓. ∎\n\n⭐ **Practical use.** When an explicit resolution of a module $M$ is unavailable, place it in the middle of a short exact sequence and resolve the two end terms. This gives a resolution of $M$ **that can be written explicitly as a termwise direct sum**. Koszul complexes, resolutions of tensor products, and double complexes in spectral sequences are all assembled this way.\n\n**Dual version.** Reverse all the arrows to obtain the horseshoe lemma for injective resolutions. It expresses the same principle as “The Total Complex Is Still an Injective Resolution” (the total complex of the double complex is again a resolution).\n\n⚠️ The three resolutions fit together **on an equal footing** to form a double complex. The horseshoe lemma thus provides a double complex and two associated spectral sequences. Taking horizontal homology first or vertical homology first gives the two different spectral sequences. See “Double Complexes Give Rise to Spectral Sequences.”",
        status: 'draft',
        basedOn: 'v0.9.3#eb394d44',
      },

    'prop.syzygy-stability': {
      statement: {
        en: "Let $M$ be an $R$-module, and let $P_{\\bullet}$ and $Q_{\\bullet}$ be two projective resolutions of it, yielding the $n$th syzygies $\\Omega^{n}M$ and $\\Omega_{1}^{n}M$, respectively. Then for every $n \\ge 0$ there are projective modules $F, F_{1}$ and an isomorphism\n\n$$\\Omega^{n}M \\oplus F \\;\\cong\\; \\Omega_{1}^{n}M \\oplus F_{1} .$$\n\nIn particular, $\\Omega^{n}M$ is projective $\\iff$ $\\Omega_{1}^{n}M$ is projective. Thus whether the $n$th syzygy is projective is independent of the choice of resolution, which is why projective dimension is well defined.",
        status: 'draft',
        basedOn: 'v0.9.3#3bf2bb2d',
      },
      notes: {
        en: "**Proof (induct on $n$, applying Schanuel's lemma at each step).**\nFor $n = 0$, we have $M \\cong M$ and take $F = F_{1} = 0$ ✓.\nFor $n = 1$, the two precovers $0 \\to \\Omega M \\to P_{0} \\to M \\to 0$ and $0 \\to \\Omega_{1}M \\to Q_{0} \\to M \\to 0$ both have projective middle terms, so Schanuel's lemma gives $P_{0}\\oplus\\Omega_{1}M \\cong Q_{0}\\oplus\\Omega M$ directly ✓.\nFor the inductive step, suppose $\\Omega^{n}M \\oplus F \\cong \\Omega_{1}^{n}M \\oplus F_{1}$.\n\nThe two resolutions give two precovers in degree $n+1$ with projective middle terms $P_{n}$ and $Q_{n}$. Their targets are $\\Omega^{n}M$ and $\\Omega_{1}^{n}M$, respectively. Since the targets are different modules, Schanuel's lemma cannot be applied directly.\n\nTo obtain a common target, incorporate the direct summands.\n\nTake\n\n$$P_{n} \\oplus Q_{n} \\twoheadrightarrow \\Omega^{n}M \\oplus F, \\qquad P_{n} \\oplus Q_{n} \\twoheadrightarrow \\Omega_{1}^{n}M \\oplus F_{1},$$\n\n(using the isomorphism from the induction hypothesis to transport the target of the second map). Both precovers have the projective middle term $P_{n}\\oplus Q_{n}$ ✓. Applying Schanuel's lemma again gives\n\n$$\\Omega^{n+1}M \\oplus \\bigl(P_{n}\\oplus Q_{n}\\bigr) \\;\\cong\\; \\Omega_{1}^{n+1}M \\oplus \\bigl(P_{n}\\oplus Q_{n}\\bigr),$$\n\nwhich has the required form, since the added summands are projective ✓. ∎\n\n⚠️ In the inductive step, the distinction between $\\Omega^{n}M$ and $\\Omega_{1}^{n}M$ matters. The induction hypothesis gives an **isomorphism after adding direct summands**, so at every step the precovers must be constructed again after taking direct sums. This is what “stable” means here.\n\n⭐ **Making the construction canonical.** Once differences by projective direct summands are quotiented out, $\\Omega^{n}$ becomes a genuine functor. The resulting quotient category is the **stable module category**, where syzygies are well defined up to isomorphism. This also explains the role of stability in $K_{0}$, stable homotopy theory, and $K$-theory.\n\n📌 An immediate consequence is that $\\operatorname{pd}_{R} M \\le n \\iff \\Omega^{n}M$ is projective. The result “Characterizing Projective Dimension by the Vanishing of Ext Groups” uses this as its main criterion.",

    'thm.pushforward-enough-injectives': {
      statement: {
        en: "Let $X$ be a topological space. Then the abelian sheaf category $\\mathbf{Ab}(X)$ **has enough injectives**: for each $\\mathcal{F} \\in \\mathbf{Ab}(X)$, embed every stalk into its injective hull in $\\mathbf{Ab}$, namely $\\mathcal{F}_{x} \\rightarrowtail I_{x}$; then the natural map\n\n$$\\mathcal{F} \\longrightarrow \\prod_{x \\in X} i_{x,*}\\, I_{x}$$\n\nis **injective**, and its target is an **injective sheaf**. Consequently:\n\n1. Every sheaf has an injective resolution, and one can take it **bounded below**;\n2. The right derived functors of the section functor $\\Gamma(X, -)$, and of the pushforward along any continuous map $f$ — that is, $f_{*}$ — namely $R^{q}\\Gamma$ and $R^{q}f_{*}$, are defined on bounded-below complexes and are independent of the choice of injective resolution;\n3. $f_{*}$ sends injective sheaves to injective sheaves ($f^{-1}$ is exact and $f^{-1} \\dashv f_{*}$), so the injectives \"on the $f_{*}$ side\" also suffice to compute $Rf_{*}$.",
        status: 'draft',
        basedOn: 'v0.9.3#ed76b90f',
      },
      notes: {
        en: "**Proof: injectivity.** A map of sheaves is injective $\\iff$ it is injective on sections over every open set.\n\nTake $U$ open and $s \\in \\mathcal{F}(U)$; its image in the $x$-th component (when $x \\in U$) is the stalk of $s$, namely $s_{x} \\in \\mathcal{F}_{x} \\rightarrowtail I_{x}$ (when $x \\notin U$ the component lands in $0$).\n\nIf $s \\ne 0$, then there is $x \\in U$ with $s_{x} \\ne 0$ (**a sheaf is determined by all its stalks**), so some component is nonzero.\n\nHence $\\mathcal{F} \\to \\prod_{x} i_{x,*}I_{x}$ is injective.\n\n∎\n\n**Proof: the target is injective.** We need $\\operatorname{Hom}_{\\mathbf{Ab}(X)}(-, \\prod_{x} i_{x,*}I_{x})$ to be exact.\n\n$\\operatorname{Hom}$ and the direct product both preserve limits in the **second** variable, so it suffices, for each $x$, to check $i_{x,*}I_{x}$: by adjunction and by \"$i_{x}^{-1} = $ takes stalks\",\n\n$$\\operatorname{Hom}_{\\mathbf{Ab}(X)}\\bigl(\\mathcal{F},\\ i_{x,*}I_{x}\\bigr) \\;\\cong\\; \\operatorname{Hom}_{\\mathbf{Ab}}\\bigl(\\mathcal{F}_{x},\\ I_{x}\\bigr),$$\n\nand $\\mathcal{F} \\mapsto \\mathcal{F}_{x}$ is exact while $I_{x}$ is injective, so the right-hand side is exact in $\\mathcal{F}$ — each $i_{x,*}I_{x}$ is injective, and a direct product of injectives is injective (the pointwise argument carries over to the product verbatim). ∎\n\n⭐ **Why this makes the definition of sheaf cohomology legitimate**: defining $H^{n}(X, \\mathcal{F}) := R^{n}\\Gamma(X, \\mathcal{F})$ presupposes an injective resolution.\n\nWithout enough injectives, resolutions do not exist at all, and $R^{n}\\Gamma$ cannot be defined.\n\nWith the two facts above, every $\\mathcal{F}$ has an injective resolution;\n\nthen by the **comparison theorem** (maps between two injective resolutions are unique up to homotopy), $H^{n}$ is independent of the choice of resolution — sheaf cohomology is only now a **well-defined functor**.\n\nThe same statement holds for $R^{n}f_{*}$.\n\n⭐ **The shape of the argument is worth remembering**: it uses only two things, \"stalks\" and \"direct products\", so it holds for any site with \"enough points\": in the abelian sheaf categories of topological spaces and of topoi, **injectivity can be checked pointwise**. This is the most naive source of \"sheaf cohomology can be read off stalk by stalk\".\n\n⚠️ Choosing $I_{x}$ uses that $\\mathbf{Ab}$ has enough injectives (products of $\\mathbb{Q}/\\mathbb{Z}$ give injective hulls) — that is the Baer criterion for $\\mathbf{Ab}$, not redone here.",

    'prop.quasi-compact-properties': {
      statement: {
        en: "1. In a site $\\mathcal{C}$, $X$ is quasi-compact $\\iff$ $X$ is quasi-compact in the standard topology;\n2. If $X$ has a **finite** cover by **quasi-compact** objects, then $X$ is quasi-compact;\n3. On a pretopos $\\mathcal{C}$ (with the precanonical topology), a sheaf $F$ is quasi-compact $\\iff$ there is a surjection $X \\to F$ with $X \\in \\mathcal{C}$.",
        status: 'draft',
        basedOn: 'v0.9.3#f97d240e',
      },
      notes: {
        en: "**Proof, item by item.**\n**1.** Covering families of the site correspond one-to-one to covering families in the sheaf category it generates under the standard topology (subcanonicity ensures representable sheaves are preserved as they are), and quasi-compactness only uses the covering families, so the two statements agree ✓.\n**2.** Suppose $X$ is covered by a finite family of quasi-compact objects $X_{1}, \\ldots, X_{n}$, and let $X$ be given an arbitrary cover $(Y_{j}\\to X)$.\n\nFor each $k$, pull back along $X_{k}\\to X$ to obtain a cover of $X_{k}$, namely $(Y_{j}\\times_{X}X_{k} \\to X_{k})$;\n\nby quasi-compactness of $X_{k}$ extract a finite subfamily $(j \\in J_{k})$.\n\nThen $J := \\bigcup_{k}J_{k}$ is finite, and $(Y_{j}\\to X)_{j\\in J}$ is already a cover (each branch $X_{k}$ is covered, and $\\{X_{k}\\}$ covers $X$) ✓.\n**3.** $(\\Longleftarrow)$ If $X \\to F$ is surjective and $X \\in \\mathcal{C}$, then any cover $(h_{X_{i}} \\to F)$ given by representable sheaves pulls back along $X \\to F$ to a cover of $X$; by \"$h_{X}$ is quasi-compact\" ($X \\in \\mathcal{C}$) extract a finite subfamily, and the composite $h_{X_{i}} \\to h_{X} \\to F$ gives a finite subcover of $F$ ✓.\n$(\\implies)$ Conversely, the objects of $\\mathcal{C}$ form a generating family of the sheaf category, so there is a surjection $\\coprod_{i}h_{X_{i}} \\to F$; by quasi-compactness of $F$ extract a finite subfamily $(i \\in I,\\ \\#I < \\infty)$, and set $X := \\coprod_{i\\in I}X_{i} \\in \\mathcal{C}$; then $h_{X} = \\coprod_{i\\in I}h_{X_{i}} \\to F$ is still surjective ✓.\n\n— In condensed sets this reads \"a condensed set is quasi-compact $\\iff$ there is a surjection from a compact Hausdorff space onto it\" ✓.\n\n∎\n\nItem 3 is the most useful: it replaces \"the sheaf $F$ is quasi-compact\" by \"$F$ can be covered by a small object\" — in condensed sets, this is \"a condensed set $X$ is quasi-compact $\\iff$ there is a surjection from a compact Hausdorff space onto it\".\n\n**Assumptions used in each direction** (the finite-cover criterion relies on the correspondence between site covers and sheaf covers):\n- Item 1 needs, in both directions, the comparison of covers between the site and its sheaf category, reading representable objects as sheafified representable presheaves; only in the subcanonical case may one omit sheafification. This is a comparison of covers, not something provable by renaming.\n- Item 2 derives quasi-compactness from a finite quasi-compact cover: pull an arbitrary cover back to each piece, take finite subfamilies, then use transitivity of covers; the one-point cover of an infinite discrete space shows that \"finite\" cannot be dropped.\n- Item 3, forward: representable sheaves generate the sheaf category, giving a surjective cover; quasi-compactness extracts finitely many branches, and finite coproducts combine them into a single $X$; the last step uses preservation of finite coproducts under the precanonical topology.\n- Item 3, backward: when $h_X\\to F$ is surjective and $h_X$ is quasi-compact, pull the cover back, take a finite subfamily, and descend the cover along surjectivity. The key point is that representable sheaves are quasi-compact for this topology; this cannot be assumed for free on an arbitrary site, e.g. the terminal sheaf of a non-quasi-compact topological space.",
        status: 'draft',
        basedOn: 'v0.9.3#34ab34c8',
