    'thm.cgwh-reflective': {
      statement: {
        en: "The category $\\mathrm{CGWH}$ of compactly generated **weak Hausdorff** spaces is a **reflective** subcategory of $\\mathrm{CG}$ (compactly generated spaces).\n\nThe reflector is\n\n$$h : X \\longmapsto X/R,$$\n\nwhere $R$ is the **smallest closed equivalence relation** on $X$.",
        status: 'draft',
        basedOn: 'v0.9.3#6a26e827',
      },
      notes: {
        en: "**Why the \"smallest closed equivalence relation\".** For the quotient $X/R$ to be weak Hausdorff, $R$ must be $k$-closed (previous item); and to quotient out as little as possible, so that the reflector is the \"most economical\" repair, one takes the smallest such closed equivalence relation. ($R \\mapsto X/R$ the larger the relation, the smaller the quotient — do not get the direction backwards.)\n\n⭐ This gives the complete chain for \"how to repair a space\": $k$-ification ($X \\mapsto kX$, producing $\\mathrm{CG}$) $\\mapsto$ quotienting by the smallest closed equivalence relation ($kX \\mapsto kX/R$, producing $\\mathrm{CGWH}$).\n\nBoth steps are **functorial**, so isomorphisms, limits, and colimits in $\\mathrm{CGWH}$ can be transported from $\\mathbf{Top}$.\n\n⭐ This is why modern algebraic topology works in $\\mathrm{CGWH}$ by default: $\\mathrm{CGWH}$ is closed under **finite products**, the function space $kC(X,Y)$ stays inside it, and it is cartesian closed — whereas $\\mathbf{Top}$ satisfies none of these.\n\n**Proof.** Let $R$ be the intersection, taken over $X$, of **all $k$-closed equivalence relations** (when $X$ is compactly generated, $k$-closed = closed, so this is the \"smallest closed equivalence relation\"; an intersection of equivalence relations is again an equivalence relation ✓), and let $\\eta_{X} : X \\to X/R$ be the quotient map.\n- **$X/R \\in \\mathrm{CGWH}$**: $X/R$ is compactly generated (a quotient of a compactly generated space) ✓; and $R$ is $k$-closed $\\implies$ $X/R$ is weak Hausdorff (previous item) ✓.\n- **Universal property of the reflector**: let $Y \\in \\mathrm{CGWH}$ and $f : X \\to Y$ be continuous. Then\n$$R_{f} := \\{(x, x') : f(x) = f(x')\\} = (f,f)^{-1}(\\Delta_{Y})$$\nis an equivalence relation on $X$; from $\\Delta_{Y}$ being $k$-closed ($Y$ is weak Hausdorff) and \"$k$-closedness pulls back along continuous maps to $k$-closedness\" we get that $R_{f}$ is $k$-closed.\n\nHence $R \\subseteq R_{f}$ ($R$ is the intersection), so $f$ is constant on each class of $R$, and therefore factors **uniquely** through the quotient: $\\exists!\\, \\bar f : X/R \\to Y$ with $\\bar f\\circ\\eta_{X} = f$ ✓.\n- **$\\mathrm{CGWH}$ sits fully faithfully in $\\mathrm{CG}$**: when $Y$ is itself weak Hausdorff, $\\eta_{Y} : Y \\to Y/R_{Y}$ is a homeomorphism ($R_{Y}$ is $\\Delta_{Y}$ itself), so the component of the reflector at $\\mathrm{CGWH}$ is the identity ✓.\nTogether these say \"$\\mathrm{CGWH}$ is a reflective subcategory of $\\mathrm{CG}$, with reflector $X\\mapsto X/R$\". ∎\n⚠️ **Direction**: the **larger** the equivalence relation, the **more** is quotiented out and the coarser the space. So the \"smallest\" closed equivalence relation gives the \"most economical\" weak Hausdorffification — which is why the reflector first $k$-ifies ($X\\rightsquigarrow kX$), then quotients by the smallest closed equivalence relation, both steps functorial.",

    'def.chaus-pretopos': {
      statement: {
        en: "In $\\mathbf{CHaus}$:\n\n1. all limits and colimits exist;\n2. finite coproducts are **disjoint** and **universal**;\n3. equivalence relations are **effective** and **universal**;\n4. epimorphisms are **regular** and **universal**.\n\nHence $\\mathbf{CHaus}$ is a **pretopos**.",
        status: 'draft',
        basedOn: 'v0.9.3#0668dab0',

      notes: {
        en: "Item 1 is read off directly from \"$\\mathbf{CHaus}$ is a reflective subcategory of $\\mathbf{Top}$\":\n\n$$\\varprojlim{}^{\\mathbf{CHaus}} D \\;\\cong\\; \\varprojlim{}^{\\mathbf{Top}} D, \\qquad \\varinjlim{}^{\\mathbf{CHaus}} D \\;\\cong\\; \\beta\\Bigl(\\varinjlim{}^{\\mathbf{Top}} D\\Bigr)$$\n\n**Limits are computed as in $\\mathbf{Top}$** (a reflective subcategory is closed under limits), while **colimits must first be computed in $\\mathbf{Top}$ and then compactified back by $\\beta$** (a left adjoint preserves colimits).\n\n**Proof.** The key is \"which colimits of $\\mathbf{Top}$ **are also** colimits of $\\mathbf{CHaus}$\":\n- the four properties all hold in $\\mathbf{Set}$ (a topos); and limits in $\\mathbf{CHaus}$ are computed pointwise in $\\mathbf{Top}$ ($\\mathbf{CHaus}$ is closed under limits), so the part involving limits is inherited directly from $\\mathbf{Set}$;\n- a colimit of $\\mathbf{Top}$ that is **itself still compact Hausdorff** is a colimit in $\\mathbf{CHaus}$ — **finite coproducts**, **coequalizers of epimorphisms**, and **coequalizers of closed equivalence relations** all belong to this class;\n- hence: finite coproducts are disjoint (first disjoint in $\\mathbf{Set}$, then note that the coproduct is itself compact Hausdorff), epimorphisms are universal (pullbacks of epimorphisms are epimorphisms, still in the compact Hausdorff world), closed equivalence relations are effective (the quotient is compact Hausdorff);\n- item 4: a **continuous surjection between compact Hausdorff spaces is a quotient map** — that is exactly \"epimorphism $\\implies$ regular\". ∎\n\n**What each of the four axioms \"leans on\" in $\\mathbf{CHaus}$** (this example shows best what the pretopos axioms amount to):\n\n- **① all limits and colimits exist**: **limits** come from limits in $\\mathbf{Top}$ (compact Hausdorff spaces are closed under arbitrary products and equalizers, by Tychonoff); **colimits** require taking \"the compact Hausdorffification after quotienting the equivalence relation\", i.e. using a Stone–Čech-type reflection to correct a general quotient to a compact Hausdorff one. ⚠️ Colimits are **not** computed by taking the quotient directly in $\\mathbf{Top}$; one must pass through a reflection — this is the only technically substantive step of the item.\n- **② finite coproducts are disjoint and universal**: **disjointness** uses \"the disjoint union of compact Hausdorff spaces is again compact Hausdorff\"; **universality** is stability under pullback, using that fibred products of compact Hausdorff spaces are again in $\\mathbf{CHaus}$ (an argument of the @@prop.cgwh-fiber-product@@ type).\n- **③ equivalence relations are effective and universal**: **effective** = the kernel pair of the quotient map is exactly the original equivalence relation — in compact Hausdorff spaces this is guaranteed by \"quotient maps are closed\" (the four items of @@prop.chaus-quotient-equiv@@).\n- **④ epimorphisms are regular and universal**: **epimorphism = surjection** (again from the special nature of compact Hausdorff spaces), so regularity is automatic.\n\n⚠️ **$\\mathbf{CHaus}$ is not a topos**: it lacks a **small generator + arbitrary colimits** (arbitrary colimits need reflection, and there is no representable small generator/subobject classifier). So it is only a **pretopos** — this example illustrates exactly the usefulness of the notion \"pretopos\": it captures the layer where \"one can form quotients and equivalence relations\".\n\n**Hypotheses used in each direction** (the pretopos conclusion is guaranteed jointly by compactness and Hausdorffness):\n- The listed finite limits, finite disjoint universal coproducts and effective equivalence relations $\\implies$ pretopos: unfold the respective definitions; arbitrary small limits and colimits are extra properties.\n- Compact Hausdorff $\\implies$ arbitrary small products are compact Hausdorff: Tychonoff's theorem; existence of colimits uses the reflection into the category of compact Hausdorff spaces and cannot be inferred merely from the right adjoint preserving limits.\n- Epimorphism $\\implies$ surjection: if not surjective the image is closed, and continuous functions on a compact Hausdorff space separate points from closed sets; surjection $\\implies$ regular epimorphism uses that a continuous surjection from a compact space to a Hausdorff space is a closed quotient map.\n- Internal equivalence relation $\\implies$ effective and universal: the relation is a closed subspace of the product, and the quotient of a closed equivalence relation is again compact Hausdorff; general topological equivalence relations have no such guarantee.\n- Finite disjoint unions are again compact, giving the finite-coproduct property; disjoint unions of infinitely many non-empty clopen branches are usually not compact. The original note's claim that this category lacks arbitrary colimits is not correct; the lack of topos structure cannot be attributed to the non-existence of these colimits.\n\nItems 2 and 3 can also be inherited from $\\mathbf{Set}$: disjointness, universality, and effectiveness are all \"pointwise\" properties, and constructions in $\\mathbf{CHaus}$ inherit sets pointwise.",
        status: 'draft',
        basedOn: 'v0.9.3#27347bd3',

        en: "**What is being done**: the fibre of a principal bundle is the group $G$, but the fibre you want is often some other space $F$ (a vector space, a sphere, a flag manifold...).     'prop.grothendieck-enough-injectives': {
      statement: {
        en: "Every **Grothendieck category** (an AB5 category with a generator) has **enough injective objects**:\n\nevery object $M$ embeds into an injective object; equivalently, every object has an injective resolution\n\n$$0 \\longrightarrow M \\longrightarrow I^{0} \\longrightarrow I^{1} \\longrightarrow \\cdots$$",
        status: 'draft',
        basedOn: 'v0.9.3#565e39a9',
      },
      notes: {
        en: "⭐ **This is the existence guarantee for \"right derived functors\"**. The standard definition of a right derived functor $R^{n}F$ is \"take an injective resolution, apply $F$, take cohomology\" — and this definition makes sense only if **injective resolutions always exist**. This theorem says: as long as the category is Grothendieck, there is nothing to worry about.\n\n**What \"enough\" means**: not \"there are many injective objects\", but \"every object embeds into some injective object\". This condition is about the **category as a whole** and cannot be checked object by object.\n\n**Where it is used**: $A\\text{-}\\mathbf{Mod}$, $\\operatorname{Ind}(\\mathcal{C})$, $\\mathbf{Ab}(\\mathcal{T})$ on a topos, $\\mathrm{CondAb}$ — all are Grothendieck categories, so right derived functors, derived categories, and spectral sequences are all available on them.\n\nThis is the whole reason Grothendieck's generation singled out the two conditions \"AB5 + generator\".\n\n📌 Another item that follows from the definition (same chapter): a Grothendieck category **automatically satisfies AB3\\\\*** (has all limits) — existence of limits needs no extra hypothesis.\n\n**Proof (transfinite iteration; the categorical version of Baer's criterion).** Let $\\mathcal{A}$ be an AB5 category with generator $U$.\n- **Criterion**: $I$ is injective $\\iff$ for every monomorphism $A\\rightarrowtail B$ and every $A\\to I$, the extension $B\\to I$ exists. Since $U$ is a generator, it suffices to check **subobjects** $A\\rightarrowtail U$ (a general $B$ is spread by a family of $U\\to B$, extended piecewise, and pasted).\n- **Step one**: the evaluation map induced by $U$ on $\\implies$, landing in $M\\hookrightarrow U^{\\operatorname{Hom}(U,M)}$ is a monomorphism (two morphisms equal on all of $U\\to M$ are equal), so $M$ always embeds into a product of a family of $U$.\n- **Step two (completing the extension)**: let $M_{0} := M$.\n\nGiven $M_{\\alpha}$, push out all the \"data to be extended\" $\\bigl(A\\rightarrowtail U,\\ A\\to M_{\\alpha}\\bigr)$ at once — let $M_{\\alpha+1}$ be the pushout of $M_{\\alpha}$ and $\\coprod_{(A,\\varphi)}U$ along $\\coprod_{(A,\\varphi)}A$.\n\nThen every map from a subobject of $U$ can be extended in $M_{\\alpha+1}$ to all of $U$.\n- **Limit ordinals**: take $M_{\\beta} := \\varinjlim_{\\alpha<\\beta}M_{\\alpha}$. $\\mathcal{A}$ is **AB5** (filtered colimits are exact), and this layer-by-layer colimit preserves monomorphisms and exactness ✓.\n- **Stabilization**: the increment depends only on the **set** \"subobjects of $U$\" (the generator plus AB5 guarantees it is small), so after a sufficiently large ordinal $M_{\\alpha}\\to M_{\\alpha+1}$ is already an isomorphism;\n\nthe stabilized $I := M_{\\infty}$ satisfies the criterion, hence is injective ✓.\n\nRepeating the process with $M\\hookrightarrow I$ gives an injective resolution.\n\n∎\n**Standard source**: Freyd, *Abelian Categories*; a shorter route goes through the Gabriel–Popescu embedding (regard $\\mathcal{A}$ as an exact reflective subcategory of $R\\text{-}\\mathbf{Mod}$, then reflect the injectives of $R\\text{-}\\mathbf{Mod}$ over).",

        basedOn: 'v0.9.3#d413b5c5',

    'def.interior-product': {
      statement: {
        en: "**In plain words**: put a vector field into the first slot of a form and the degree drops by one — that is the interior product.       notes: {
        en: "**Why it is \"pointwise\" while the Lie derivative is \"global\"**: the interior product is computed from $X_{p}$ alone, yielding $(\\iota_{X}\\omega)_{p}$, so it is a tensor operation (linear in $X$ over $C^{\\infty}$) and requires solving no equation;\n\nthe Lie derivative differentiates along the flow, is defined only on vector fields, and is linear in $X$ only over $\\mathbb{R}$.\n\nThe two are exactly complementary in Cartan's formula: $\\mathrm{d}\\iota_{X}$ is \"contract first, then differentiate\", $\\iota_{X}\\mathrm{d}$ is \"differentiate first, then contract\".\n\n**Why $\\iota_{X}^{2} = 0$**: contracting twice puts two vectors into the same slot, and forms are alternating, so exchanging the two contractions produces a sign $-$; hence $(\\iota_{X}^{2}\\omega)(v_{3},\\ldots) = \\omega(X,X,v_{3},\\ldots) = 0$ (alternation plus the fact that a vector paired with itself gives zero).\n\n**In physics notation it is \"contracting one index\"**: the electromagnetic field $F = \\frac{1}{2}F_{ij}\\,\\mathrm{d}x^{i}\\wedge\\mathrm{d}x^{j}$ and $\\iota_{E}F$ give quantities such as \"the magnetic flux in the direction of the electric field\";\n\nin Maxwell's equations $\\mathrm{d}F = 0$, $\\mathrm{d}\\star F = J$, where $\\star$ depends on the metric while $\\iota$ and $\\mathrm{d}$ are purely combinatorial.\n\n**Relation to contraction**: $\\iota_{X}\\omega$ is exactly putting $X$ into the first slot and contracting — a special case of tensor contraction on alternating tensors, except that the inserted vector need not be a basis vector.\n\n**Interaction with the wedge product**: the combination $\\mathrm{d}\\iota_{X} + \\iota_{X}\\mathrm{d}$ is natural because $\\mathrm{d}$ is an antiderivation of degree $+1$ and $\\iota_{X}$ is an antiderivation of degree $-1$, and the composite of two antiderivations is a \"derivation of degree $0$\" — the same type as the Lie derivative.\n\nThe proof of Cartan's formula compares exactly this type.",

      statement: {
        en: "Let $\\mathcal{C}$ be a **small abelian** category and $E : \\mathcal{C} \\to A\\text{-}\\mathbf{Mod}$ the Freyd–Mitchell embedding (fully faithful, exact).         status: 'draft',
        basedOn: 'v0.9.3#ca878c9e',
      },
      notes: {
        en: "**Proof (uses only \"fully faithful + exact\").**\n**① Exactness.** This is part of the statement of the Freyd–Mitchell theorem (exact = preserves finite limits and finite colimits), so kernels, cokernels, images, and short exact sequences are all carried over verbatim ✓.\n**② Reflection of projectives.** Suppose $EP$ is projective in $A\\text{-}\\mathbf{Mod}$, i.e. $\\operatorname{Hom}_{A\\text{-}\\mathbf{Mod}}(EP, -)$ is exact. Both are \"find a concrete large category that contains you\", and this item supplies \"after embedding, which properties can be transported back\".",

      notes: {
        en: "Item 2 is exactly the one repeatedly needed in condensed mathematics: assembling \"objects built from finitely many pieces\" into a larger one along a filtered colimit, without losing the property $\\mathrm{CGWH}$.\n\n**Proof.**\n- **Open subspaces**: $U \\subseteq X$ open.\n\nFor a compact Hausdorff $g : S\\to U$, its image $g(S)$ is closed in $X$, hence in the subspace topology of $U$ $g(S) = g(S)\\cap U$ is closed $\\implies$ $U$ is weak Hausdorff ✓;\n\n$k$-ification commutes with \"taking open subspaces\" (the $k$ topology is characterized by pullback along compact Hausdorff pieces, and whether a piece lands in $U$ is a local condition) $\\implies$ $U$ is compactly generated ✓.\n- **Closed subspaces**: $A \\subseteq X$ closed.       notes: {
        en: "⭐ **This is the bridge joining the two cohomology theories.** Condensed cohomology is computed from \"sheaves on $X$\"; sheaf cohomology is computed from \"sheaves on the open sets of $X$\". This says: as long as $X$ is locally compact and the coefficients are discrete, the two sides **agree**.\n\n**How to prove it.**\n\nUsing the topos morphism $c_{X} : \\mathrm{Cond}/X \\rightleftarrows \\mathrm{Open}(X)$ and its derived functors gives a spectral sequence whose $E_{2}$ terms are $R^{q}c_{*}$ applied to $M$.\n\nThe key computation is $R^{q}c_{*}\\underline{M} = 0$ ($q > 0$), given by a computation on **stalks**: after passing to stalks, $M$ is discrete and carries no higher information.\n\nThe spectral sequence degenerates and the two sides are isomorphic.\n\n**Why \"coefficients discrete\" is required.** Once the coefficients carry a topology (say a real Banach space), $R^{q}c_{*}$ no longer vanishes in general and the two sides fail to agree — this is precisely the situation §8.2 has to handle.\n\n📌 **Use**: condensed cohomology on any **locally compact** space can be computed **directly** with classical sheaf cohomology (open covers, Čech, fine resolutions — the whole old toolkit applies).\n\n**Proof (use the spectral sequence of $c_{X}$ to compute $R^{q}c_{X*}$ as zero).**\n- From $c_{X}^{-1}(X) = X$ ($X$ as an object of $\\mathrm{Open}(X)$, whose inverse image recovers it), one gets the Grothendieck spectral sequence\n$$E^{p,q}_{2} = H^{p}_{\\mathrm{sheaf}}\\bigl(X,\\ R^{q}c_{X*}M\\bigr) \\;\\Longrightarrow\\; H^{p+q}_{\\mathrm{cond}}(X,\\ M).$$\n- Also $c_{X*}M = M$, so **it suffices to prove $R^{q}c_{X*}M = 0$ ($q > 0$)**.\n- $R^{q}c_{X*}M$ is the sheaf obtained by sheafifying the presheaf $U \\mapsto H^{q}_{\\mathrm{cond}}(U, M)$. Since $X$ is **locally compact**, its stalk at $x$ is\n$$(R^{q}c_{X*}M)_{x} = \\varinjlim_{x \\in U} H^{q}_{\\mathrm{cond}}(U, M) = \\varinjlim_{x \\in K} H^{q}_{\\mathrm{cond}}(K, M)$$\n($U$ runs over open neighbourhoods of $x$, $K$ runs over compact neighbourhoods of $x$ — local compactness makes the two filtered systems cofinal).\n- Fix $x$ and a compact neighbourhood $K$, a Stone space $K_{0}$, and a surjection $K_{0} \\twoheadrightarrow K$; for $x$ and each compact neighbourhood $S \\subseteq K$, pulling back gives $S_{0} \\to S$.\n\n**Products and subspaces of Stone spaces are again Stone**, so all $S_{n}$ are Stone spaces; hence, by \"Čech = sheaf cohomology on compact Hausdorff spaces\" and the previous item \"constant coefficients on Stone spaces are acyclic\",\n$$H^{n}_{\\mathrm{cond}}(S, M) = \\check{H}^{n}(S_{\\bullet}, M).$$\n- Filtered colimits are exact, so the colimit in the stalk reduces to the case $S = \\{x\\}$; and the Čech complex of a point is zero directly from \"having a section $\\implies$ is acyclic\".\n\nHence $R^{q}c_{X*}M = 0$ ($q>0$), the spectral sequence degenerates, and $H^{n}_{\\mathrm{cond}}(X,M) \\cong H^{n}_{\\mathrm{sheaf}}(X,M)$.\n\n∎",

      notes: {
        en: "**Why colimits fail.** The inclusion functor $i:\\mathrm{Cond}\\hookrightarrow\\widehat{\\mathrm{CHaus}}$ is a right adjoint and does not preserve colimits; sheafification $a$ is its left adjoint and only repairs the presheaf colimit into a sheaf, so a colimit must first be taken pointwise in presheaves and then sheafified once.\n\n**A concrete counterexample.** Take a surjection $F \\twoheadrightarrow S$ of compact Hausdorff spaces, for example $S = S^{1}$, $F = S^{1}$, $z \\mapsto z^{2}$.       statement: {
        en: "For a category $\\mathcal{T}$, the following four conditions are equivalent:\n\n1. $\\mathcal{T}$ is a topos;\n2. every sheaf on $\\mathcal{T}$ for the **standard topology** is **representable**, and a small set of generators exists;\n3. there is a site $\\mathcal{C}$ such that $\\mathcal{T} \\simeq \\widehat{\\mathcal{C}}$;\n4. there is a category such that $\\mathcal{T}$ is a **reflective subcategory** of it, and the reflection is **exact** (preserves finite limits).",
        status: 'draft',
        basedOn: 'v0.9.3#c262a8bf',
      },
      notes: {
        en: "Translations among the four: (2)$\\iff$(3) take $\\mathcal{C} = \\mathcal{T}$ with the standard topology; (3)$\\iff$(4) is trivial (sheafification from presheaves to sheaves is an exact reflection); (4)$\\implies$(1) uses \"the presheaf category is a topos\" together with the reflection to transport the structure down;\n\n(1)$\\implies$(2) also equips $\\mathcal{T}$ with the standard topology.\n\nItem 4 is the most useful: **to build a topos, it suffices to build a presheaf category together with an exact reflection**. Condensed sets arise exactly this way — as sheaves on compact Hausdorff spaces.\n\n**The four are four different levels of description** (the value of this theorem is that it welds together the \"axiomatic\", \"sheaf-theoretic\", and \"reflective\" characterizations):\n\n- (1) $\\implies$ (2): **from axioms to sheaf theory**. Use exactness and a small generator in the topos to verify \"every sheaf for the standard topology is representable\" — this step uses **① (small generator) and ③ (arbitrary colimits)**, and is the most technical link of the four.\n- (2) $\\implies$ (3): **from sheaf theory to representability**. Take $\\mathcal{C}$ to be all objects of a small generator and $J$ the standard topology (\"$R$ covers $\\iff$ $\\coprod_{Y\\in\\mathcal{C}/R}\\underline Y \\to \\underline X$ is an epimorphism\"), then $\\mathcal{T} \\simeq \\widehat{\\mathcal{C}}$. **This step is exactly the definitional unfolding of \"topos = category of sheaves\"**, and needs the small generator to ensure that $\\mathcal{C}$ is a small category.\n- (3) $\\implies$ (4): **from sheaf categories to reflections**. $\\widehat{\\mathcal{C}}$ is a reflective subcategory of $\\mathbf{Set}^{\\mathcal{C}^{\\mathrm{op}}}$ (sheafification $\\mathbf{a} \\dashv \\iota$), and sheafification **preserves finite limits** (the core property of Grothendieck topologies). **Note that \"exact reflection\" cannot be weakened to \"has a reflection\"** — an arbitrary reflective subcategory is not exact, and exactness is precisely the hallmark of \"sheaves\".\n- (4) $\\implies$ (1): **from reflections to axioms**. Exactness of the reflection gives finite limits (preserved by the reflection), and colimits and effective equivalence relations of $\\mathbf{Set}^{\\mathcal{C}^{\\mathrm{op}}}$ descend along the reflection, giving ②③④.\n\n⚠️ **The four words \"exact reflection\" are the whole content**: with only a reflection (left adjoint) and no exactness, what one gets is a **pretopos** (some books define a pretopos exactly this way: a pretopos $=$ a reflective subcategory with exact reflection, but **without** requiring a small generator); adding smallness and colimits upgrades it to a topos.\n\n⚠️ **The comparison to keep in view**: (1)$\\iff$(3) is the **backbone of Giraud's theorem** (topos $=$ category of sheaves), and is the most commonly used criterion in practice: **to prove that a category is a topos, one usually realizes it directly as a category of sheaves**, rather than verifying the four axioms one by one.",

    'def.kernel-cokernel': {
      statement: {
        en: "Let $\\mathcal{C}$ be a (pre)additive category and $f : M \\to N$.\n\n- The **kernel** of $f$, written $\\ker f := \\ker(f, 0)$, is the **equalizer** of $f$ and the **zero morphism** $0_{M,N}$ (if it exists);\n- the **cokernel** of $f$, written $\\operatorname{coker} f$, is the kernel of $f$ in $\\mathcal{C}^{\\mathrm{op}}$ (if it exists), i.e. the **coequalizer** of $f$ and the zero morphism;\n- the sequence $0 \\to M' \\xrightarrow{f} M \\xrightarrow{g} M''$ is called **left exact** if $f$ realizes $\\ker g$ (that is, $M' \\cong \\ker g$);\n\ndually $M \\xrightarrow{g} M'' \\to 0$ is called **right exact** if $g$ realizes $\\operatorname{coker} g$;\n\n- a sequence $0 \\to M' \\xrightarrow{\\varphi} M \\xrightarrow{\\psi} M'' \\to 0$ that is both left exact and right exact is called a **short exact sequence**; then $M$ is called an **extension** of $M'$ by $M''$.",
        status: 'draft',

    'thm.splitting-principle': {
      statement: {
        en: "**What is being done**: the formula for Chern classes in general rank is hard to write down directly.         status: 'draft',
        basedOn: 'v0.9.3#e1eddd75',

      notes: {
        en: "**Why this is not \"cheating\"**: injectivity of $\\pi^{*}$ means \"a polynomial identity holding on $\\tilde B$ can be pushed back to $B$\".       notes: {
        en: "**Disambiguation**: in a general category there is no \"intersection\" of objects; what one can speak of is the intersection of **subobjects** (fibred intersection), and the relevant fibred product must exist.\n\n**Why the fibred product**: the intersection must express \"lies in $Y$ and $Z$ simultaneously\". In terms of elements this is $t \\in Y \\cap Z$; without elements it is $T \\to X$ factoring simultaneously through $Y \\rightarrowtail X$ and $Z \\rightarrowtail X$. The universal property of the fibred product is exactly the universal solution of this \"simultaneous factorization\"; hence any object with that property factors uniquely through $Y \\times_{X} Z$, i.e. it is the greatest lower bound.\n\n**Existence**: the intersection is a fibred product, so \"the intersection exists\" iff the fibred product along $X$ exists; in a category without fibred products one cannot speak of intersections. The pullback of a monomorphism along any morphism is still a monomorphism (see @@def.mono@@), so $Y \\times_{X} Z \\to X$ really is a subobject.\n\n**Relation to set-theoretic intersection**: in $\\mathbf{Set}$, $Y \\times_{X} Z = \\{(y,z) : y = z\\}$, the map $t \\mapsto (t,t)$ gives an isomorphism $Y \\cap Z \\to Y \\times_{X} Z$; so the intersection is the set-theoretic one. In concrete categories such as $\\mathbf{Grp}$, $\\mathbf{Top}$, subobjects are determined by injections, and the underlying set of the intersection is likewise the set-theoretic intersection.\n\n**Relation to union**: the union is the image of the coproduct $Y \\cup Z := \\operatorname{im}(Y \\coprod Z \\to X)$, not a fibred coproduct. In a pretopos the subobjects form a bounded lattice: meet = fibred product (greatest lower bound), join = image (least upper bound), see @@prop.subobject-lattice@@.\n\n**Warning**: in a general category $Y \\cap Z$ need not exist; even when it does, do not confuse it with the product $Y \\times Z$ or the coproduct $Y \\coprod Z$.       statement: {
        en: "Let $X = \\varinjlim_{i \\in I} X_{i}$ be a filtered colimit of **compact Hausdorff spaces** along **closed inclusions**.         status: 'draft',
        basedOn: 'v0.9.3#c7dffc22',
      },
      notes: {
        en: "**Item 3 is the substance of this proposition.** When taking a colimit of topological spaces, the topology may be coarser or finer than the \"piecewise\" topology; this item says that in this situation the two **coincide exactly** — a set is open iff its trace on every piece is open. Hence \"continuous functions on $X$\" = \"a compatible family of functions continuous on each piece\", and one can work piece by piece.\n\n**Why the pieces must be compact Hausdorff.** Compactness is the geometric incarnation of the finiteness condition \"finite subcover\"; in condensed mathematics, compact Hausdorff spaces are exactly the \"probes\". Writing a large object as a filtered colimit of compact pieces amounts to writing it as a limit of \"larger and larger finite information\" — every computation can stop at some piece.\n\n⭐ **Where it is used**: to prove \"the underlying space of a quasi-separated condensed set is weak Hausdorff\", first write the quasi-separated $X$ as a filtered colimit of compact Hausdorff pieces along inclusions, then take underlying spaces of this chain — by this item the colimit is still compactly generated weak Hausdorff.\n\n📌 Division of labour with \"closure of $\\mathrm{CGWH}$\": that one says that a general CGWH space glued along closed inclusions is still CGWH; this one replaces the pieces by **compact Hausdorff** ones and additionally gives an explicit description of the topology and the reverse decomposition.\n\n**Hypotheses used in each direction** (the topological description is the definition of colimit; the separation conclusion needs a separate argument):\n- In a topological space, a colimit $\\implies$ has open sets detected piecewise: this is the definition of the final topology, and does not require proving compact generation or weak Hausdorffness first; the note above calling item 3 extra substance should be qualified in this way.\n- In a filtered system of closed inclusions $\\implies$, each piece is closed in the colimit: the intersection of two pieces is closed in a common successor, and the final topology detects it; filteredness and closed inclusions are used in this step.\n- The final topology on compact Hausdorff pieces $\\implies$ is compactly generated: the structure maps of the pieces are precisely the compact Hausdorff probes; the weak Hausdorff conclusion does not follow from this definition alone, and the full proof of this step for an arbitrary filtered index has not been verified, so it is recorded as a gap.\n- A compactly generated weak Hausdorff space $\\implies$ is a filtered union of compact Hausdorff subspaces: weak Hausdorffness guarantees that the images of compact Hausdorff probes are closed and Hausdorff, finite unions give common upper bounds, and compact generation guarantees that the final topology recovers the original topology.\n- Compact generation cannot be dropped: the cocountable topology on an uncountable set is weak Hausdorff, its compact Hausdorff subspaces are all finite, and their final topology is discrete, so it cannot recover the original topology.",

    'def.filtered-category': {
      statement: {
        en: "A category $I$ is called **filtered** if:\n\n1. **nonempty**: $I$ has at least one object;\n2. **upper bounds**: for any $i, j \\in I$ there exist $k \\in I$ and morphisms $i \\to k$, $j \\to k$;\n3. **coequalization**: for any parallel $u, v : i \\rightrightarrows j$, there exists $w : j \\to k$ with $w \\circ u = w \\circ v$.\n\nEquivalently: **every finite diagram in $I$ has a cocone.** A colimit taken along a filtered category is called a **filtered colimit**.",
        status: 'draft',
        basedOn: 'v0.9.3#aa122a1c',
      },
      notes: {
        en: "**The three items $\\iff$ finite cocones**: the three correspond exactly to the three cases of \"a cocone for a finite diagram exists\" — the empty diagram (item 1), two points (item 2), and a pair of parallel arrows (item 3). So \"filtered\" = \"every finite diagram has a cocone\" = **all information can later be caught up**.\n\n**Relation to directed sets**: a directed set (a poset in which any two elements have an upper bound), regarded as a category, is filtered; conversely, **any filtered category can be replaced by a cofinal directed set** (see \"a filtered category can be replaced by a directed set\"). So \"filtered colimits\" and \"colimits along directed sets\" are the same thing.\n\n⭐ **Why it is required everywhere**: filtered colimits are **exact** (commute with finite limits) in $\\mathbf{Set}$, $A\\text{-}\\mathbf{Mod}$, $\\mathrm{CondAb}$; this is Grothendieck's **AB5** axiom and the key condition when homological algebra selects categories. Colimits along a general category lack this good property.\n\n⚠️ Distinguish from \"chain\": a chain is a **totally ordered** subset, stronger than filtered; filtered only requires \"a common upper bound\", and any two elements need not be comparable.\n\n**Hypotheses used in each direction** (finite cocones and the finite combinations of the three axioms):\n- Every finite diagram has a cocone $\\implies$ the three items: take respectively the empty diagram, two discrete objects, and a pair of parallel arrows, and only unfold the definition of a cocone.\n- The three items $\\implies$ finite cocones: first take common upper bounds finitely many times, then coequalize the incompatibilities created by arrows one by one; this needs finite induction, not Zorn's lemma or infinite choice.\n- For a poset regarded as a category, upper bounds and nonemptiness $\\implies$ filteredness: there is at most one parallel arrow, so item 3 is automatic; a general category cannot drop item 3, and a nontrivial group regarded as a one-object category is a counterexample.\n- Nonemptiness cannot be dropped: the empty category vacuously satisfies the last two items but has no cocone for the empty diagram. The further theorem about replacing by a cofinal directed set needs smallness conventions and the corresponding set-theoretic constructions, which are not part of this group of equivalent definitions.",

    'def.pretopos': {
      statement: {
        en: "A category $\\mathcal{C}$ is called a **pretopos** if\n\n1. **finite limits exist**;\n2. **finite coproducts exist** and are **disjoint** and **universal**;\n3. **equivalence relations are effective and universal**;\n4. **epimorphisms are regular and universal**.",
        status: 'draft',
        basedOn: 'v0.9.3#7acce7fc',
      },
      notes: {
        en: "Item 4 **is not independent** — it can be deduced from the first three, so some textbooks write only three. The advantage of writing it is to recall one thing: **here epimorphisms are automatically regular**, so \"epimorphism\" and \"quotient map\" are the same thing.\n\nStandard fact: **monomorphisms can be defined by limits** ($i$ is a monomorphism $\\iff$ that square is cartesian), and pullbacks and limits preserve monomorphisms; this guarantees that pushouts also preserve monomorphisms — which is what makes the \"disjoint\" and \"effective\" conditions in items 2 and 3 meaningful.\n\nExamples: $\\mathbf{Set}$, $\\mathbf{FinSet}$ are pretopoi; $\\mathbf{CHaus}$ is a topos; **$\\mathbf{Top}$ is not a pretopos** (epimorphisms need not be quotient maps). The sheaf category of any site is a pretopos.\n\n**The four axioms do not carry equal weight** (this is where the difference between a pretopos and a topos hides):\n\n- **① Finite limits exist**: a **purely structural requirement**, guaranteeing only that one can speak of fibred products and equalizers (hence of \"relations\"). It involves neither colimits nor epi/mono.\n- **② Finite coproducts are disjoint and universal**: **this is a real condition and cannot be dropped**. \"Disjoint\" = the pullback $A \\times_{A\\sqcup B} B \\simeq \\emptyset$ is empty (one requires an initial object and that it is \"really empty\"); \"universal\" = coproducts are stable under pullback. **Merely requiring \"finite coproducts exist\" is far from enough** — coproducts in $\\mathbf{Set}$ are disjoint and universal, but coproducts in $\\mathbf{Grp}$ (free products) are not universal, so $\\mathbf{Grp}$ is not a pretopos.\n- **③ Equivalence relations are effective and universal**: **this item guarantees that \"quotients\" correspond to \"relations\"** (effective = every equivalence relation is the kernel pair of some epimorphism; universal = preserved under pullback). ⚠️ Without it, \"quotient objects\" and \"equivalence relations\" do not match, and the first isomorphism theorem cannot even be stated.\n- **④ Epimorphisms are regular and universal**: **this is the most technical item** (regular = the first projection of some kernel pair). ⚠️ It overlaps with ③ but **they do not imply each other**: ③ says equivalence relation $\\to$ epimorphism, ④ says epimorphism $\\to$ equivalence relation.\n\n**Pretopos vs topos: the missing item**. A topos = a pretopos **+** (effective epimorphisms + local representability, or equivalently Giraud's axioms). So the whole meaning of the four letters \"pretopos\" is: **it already has all the apparatus for quotients, fibred products, and equivalence relations, but not yet the layer that makes \"sheaves\"**. This is also why many constructions (internal equivalence relations, stability of effective epimorphisms) already work for pretopoi.\n\n⚠️ **The most common comparison**: $\\mathbf{Set}$ is a topos; $\\mathbf{Grp}$, $\\mathbf{Ring}$, $\\mathbf{Cat}$ are all **not** pretopoi (coproducts are not universal). To decide whether a concrete category is a pretopos, **check ② first** — it is the item that fails most easily.",
        status: 'draft',
        basedOn: 'v0.9.3#afc4c149',
      },

      notes: {
        en: "**What \"effective\" means.** Given an equivalence relation, first take the quotient $Y = X/R$ (always possible), then pull back $Y$ along the quotient map to $X \\times X$. Effective equivalence relations are exactly those that pull back to **$R$ itself**.\n\n**Why it matters**: only effective equivalence relations allow \"quotient and pull back to recover\", that is, the operation of \"taking quotients\" **loses no information**. Specialized to $\\mathrm{Cond}$, it guarantees: gluing two compact Hausdorff pieces along an equivalence relation, the quotient object \"is\" that equivalence relation in the sheaf-theoretic sense.\n\n⭐ **Together these three are exactly the input to Giraud's theorem**: a category with all colimits, universal colimits, and effective equivalence relations is a topos.         status: 'draft',
        basedOn: 'v0.9.3#116c4eb6',

    'def.topos': {
      statement: {
        en: "A category $\\mathcal{T}$ is called a **topos** if\n\n1. there is a **small** set of generators;\n2. finite limits exist;\n3. coproducts exist and are disjoint and universal (equivalently: all colimits exist);\n4. equivalence relations are effective and universal.",
        status: 'draft',
        basedOn: 'v0.9.3#610177ed',
      },
      notes: {
        en: "The only difference from a pretopos is item 1: **a topos = a pretopos with a small set of generators**. So \"a topos is a pretopos shrunk until a set can control it\".\n\nExamples: $\\mathbf{Set}$, $G\\text{-}\\mathbf{Set}$ are topoi; the presheaf category $\\widehat{\\mathcal{C}}$ is a topos; sheaf categories of sites are topoi; sheaves on a topological space and its étalé space also form a topos. **$\\mathbf{Top}$ is not a topos.**\n\n⭐ **The category of condensed sets is a topos** — this is the foundation of the Cond section later.\n\n**Which of the four is unique to topoi** (the difference from pretopoi is all in item 1 and the strengthening of item 3):\n\n- **① Small set of generators**: **this is the \"smallness\" condition, which pretopoi lack**. It guarantees that a topos behaves well enough (supports internal language, admits sheafification), and it is the hypothesis for \"$\\mathcal{T} \\simeq \\operatorname{Sh}(\\mathcal{C}, J)$ with $\\mathcal{C}$ small\". ⚠️ Without it, the category may be \"too large\" to be a sheaf category.\n- **② Finite limits**: same as pretopos, a purely structural requirement.\n- **③ Coproducts exist and are disjoint and universal** (equivalently: **all** colimits exist): **one notch stronger than a pretopos** — a pretopos requires only **finite** coproducts. Together with ② this gives \"all colimits can be computed\", which is what makes \"sheafification\" possible (sheafification takes colimits along sieves).\n- **④ Equivalence relations are effective and universal**: same as pretopos.\n\n**Together with pretopos**: topos $=$ pretopos $+$ \"small generator $+$ arbitrary colimits\". So a \"pretopos\" is \"a topos lacking smallness and large colimits\"; many constructions (internal equivalence relations, effective epimorphisms, kernel pairs) already work at that level.\n\n⚠️ **The four items cannot be dropped at will**: drop ①, and $\\operatorname{Sh}(\\mathcal{C})$ (with $\\mathcal{C}$ large) still satisfies ②③④ but is not a topos (no small generator); keep only \"coproducts exist\" from ③ without \"disjoint universal\", and things like $\\mathbf{Grp}^{\\mathrm{op}}$ creep in; drop ④, and \"quotient = equivalence relation\" fails, so the \"set\" semantics of the internal language collapses.\n\n⚠️ **\"Disjoint and universal\" in ② is the most often ignored**: merely saying \"coproducts exist\" does not make a topos — coproducts in a topos must be **stable** under pullback (universal); this is the source of the behaviour of \"disjoint unions\" in the internal language.",

        basedOn: 'v0.9.3#823930f5',

        en: "⭐ **It guarantees that projective resolutions exist**: starting from $P_{0} \\twoheadrightarrow X$, cover the kernel $\\ker(P_{0} \\to X)$ by another projective object, and so on recursively, obtaining a **projective resolution**\n\n$$\\cdots \\to P_{2} \\to P_{1} \\to P_{0} \\to X \\to 0,$$\n\nThen one can take left derived functors ($\\operatorname{Tor}$, $L^{n}F$). So homological algebra names categories by \"having enough projectives / injectives\".\n\n**Examples**: $A\\text{-}\\mathbf{Mod}$ has both (free modules are projective, every module embeds into an injective module); $\\mathrm{CondAb}$ has enough projectives (the finitely presented projective condensed abelian groups generate it); **a Grothendieck category automatically has enough injectives**.",

    'def.universal-colimit': {
      statement: {
        en: "1. A coproduct $X = \\coprod_{i \\in I} X_{i}$ is called **disjoint** if (a) every $X_{i} \\to X$ is a monomorphism and (b) for $i \\ne j$, $X_{i} \\times_{X} X_{j}$ is an initial object.\n\nAn effective equivalence relation is called **universal** if its quotient map is a universal regular epimorphism.",
        status: 'draft',
        basedOn: 'v0.9.3#d8e955d0',

      notes: {
        en: "\"Universal\" = **still there after pullback**: no matter along which map the whole diagram is pulled back, the property is preserved. These three are the template for translating \"the obviously good properties in sets\" into category language — they cause no trouble in $\\mathbf{Set}$, but need not hold in another category, which is why they must be written down.\n\n**Hypotheses used in each direction** (the three kinds of terms are different pullback-stability requirements):\n- Universal colimits $\\iff$ remain the colimit of that diagram after an arbitrary base change: unfolding the definition is free, provided the pullbacks and colimits written exist.\n- Universal regular epimorphisms $\\iff$ have all pullbacks still regular epi: again unfolding the definition; taking only the identity base change gives only regular epi, which does not conversely imply universal.\n- Disjoint coproducts $\\iff$ have injections that are monomorphisms and fibred products of distinct terms initial: unfolding the definition; this does not automatically imply that the coproduct is universal.\n- Stability in sets needs the concrete constructions of set colimits and pullbacks; a general category cannot copy this, for example the coproduct in groups is the free product, but colimits in the category of groups are not universally so.",
        status: 'draft',
        basedOn: 'v0.9.3#d692b302',

    'prop.site-properties': {
      statement: {
        en: "If $\\mathcal{C}$ is a site, then all limits and colimits exist in the sheaf category $\\widehat{\\mathcal{C}}$, and:\n\n1. colimits are **universal**;\n2. **filtered colimits are exact**;\n3. epimorphisms are **regular** and **universal**;\n4. coproducts are **disjoint**;\n5. equivalence relations are **effective** and **universal**.",
        status: 'draft',
        basedOn: 'v0.9.3#2e91fc99',
      },
      notes: {
        en: "One-line reason: **these properties hold in $\\mathbf{Set}$, and constructions in a sheaf category inherit the properties of $\\mathbf{Set}$ pointwise**, together with sheafification preserving finite limits and colimits. Item 3 is especially worth remembering: here \"epi = regular epi\" is automatic, with no extra hypothesis — this is the source of that axiom in the definition of a pretopos below.\n\n**The five items are not parallel axioms but properties a sheaf category has automatically** (only item 2 needs an extra condition):\n\n- **① Universal colimits**: **automatic in a sheaf category** (sheaves are limits along covers, and limits commute with pullback). No condition on the site is needed, only that $\\widehat{\\mathcal{C}}$ exists.\n- **② Filtered colimits are exact**: **this needs \"sheafification commutes with filtered colimits\"** — true for a general Grothendieck topology; for **finite** filtered colimits (i.e. over $\\mathbf{Ab}$) the site must satisfy an extra condition (for instance \"quasi-compact + quasi-separated\").\n- **③ Epimorphisms are regular and universal**: a sheaf category is a **regular category**, so epi = regular epi; universality comes from pullback stability as in ①.\n- **④ Coproducts are disjoint**: from \"a sheaf decomposes along a disjoint union\" ($F(\\coprod X_{i}) \\simeq \\prod F(X_{i})$), as long as the site has disjoint unions.\n- **⑤ Equivalence relations are effective and universal**: a sheaf category is an **exact category** — by ③ plus effective equivalence relations.\n\n⚠️ **This is the \"reverse direction\" of Giraud's theorem**: Giraud says \"a topos $\\iff$ is a category satisfying these five items plus a small generator and arbitrary colimits\"; this section is exactly the half \"a sheaf category $\\implies$ satisfies the five\". ⚠️ Do not treat ② as unconditional: **finite filtered colimits** and **arbitrary filtered colimits** behave differently on a general site.\n\n**Hypotheses used in each direction** (these are theorems for set-valued sheaf categories, not five equivalent definitions):\n- The five properties of the sheaf category $\\implies$: one must use a small site, or a site small enough in a fixed universe, to guarantee the relevant sheafifications and small colimits exist.\n- Presheaf limits are computed pointwise for free; sheaf colimits must first take the presheaf colimit and then sheafify, at the cost that sheafification is a left exact left adjoint, so one cannot say sheaf colimits are all computed pointwise.\n- Filtered colimits are exact: use that filtered colimits in sets commute with finite limits and that sheafification is left exact; no extra quasi-compact or quasi-separated hypothesis is needed, and the remark above about an extra condition there should be corrected.\n- Disjoint coproducts, regular epimorphisms, and effective equivalence relations need the topos theorems; one does not require the underlying site itself to have disjoint coproducts.\n- That the five properties $\\implies$ come from some site is not free; the Giraud characterization also needs a small generating family and so on; this node does not give the complete reverse hypotheses, so one cannot infer backwards from the five alone.",
        status: 'draft',
        basedOn: 'v0.9.3#a7917ede',

    'def.etale-sheaf': {
      statement: {
        en: "Let $X$ be a scheme.\n\nWrite $X_{\\text{et}}$ for the **small étale site** of $X$: objects are étale maps $U \\to X$ (usually restricted to the **affine, locally of finite presentation** ones, so that the category is essentially small), morphisms are $X$-morphisms, and **coverings** are jointly surjective étale families $\\{U_{i} \\to U\\}$.\n\nA **presheaf** on $X_{\\text{et}}$ is a contravariant functor\n\n$$\\mathcal{F} : \\bigl(X_{\\text{et}}\\bigr)^{\\mathrm{op}} \\longrightarrow \\mathbf{Ab} .$$\n\nIt is an **étale sheaf** if for every étale covering $\\{U_{i} \\to U\\}$ the sequence is exact:\n\n$$0 \\longrightarrow \\mathcal{F}(U) \\longrightarrow \\prod_{i} \\mathcal{F}(U_{i}) \\longrightarrow \\prod_{i,j} \\mathcal{F}\\bigl(U_{i} \\times_{U} U_{j}\\bigr).$$\n\nThe category of étale sheaves is written $\\mathbf{Sh}(X_{\\text{et}}, \\mathbf{Ab})$.",
        status: 'draft',
        basedOn: 'v0.9.3#787c6b3a',
      },
      notes: {
        en: "**How the name \"étale\" relates in the two places**: an étale map of schemes is a morphism **simultaneously** satisfying:\n\n- locally of finite presentation;\n- **flat**;\n- **unramified** (that is, $\\Omega^{1}_{U/X} = 0$).\n\nThese three are the algebraic counterparts of \"local homeomorphism\" in topology, but they additionally allow **finite coverings** (for example $\\operatorname{Spec}\\mathbb{C} \\to \\operatorname{Spec}\\mathbb{R}$: two points covering one).\n\n**Why the topology must change**: the Zariski topology is too coarse — on $\\operatorname{Spec} k(t)$ it does not see a Galois covering with group $k$. After passing to the étale topology, coefficients such as $\\mathbb{Z}/n$ can see geometry (Galois groups, Kummer sequences and the whole Weil-conjecture story become meaningful only after this step).\n\n⭐ **Zariski coverings are étale coverings** (open immersions are étale, and open coverings are of course jointly surjective). Thus the topology \"refines\" and sheaves \"become fewer\":\n\n$$\\mathbf{Sh}(X_{\\text{et}}, \\mathbf{Ab}) \\longrightarrow \\mathbf{Sh}(X_{\\mathrm{Zar}}, \\mathbf{Ab})$$\n\nis \"forgetting the étale structure\", **not** an equivalence. In one sentence: étale sheaves are always Zariski sheaves, but not conversely.\n\n⚠️ **The technical condition cannot be dropped**: the \"small étale site\" must be a set (not so large that no set can contain it), so one usually takes \"affine étale $X$-schemes of locally finite presentation\"; different references choose slightly differently, but the resulting topos is the same. This point is not pursued here.\n\n📌 This graph goes only as far as \"what an étale sheaf is\".\n\nTo do étale cohomology one further needs: $\\mathbf{Sh}(X_{\\text{et}}, \\mathbf{Ab})$ is a Grothendieck category, it has enough injectives (again via \"enough points\", with points taken to be geometric points), and compatibility with the long exact sequences of $f_{*}$, $R^{n}f_{*}$ — that is another whole apparatus.",

      statement: {
        en: "Let $\\mathcal{A}$ be an abelian category and let $I^{\\bullet} \\to M$, $J^{\\bullet} \\to N$ be **injective resolutions** in $\\mathcal{C}^{+}(\\mathcal{A})$ ($I^{n}$, $J^{n}$ injective, bounded below, quasi-isomorphic to $M$, $N$).\n\nThen for every morphism $f : M \\to N$:\n\n1. **there exists** a morphism of complexes $F : I^{\\bullet} \\to J^{\\bullet}$ lifting $f$ (that is, inducing $H^{0}(F) = f$);\n2. any two such lifts are **homotopic**.",
        status: 'draft',
        basedOn: 'v0.9.3#9cce1d70',

      notes: {
        en: "**Proof (the two standard steps of the comparison theorem).**\n**① Existence (lifting layer by layer).** Degree $0$: $M = Z^{0}_{I} \\hookrightarrow I^{0}$ is a monomorphism (because $I^{-1} = 0$), and the composite $M \\xrightarrow{\\ f\\ } N \\to J^{0}$, by the **injectivity** of $J^{0}$, lifts along this monomorphism to $F^{0} : I^{0} \\to J^{0}$ ✓.\nInductive step: suppose $F^{0}, \\ldots, F^{n}$ has been constructed and, for $k \\le n$, $F^{k}d^{k-1}_{I} = d^{k-1}_{J}F^{k-1}$ holds.\n\nTo construct $F^{n+1}$ it suffices to fix its value on the **coboundary** $B^{n+1}_{I} = \\operatorname{im}d^{n}_{I}$, since $B^{n+1}_{I}\\hookrightarrow I^{n+1}$ is a monomorphism.\n\nKey observation: when $n \\ge 1$, $H^{n}(I^{\\bullet}) = 0$, i.e. $Z^{n}_{I} = B^{n}_{I}$ — so the value of $F^{n}$ on $Z^{n}_{I}$ is already determined by the previous layer and lies in $Z^{n}_{J}$ (for $n \\ge 1$ it equals $d^{n-1}_{J}F^{n-1}$; for $n = 0$ one uses instead the commutativity at the augmentation) ✓.\n\nHence $d^{n}_{J}F^{n}$ vanishes on $\\ker d^{n}_{I}$ and therefore factors through $B^{n+1}_{I} \\hookrightarrow I^{n+1}$;\n\nthen use the **injectivity** of $J^{n+1}$ to lift it to $F^{n+1} : I^{n+1}\\to J^{n+1}$ ✓.\n\nBounded below guarantees that the process terminates after some $n$ ✓.\n**② Uniqueness up to homotopy.** Let $F, G$ both lift $f$.\n\nThe difference $F - G$, on $H^{0}$, induces $0$, so the **same layer-by-layer lifting** produces a homotopy $s^{n} : I^{n}\\to J^{n-1}$: at each layer the equation $F^{n}-G^{n} = s^{n+1}d^{n}_{I} + d^{n-1}_{J}s^{n}$ is a problem of \"lifting along a monomorphism\", again solved by the injectivity of $J^{n-1}$;\n\nat the starting point $F^{0}-G^{0}$ vanishes on $M$, so it factors through $I^{0}/M = B^{1}_{I}$ ✓.\n\nHence $F \\sim G$ ✓.\n\n∎\n\n⭐ Together the two say that **injective resolutions are unique up to homotopy**: a morphism in $\\operatorname{Hom}(M,N)$ lifts to a morphism of complexes, uniquely up to homotopy.         status: 'draft',
        basedOn: 'v0.9.3#d4ff27b8',

      notes: {
        en: "⭐ **The word \"closed\" comes from topology**: in a simplicial complex a chain with $\\partial z = 0$ is a \"chain without boundary\" — intuitively a bunch of simplices closed up (a circle, a sphere). In the de Rham complex a form with $d\\omega = 0$ is called **closed**, with the very same definition. So \"cycle / closed form / cocycle\" are three ways of saying one thing.\n\n⚠️ **Origin of the letter $Z$**: German *Zyklus* (cycle). $B$ comes from *boundary* (edge) — see @@def.boundary@@. Older literature also writes $C^{n}$ (cocycle) and $B^{n}$; check the notation when reading.\n\n⚠️ **$Z^{n}$ is a subobject of $K^{n}$** (a kernel), but it is **not** a subcomplex: restricting $d$ to $Z^{n}$ gives the zero map, so $Z$ carries a complex structure that is \"zero everywhere\". Be careful whether you mean the subobject or the subcomplex.\n\n⭐ **Homology measures \"how much of the cycles is not a boundary\"**: $H^{n} = Z^{n}/B^{n}$ (@@def.cohomology@@).     'def.boundary': {
      statement: {
        en: "**What is being made precise**: the denominator in the homology formula $H^{n} = Z^{n}/B^{n}$. A boundary is an element **coming from one level up** — a \"trivial cycle\", and homology is about dividing them out.\n\nLet $K$ be a complex in an additive category $\\mathcal{C}$, with differential $d^{n} : K^{n} \\to K^{n+1}$.\n\n**Cochain case**: the $n$-th **group of coboundaries** is\n\n$$B^{n}(K) := \\operatorname{im} d^{n-1} = \\{\\, d^{n-1}x : x \\in K^{n-1} \\,\\} ,$$\n\nits elements are called **coboundaries**.\n\n**Chain case**: the $n$-th **group of boundaries** is\n\n$$B_{n}(K) := \\operatorname{im} d_{n+1} ,$$\n\nits elements are called **boundaries**.\n\n**Why $B \\subseteq Z$**: by the complex axiom $d^{n} \\circ d^{n-1} = 0$, for $x = d^{n-1}y$ we have $d^{n}x = d^{n}d^{n-1}y = 0$, so every coboundary is a cocycle.       notes: {
        en: "⭐ **All of $d^{2} = 0$ is this single statement**: $B^{n} \\subseteq Z^{n}$. The axiom $d\\circ d = 0$ has no other use — its only purpose is to make the quotient \"cycles / boundaries\" exist. So a complex is \"a device that lets homology be defined\".\n\n⚠️ **\"Boundary\" collides with the topological boundary**: the boundary of a manifold $\\partial M$ and the boundary operator of a simplicial complex $\\partial_{n}$ are, in the language of complexes, the boundary here ($\\partial_{n}$ is $d_{n}$).\n\nBut a **general category has no notion of \"point-set boundary\"**, only images.\n\nAlways read \"boundary\" as $\\operatorname{im} d$.\n\n⚠️ **Notation**: $B^{n}$ (boundary), $\\operatorname{im} d^{n-1}$ and $d(K^{n-1})$ are all in use.\n\nWhen $B$ and $Z$ appear together, mind the shift: $B^{n} = \\operatorname{im} d^{n-1}$ is the differential **one degree lower** than $Z^{n} = \\ker d^{n}$; this \"misalignment\" is the source of every sign error in homological algebra.\n\n⭐ **de Rham version**: forms with $d\\omega = 0$ are closed (= cycles), forms with $\\omega = d\\eta$ are **exact** (= boundaries). So \"closed implies exact\" is the part with $\\iff$ $H^{1} = 0$ — exactly the content of the Poincaré lemma.",

    'def.category-of-complexes': {
      statement: {
        en: "Let $\\mathcal{C}$ be an additive category. The **category of complexes** $\\mathcal{C}(\\mathcal{C})$ has as objects the cochain complexes of $\\mathcal{C}$, and as morphisms the **chain maps** $f : K \\to L$: a family $f^{n} : K^{n} \\to L^{n}$ such that\n\n$$d_{L}^{n} \\circ f^{n} = f^{n+1} \\circ d_{K}^{n}$$\n\nholds for all $n$. It is a **full** additive subcategory of $\\mathcal{C}^{(\\mathbb{Z}, \\le)}$ — a morphism of sequences is precisely a natural transformation, whose condition is exactly the commutativity above.",
        status: 'draft',
        basedOn: 'v0.9.3#e279d123',
      },
      notes: {
        en: "**Fullness**: every morphism of sequences between two complexes automatically commutes with the differentials, so the Hom set of $\\mathcal{C}(\\mathcal{C})$ is that of the sequence category; no further filtering is needed.\n\nBounded below, bounded above and bounded complexes give **full subcategories** $\\mathcal{C}^{+}(\\mathcal{C})$, $\\mathcal{C}^{-}(\\mathcal{C})$, $\\mathcal{C}^{b}(\\mathcal{C})$; the homotopy category receives $+$, $-$, $b$ similarly.\n\nLimits and colimits are computed **termwise**, and so is the additive structure (see @@prop.complex-additive@@); when $\\mathcal{C}$ is abelian, $\\mathcal{C}(\\mathcal{C})$ is abelian too.",
